Table 1
Accounts Payable and Accrued Liability Controls
1. Q: What are the principal objectives of an Accounts Payable SOP covering supplier invoices and accrued liabilities?
A: Show answer
The principal objective is to ensure that every recorded supplier liability is genuine, authorized, complete, correctly measured, classified in the appropriate account and reporting period, independently reviewed, supported by evidence, and traceable through posting and reconciliation. The SOP 020 example establishes separate control gates for supplier identity, invoice authenticity, purchase authorization, receipt or service acceptance, matching, duplicate detection, accounting and tax review, approval, posting, reconciliation, and hand-off. An invoice must not become payable merely because it has arrived, balances arithmetically, is overdue, or is requested urgently. The example identifies Harborlight Learning Network Foundation as fictional and labels the procedure as not adopted. Its entity details, amounts, approvals, system references, and accounting framework are illustrative rather than evidence of actual organizational arrangements. Before implementation, management must verify the applicable reporting framework, legal entity, policies, delegated authorities, contracts, donor conditions, tax requirements, and system configuration.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the objective of each AP control gate and identify the evidence needed before a liability can proceed to posting."
Key point:
- A supplier invoice must satisfy independent control requirements before posting and hand-off.
- A fictional demonstration cannot substitute for an approved organizational SOP.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
2. Q: Which activities should fall within Accounts Payable, and which must remain under separate organizational processes?
A: Show answer
AP ordinarily covers invoice intake, document validation, purchase and receipt matching, permitted non-purchase-order exceptions, liability classification, calculation, review, approval coordination, posting, supplier balance reconciliation, accrued-liability control, and controlled hand-off to payment processing. Related processes may own supplier onboarding, procurement approvals, physical receipt, inventory custody, tax filing, payroll, reimbursement, travel advances, asset accounting, lease accounting, bank reconciliation, and bank disbursement. The SOP 020 example identifies interfaces with SOP 003 for procurement and supplier management, SOP 021 for payment processing, SOP 028 for bank reconciliation, SOP 029 for payroll, SOP 031 for tax, SOP 036 for property and equipment, SOP 038 for inventory, and other specialist procedures. These interface identifiers and titles must be checked against the organization's actual SOP register. An invoice that belongs to another process must be referred to its designated owner rather than forced through AP to bypass source-process controls. A clear boundary reduces duplicate processing, conflicting responsibilities, and unauthorized payments.
Study instruction:
- "Map one supplier transaction from procurement through AP and payment, identifying the responsible procedure and hand-off evidence at each stage."
Key point:
- AP coordinates liability processing but does not automatically own every related financial process.
- Cross-process hand-offs require explicit ownership and acknowledgment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
3. Q: How should the accounting authority hierarchy be established before AP rules are configured?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying the reporting framework legally and contractually applicable to the organization. Then establish which statutory requirements, accounting policies, supplier contracts, donor conditions, approved internal procedures, and delegated authorities govern the transaction. The applicable requirements must be evaluated within their respective scopes; a donor condition does not automatically determine the accounting classification, and a system default does not override an applicable law or approved accounting policy. Maintain an authority register containing the source title, issuing organization, exact reference, relevant paragraph or section, effective period, applicability conclusion, reviewer, approval date, and future review trigger. Where the entity, jurisdiction, framework, or tax treatment remains unverified, record a hold and obtain a decision from an appropriately qualified reviewer. The supplied SOP example explicitly labels its actual-entity applicability as unverified. It should therefore be used as a control-design reference rather than as confirmation that its accounting or tax rules apply universally.
Study instruction:
- "Build an authority register for the organization's reporting framework, AP policies, tax requirements, contracts, and donor conditions, documenting their applicability."
Key point:
- AP rules must be based on verified requirements and authorized decisions.
- Unresolved legal, accounting, or contractual applicability must not be cleared by software configuration alone.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
4. Q: How should trade payables, accrued liabilities, provisions, commitments, advances, and prepayments be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Trade payables generally represent amounts owed to suppliers for goods or services received under an invoice or established billing arrangement. Accrued liabilities cover obligations arising from goods or services already received when the supplier invoice has not yet been processed or received. Provisions address obligations whose timing or amount is uncertain and require separate analysis under the applicable accounting framework. Commitments generally concern authorized future expenditure that has not necessarily resulted in a recognized liability. Advances and prepayments require assessment of whether a future benefit, recoverable balance, or other asset exists rather than assuming that the amount is an immediate expense or trade payable. The distinction depends on the underlying event, the existence of a present obligation, the reliability of measurement, and the applicable accounting criteria. Each classification has different implications for recognition, settlement, reversal, presentation, and disclosure. The AP register should not combine all these balances in one undifferentiated supplier-payable category. Uncertain cases should be referred to the relevant accounting specialist.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five sample transactions as trade payables, accruals, provisions, commitments, advances, or prepayments and explain the recognition basis for each."
Key point:
- Similar cash or purchasing events can produce different accounting classifications.
- The applicable framework determines recognition and presentation requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 15 — Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets
5. Q: How should responsibilities and segregation of duties be structured across the AP lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Assign distinct responsibilities for requesting goods or services, authorizing procurement, maintaining supplier master data, confirming receipt, preparing invoices, reviewing calculations, determining accounting and tax treatment, approving transactions, posting entries, reconciling balances, and receiving hand-offs. The AP preparer should not be the sole person confirming delivery, clearing duplicate alerts, approving the invoice, changing supplier bank details, and executing payment. Supplier-master changes require independent verification and separate authorization. The person accepting services should provide evidence based on actual performance rather than simply endorsing the supplier's statement. If limited staffing makes complete separation impractical, document compensating controls such as independent review of supplier changes, post-posting review by a finance controller, and periodic examination of user activity. Maintain a current role matrix with named users, delegated limits, effective dates, authorized substitutes, conflicts, and revocation dates. Review access and delegation when staff responsibilities change.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a segregation-of-duties matrix and test whether any individual can create, approve, post, and independently clear the same invoice."
Key point:
- Critical financial decisions must not depend on one uncontrolled individual.
- Compensating controls require documented performance and independent verification.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
6. Q: How should a supplier invoice enter the AP workflow and receive a traceable case identifier?
A: Show answer
Every invoice, credit note, or other AP-related submission should enter through an approved channel and receive a unique, immutable case identifier. Record the received timestamp, original document reference, supplier, legal entity, currency, amount, source channel, owner, document version, and preliminary status. Classify the submission before routing it, distinguishing purchase-order goods, services, partial deliveries, recurring bills, authorized non-PO transactions, credit notes, foreign-currency or tax-sensitive invoices, inter-entity invoices, received-not-invoiced items, and transactions belonging to another process. Missing information should be entered in an outstanding-items register with a responsible owner and follow-up date. Preserve the original submission and link subsequent versions rather than overwriting the original. Receipt of the document confirms intake only; it does not establish authenticity, approval, accounting recognition, or payment readiness. Workflow status should reflect the actual evidence available at the time of each change.
Study instruction:
- "Create an invoice intake checklist and trace one document from receipt through classification, review, posting, and archival."
Key point:
- Every AP case needs a unique identity, controlled ownership, and document history.
- Receiving an invoice is not approval to post or pay it.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
7. Q: How should supplier identity and invoice authenticity be verified before further processing?
A: Show answer
Confirm that the supplier exists in the approved supplier master, is active for the relevant legal entity, and has a verified legal identity. Compare the name, registration or tax identity where applicable, supplier reference, contractual relationship, invoice origin, and transaction details with independently maintained records. Confirm suspicious documents using a previously verified contact channel or another approved independent source, not merely the email address printed on the invoice. Changes to bank details, contact information, ownership, or tax status should trigger a separate verification and authorization process. An invoice submitted through a familiar mailbox can still be fraudulent or altered. Preserve the original file, verification evidence, source references, reviewer, date, and outcome. If the supplier cannot be verified, the invoice differs from authorized records, or fraud indicators remain unresolved, place the case on hold and escalate it through the designated route. Do not clear the hold based on urgency or seniority.
Study instruction:
- "Examine a supplier record and a sample invoice, then identify independent evidence supporting the supplier's identity and document authenticity."
Key point:
- Supplier existence and invoice authenticity are separate checks.
- Unverified supplier or remittance information must block the affected transaction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
8. Q: What mandatory invoice information should be validated before matching or approval?
A: Show answer
Validate the legal entity, supplier identifier and legal name, original invoice number, document version, date, received timestamp, service or delivery period, currency, net amount, applicable taxes, gross amount, purchase order or contract reference, receipt or service-acceptance evidence, payment terms, transaction purpose, account and project dimensions, and relevant supporting documents. Requirements vary by transaction and jurisdiction, so the checklist must be aligned with applicable statutory invoice rules, contracts, approved policies, and system requirements. Recalculate line extensions, authorized charges, discounts, taxes, deductions, and the resulting payable amount independently. Missing critical information must produce an incomplete or held status rather than a successful approval. Any approved alternative evidence must be explicitly documented and authorized; a staff member should not silently waive a mandatory field simply because the invoice is familiar. Retain the source document and record the results of completeness and arithmetic checks.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the mandatory-field checklist to an invoice and record each missing document, inconsistent amount, and required corrective action."
Key point:
- Complete invoice data supports classification, matching, calculation, and auditability.
- Missing evidence must trigger an identified exception rather than an informal override.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
9. Q: How does three-way matching establish whether a purchase-order goods invoice is supported?
A: Show answer
Three-way matching compares the authorized purchase order, the supplier invoice, and evidence that the goods were received, inspected, and accepted. The review should confirm the legal entity, supplier, currency, ordered quantity, invoice quantity, accepted quantity, authorized price, delivery reference, prior billings, returns, credits, and remaining contractual or purchase-order ceiling. An order proves purchasing authorization, but it does not prove delivery. An invoice proves what the supplier claims, but it does not independently prove performance. Receiving evidence establishes the relevant delivery or acceptance facts and should be provided by an appropriately authorized receiving function. Differences within a tolerance may be accepted only where the tolerance has been formally approved for the specific transaction type. Any unexplained excess quantity, rate variance, missing receipt, quality issue, or wrong-entity charge remains on hold until investigated and resolved.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a purchase order, invoice, and goods receipt at line level, including prior invoices, returns, and the remaining authorized quantity."
Key point:
- Three-way matching tests authorization, supplier billing, and accepted receipt together.
- A variance must be resolved under an approved rule rather than dismissed because the invoice total appears reasonable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and delivery-based recognition
10. Q: When are two-way matching, service-acceptance matching, and authorized non-PO procedures appropriate?
A: Show answer
Two-way matching may be appropriate where the contract and documented business process establish that a separate goods-receipt record is not applicable. Service invoices require evidence of the actual service period, deliverables, accepted milestone, authorized rate, and quantity or hours. Partial deliveries require cumulative checks of previous invoices, accepted receipts, credits, returns, and contract limits. Recurring utilities, rent, subscriptions, and maintenance invoices need contract validity, billing-period verification, occurrence checks, and analysis of rate changes. Non-PO processing should be permitted only for approved categories supported by business rationale, budget authority, performance evidence, and the correct independent approval. A missing purchase order alone does not create a valid exception. The route and evidence must be specified before the invoice is processed. If the required acceptance, contract authority, milestone, or approved exception is missing, keep the record on hold or refer it to the appropriate source-process owner.
Study instruction:
- "Compare goods, service, recurring, partial-delivery, and non-PO invoices and determine the correct matching evidence for each."
Key point:
- Matching methods vary by transaction but must establish both authorization and performance.
- Non-PO processing requires an approved exception, not an informal bypass.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and delivery-based recognition
11. Q: How should matching tolerances and quantity or price variances be controlled?
A: Show answer
Define tolerances by an authorized policy or configuration that specifies the transaction types covered, applicable amount or percentage, calculation basis, approval level, effective dates, and required evidence. Do not invent a tolerance within an individual case or treat a system warning as automatically acceptable. Compare quantities, unit prices, taxes, freight, discounts, accepted performance, currency, cumulative billing, and remaining contract value. Distinguish a legitimate documented adjustment from an unsupported charge. Any variance outside an approved tolerance, or any variance whose governing rule is unknown, should be held for investigation. Record the original amount, expected amount, difference, cause, resolution, reviewer, approval, and rematching result. Changes to tolerances require controlled authorization and regression testing because they can change which transactions are accepted or blocked. Report repeated exceptions to procurement, receiving, or contract management when the root cause is upstream rather than within AP.
Study instruction:
- "Test invoices just below and above an approved matching tolerance and verify that the correct outcome and escalation occur."
Key point:
- Only formally approved and applicable tolerances can clear matching differences.
- Recurring variances should trigger root-cause investigation and control review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
12. Q: How can duplicate supplier invoices be identified without rejecting legitimate transactions?
A: Show answer
Search exact and near-duplicate candidates using multiple attributes, including supplier, legal entity, original invoice number, a separately stored normalized comparison key, date, currency, amount, purchase order, receipt, description, document fingerprint, prior posting, payment, credit, replacement, and accrued-liability history. Normalization may remove certain punctuation or standardize case for comparison, but it must never overwrite the original invoice number. Two unrelated suppliers can legitimately use the same number, and the same invoice may appear under different file names or reference formats. Therefore, a matching key should trigger review rather than automatic deletion. Investigate pending, rejected, cancelled, posted, paid, credited, replaced, and accrual-settlement records. An independent reviewer must document whether the candidate is a genuine duplicate or a distinct transaction and link the supporting evidence to the case. Only the authorized disposition may determine whether the record enters clean processing totals.
Study instruction:
- "Test exact and near-duplicate invoices with differences in punctuation, supplier identity, amount, currency, and historical posting status."
Key point:
- Duplicate detection should identify candidates while preserving legitimate records.
- Original identifiers and the rationale for every disposition must remain traceable.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Working with duplicate values in Power Query
13. Q: How should supplier credit notes, cancellations, replacements, and invoice corrections be linked to the original transaction?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original invoice and create a linked record for each credit note, cancellation, replacement, correction, or other adjustment. Record the reason, supplier document, affected period, accounting and tax impact, related posting, required approvals, and reference to the original case. Recalculate the net outstanding liability after considering the authorized adjustment and verify that the reduction has not already been reflected elsewhere. A credit note for a quality issue, for example, may reduce an outstanding payable, but the original invoice should remain available for audit. Replacement invoices must not be counted alongside superseded invoices without appropriate accounting treatment. Similarly, reversing a previous posting and entering a corrected version must not cause a second credit or double reduction. The AP register should display the full relationship and current settlement position, while status changes should record who made the change, when, why, and under which authority.
Study instruction:
- "Trace an original invoice through a credit note and replacement invoice, then reconcile the final outstanding amount to the linked postings."
Key point:
- Corrections must preserve the original record and the full transaction history.
- Credits and reversals must be linked to prevent double recognition or double reduction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
14. Q: When should an expense or asset-related liability be recognized if the supplier invoice has not yet arrived?
A: Show answer
Under accrual-based accounting, recognition ordinarily follows the relevant event giving rise to the expense or asset, rather than the later arrival of the invoice or the payment date. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual's expense guidance explains the delivery principle: goods or services are recognized based on receipt or delivery, with the precise accounting treatment depending on the transaction and applicable requirements. When an organization has received qualifying goods or services before receiving the supplier's invoice, it may need to recognize the appropriate expense or asset and an accrued liability at the reporting date. The debit side must reflect the substance of the transaction; not every purchase is an immediate operating expense. An invoice received late must be assessed against the original service or delivery period and any accrual already recorded. The organization's accounting framework and policies govern the precise recognition criteria, measurement, journal, and presentation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the delivery date, invoice date, posting date, and payment date for a completed transaction and determine the appropriate recognition period."
Key point:
- Invoice arrival and cash settlement do not by themselves determine the period of expense or asset recognition.
- Recognition and measurement must follow the applicable accounting framework and evidence of performance.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and delivery-based recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
15. Q: How should the payable amount be calculated and independently verified?
A: Show answer
Establish a controlled calculation that separates line values, authorized freight and charges, approved discounts, verified tax, withholding, retention, linked credits, and authorized offsets. A typical calculation is: net value equals the total of line quantities multiplied by authorized unit prices, adjusted for authorized charges and discounts; gross value includes applicable verified taxes and adjustments; outstanding payable then reflects only supported and authorized deductions or linked credits. This formula is a calculation framework, not a substitute for jurisdiction-specific tax law or the supplier's contractual terms. Calculations should use appropriate decimal precision and approved currency-specific rounding, not rely on imprecise binary floating-point arithmetic for financial decisions. Independently recalculate material transactions and compare calculated results with the supplier invoice and approved system output. If a difference cannot be explained, retain the original figures, record the variance, and prevent posting until the issue is resolved or an authorized disposition is documented.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate an invoice from quantities and prices through tax, withholding, retention, credits, and the final payable amount."
Key point:
- Each component of the payable amount must have a traceable calculation and supporting evidence.
- Arithmetic agreement alone does not prove that a transaction is authorized or correctly classified.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
16. Q: How should tax treatment be reviewed before an AP invoice is approved?
A: Show answer
Identify the transaction's substance, applicable jurisdiction, supplier and recipient status, contract terms, invoice details, delivery or service facts, and tax rules relevant to the reporting period. Determine whether withholding, indirect tax, exemptions, recoverability, reverse-charge treatment, or other tax adjustments apply. Do not infer the correct tax treatment solely from an expense-account name, prior invoice, supplier description, or automated system code. Tax calculations and reporting may belong to a designated tax function even though AP must retain a control gate before posting. Keep the legal or official source, effective period, tax calculation, reviewer decision, evidence, and approved tax code linked to the invoice. Where applicability is unclear, hold the affected treatment and obtain a decision from a competent tax specialist. A completed spreadsheet calculation is not evidence that a tax conclusion is legally correct, and a tax hold must not be bypassed to meet an internal payment target.
Study instruction:
- "Select invoices involving different supplier types and transaction categories, and document the facts and authority needed to determine the tax treatment."
Key point:
- Tax treatment depends on verified facts and applicable law, not just the ledger description.
- Unresolved tax questions require specialist review and a documented decision.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
17. Q: How should foreign-currency invoices be measured and reconciled?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original transaction currency, foreign amount, contractual reference, relevant transaction or recognition date, authorized exchange-rate source, rate direction, conversion result, rounding method, and supporting evidence. The correct rate and date depend on the applicable accounting framework, transaction, and policy. Do not use a convenient spreadsheet rate without verifying its authorization and applicability. Distinguish initial recognition from later remeasurement or settlement; exchange differences may require separate accounting treatment rather than silently changing the original invoice value. Tax-related currency conversion may follow different requirements from accounting translation. Independently verify the calculation, retain the rate version, and examine material differences between the source amount, the accounting entry, and the outstanding payable. If the correct rate, rate date, or treatment is unresolved, use an appropriate review hold. A change in rates or calculation configuration requires controlled approval and testing before production use.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a foreign-currency payable using an authorized rate and distinguish the original transaction value from subsequent exchange differences."
Key point:
- Currency conversion requires an authorized rate, date, method, and reproducible evidence.
- Accounting and tax conversion rules must be assessed separately where they differ.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
18. Q: How should the debit-side accounting classification and financial dimensions be determined for a supplier invoice?
A: Show answer
The debit-side classification must reflect the nature of the goods or services, when the related benefits are received, and the applicable accounting policy. Depending on the facts, the debit may relate to an expense, property and equipment, inventory, a prepayment, an intangible asset, a lease-related balance, or another appropriate category. Donor funding or the fact that a project paid the invoice does not by itself determine the accounting classification. Finance should verify the legal entity, account, project, fund, donor, cost center, and other required dimensions against approved master data and transaction evidence. Asset, inventory, software, lease, and other specialist accounting decisions should be referred to their designated process where AP lacks authority to decide them. Block unsupported account mappings and invalid combinations. The resulting journal should balance, but a balanced entry is not proof that the debit account, reporting dimensions, or period is correct.
Study instruction:
- "Classify invoices involving operating expenses, inventory, assets, and prepayments, identifying the evidence and specialist approvals required."
Key point:
- Accounting classification follows the transaction's substance and the governing policy.
- Valid account coding and project dimensions are necessary for reliable financial and donor reporting.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
19. Q: What must an independent reviewer verify before an AP transaction is approved?
A: Show answer
The independent reviewer should confirm supplier identity, invoice authenticity, document completeness, purchase or contract authority, delivery or service acceptance, matching results, duplicate disposition, calculations, tax and currency treatment, account and project coding, period, payment terms, delegated approval authority, and all outstanding exceptions. Approval must refer to the exact invoice version and material transaction attributes, including entity, supplier, amount, currency, tax, coding, period, and supporting evidence. A material change after approval should trigger revalidation and the required reapproval. The reviewer should record the documents examined, questions raised, evidence obtained, corrections made, unresolved issues, and final decision. Approval should not be reduced to a generic email acknowledging receipt or a status field with no supporting record. Transactions with unresolved mandatory controls remain held, and the workflow should prevent posting or hand-off until the authorized approval conditions are met.
Study instruction:
- "Use an AP review checklist to examine one invoice and verify that approval refers to the final supporting-document version."
Key point:
- Approval is a documented decision on a specific, validated transaction.
- Material changes invalidate the assumption that the previous approval remains sufficient.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
20. Q: Under what conditions should an AP liability be posted to the general ledger?
A: Show answer
Posting should occur only after mandatory identity, evidence, matching, duplicate, accounting, tax, approval, coding, and period gates have passed, and after an authorized user has confirmed that the transaction is eligible for posting. The accounting period must be open under the organization's close policy, and the journal must reflect the correct recognition period. Retain the approved invoice version, calculations, supporting references, approval record, account and dimensions, system transaction reference, posting timestamp, and actual status evidence. Where the system uses parked documents or a workflow, distinguish the unposted document from the final posted liability. A spreadsheet flag or prepared journal is not equivalent to an official general-ledger posting. If a required prerequisite is missing, the workflow must hold the case. Any exceptional posting or correction must follow the approved authority and audit-trail requirements rather than an undocumented manual override.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one invoice from approved working papers to the official posting record and reconcile the posted entry to the invoice amount."
Key point:
- Only authorized, validated transactions may move to posted status.
- Posting evidence must come from the authoritative accounting system or approved ledger record.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
21. Q: How should failed, partial, timed-out, or ambiguous accounting-system postings be handled?
A: Show answer
An unsuccessful response does not prove that a transaction failed to post, because the system may have committed the transaction before the response was interrupted. Before retrying, query the authoritative system using the transaction reference, source batch, idempotency key where supported, or another reliable identifier. Determine whether the posting is absent, complete, partially processed, duplicated, or still ambiguous. Preserve the original attempt, response, timestamps, system evidence, and reviewer decision. Retry only after the actual state is understood and the retry mechanism is safe against duplicate posting. Partial batches require reconciliation of submitted, accepted, rejected, and posted records. Unknown states remain held and must be escalated to the system owner or authorized poster. Do not mark a record POSTED merely because an upload was attempted, a file was created, or a user reported success. After recovery, verify ledger state, control totals, and duplicate status.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate a posting timeout and demonstrate the checks needed before retrying or declaring the invoice posted."
Key point:
- A failed response and a failed accounting transaction are not necessarily the same event.
- The actual ledger state must be verified before retry or closure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
22. Q: How should supplier payment terms and due dates be calculated and monitored?
A: Show answer
Use the executed contract, authorized purchase order, and applicable law to establish the payment-term clause and calculation convention. Record the date from which the term runs, day-count method, calendar rules, non-business-day convention, discount window, and due date. Independently recompute the result and compare it with any ERP-calculated date. Do not assume that all suppliers receive the same default term or that an invoice date is always the date from which the contract counts. Unauthorized changes to terms or dates can distort aging, create premature payments, miss discounts, or conceal overdue obligations. The terms register should retain the contractual reference, approved interpretation, date calculations, reviewer, and effective status. If the relevant term or calendar rule remains uncertain, use an appropriate hold and request clarification from the contract owner. Urgency must not authorize an undocumented variation to supplier terms.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate payment dates for several invoice terms, including a non-business day and a potential discount deadline."
Key point:
- Due dates must follow verified contractual and legal requirements.
- Unapproved changes to dates or terms can undermine payment control and liability aging.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
23. Q: Where should the boundary between AP liability processing and payment execution be drawn?
A: Show answer
AP should establish that the liability is genuine, correctly classified, authorized, approved, posted, and supported by the necessary records. Once the defined gates pass, the AP process can transmit the controlled hand-off to the designated payment process, identified in the example as SOP 021. The receiving process should obtain the AP record identifier, posting identifier, legal entity, supplier, invoice version, currency, outstanding posted amount, tax and withholding information, contractual terms, due date, account dimensions, approvals, supporting references, and hold or dispute status. The receiving process should acknowledge acceptance or return the record with a documented reason and owner. The hand-off must not be described as bank authorization, payment-batch approval, payment execution, or proof of settlement. Those are separate responsibilities supported by their own authorization and bank evidence. If the real SOP 021 or equivalent process does not exist or its authority is unverified, implementation must remain pending.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a mock AP-to-payment hand-off and identify which evidence proves posting, hand-off acceptance, payment authorization, and bank settlement."
Key point:
- Posted liability, accepted hand-off, authorized payment, and completed settlement are distinct states.
- Each process must retain its own authorization and evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
24. Q: When should accrued liabilities or received-not-invoiced items be recorded at period-end?
A: Show answer
At each reporting cut-off, identify goods received or services performed and accepted before the cut-off for which the supplier invoice has not yet been received or processed. Establish that an obligation exists, determine the appropriate account classification, identify the period to which the goods or services relate, and gather evidence from goods-received records, service-acceptance forms, milestones, approved contracts, and other reliable sources. An open purchase order alone does not prove that goods were delivered or services were performed. Future or unperformed services should not automatically be accrued merely because budget or contract value remains available. The UN IPSAS expense guidance supports recognizing expenses based on the applicable delivery or performance principle, while the precise accrual criteria and method must follow the entity's approved framework and policies. Record the source event, measurement basis, estimate, reviewer, posting reference, reversal or release plan, and subsequent-invoice follow-up responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Review the period-end receipt and service records and identify obligations incurred before the cut-off for which invoices remain outstanding."
Key point:
- Recognition depends on the relevant obligation and delivery or performance evidence, not merely an open purchase order.
- Accruals require support, review, posting evidence, and a settlement plan.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and delivery-based recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
25. Q: How should an accrued liability be estimated when the final supplier invoice is unavailable?
A: Show answer
Use the best available authorized evidence, such as an accepted quantity multiplied by an approved contractual rate, a verified service milestone, approved hours, or another evidence-based estimate permitted by the applicable policy. Document the source event, recognition date, period, quantity, rate or estimation method, currency, assumptions, uncertainty, expected invoice timing, preparer, independent reviewer, approver, journal reference, and planned reversal or settlement. The estimate must be proportionate to the information available and should not simply copy a previous month without evidence that the current obligation is comparable. Material uncertainty or disputed performance requires escalation. Where amounts cannot be reliably supported or the accounting treatment is uncertain, consult the applicable authority and specialist rather than inventing a number. Subsequent invoices should be linked to the estimate so that any difference can be analyzed and adjusted using the approved process.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a supported accrual estimate from acceptance evidence and contractual rates, and document the assumptions and review required."
Key point:
- Accrual estimates need a documented, reproducible measurement basis.
- Unsupported estimates and unexplained roll-forwards create misstatement risk.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
26. Q: How should later supplier invoices be matched to existing accruals to prevent double recognition?
A: Show answer
Maintain a unique accrual identifier linked to the service period or delivery event, supporting evidence, estimate, posting reference, reversal or release entry, and subsequent supplier invoice. When the invoice arrives, compare the actual bill with the accrued amount, confirm the previously recognized item covers the same goods or services, and determine the authorized treatment for reversing, releasing, or adjusting the accrual. Do not recognize the full invoice as a new expense while leaving the corresponding accrual unreconciled. Also avoid automatically clearing unrelated accruals simply because supplier, description, or amount appears similar. Record any difference between the estimate and actual invoice and explain whether it reflects a measurement estimate, a different period, a quantity change, a price change, a credit, or a genuine error. Reconcile the remaining balance and assign a follow-up owner until the case is fully settled.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one accrual through its reversal, actual invoice, adjustment, and final reconciliation, verifying that no cost is counted twice."
Key point:
- Subsequent invoices must be linked to the accruals they settle.
- Reversal and true-up procedures must preserve both the original entry and the correction history.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and journal workflows
27. Q: How should AP cut-off testing identify liabilities omitted from the reporting period?
A: Show answer
Begin with the full population of accepted goods, completed services, receiving records, service-entry records, supplier invoices received after the reporting date, open AP items, recurring obligations, contract milestones, and unresolved accruals. Examine transactions around the cut-off and perform a look-forward review of subsequent invoices and credit notes. Establish the date of delivery or service performance, the relevant obligation, the appropriate period, the source evidence, and the accounting treatment. Do not use the invoice or payment date as a universal substitute for the underlying event. Reconcile the review population to the AP register, accrual register, and relevant ledger accounts. Record missing liabilities, duplicate recognition, period errors, and unresolved evidence gaps as exceptions with an owner and resolution date. The closing reviewer should assess whether all material differences have been corrected or handled under an approved close policy before the period is finalized.
Study instruction:
- "Perform a cut-off review using subsequent invoices and receipt records, documenting the evidence for each inclusion or exclusion."
Key point:
- Cut-off completeness requires a population-based review and investigation of later evidence.
- A zero reconciliation difference alone cannot prove that all liabilities were identified.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
28. Q: How should disputed or unsupported supplier invoices be controlled?
A: Show answer
A disputed invoice must retain its original record and be assigned a documented dispute status. Record the disputed amount, reason, contract or delivery issue, missing evidence, affected period, responsible owner, required action, follow-up date, and escalation route. If only part of an invoice is disputed, distinguish the disputed portion from the undisputed portion, but do not release any portion contrary to the applicable contract, accounting policy, or approval rules. Obtain independent evidence from procurement, receiving, the service owner, or the supplier as appropriate. Acknowledging receipt of a complaint or obtaining a verbal assurance does not resolve the issue. Any adjustment, credit, settlement, reversal, or change in classification must receive the required authority and be linked to the original transaction. Fraud or legal concerns require the designated escalation route. Keep unresolved cases visible in the AP and management reports rather than hiding them in miscellaneous balances.
Study instruction:
- "Document a disputed invoice from initial identification through evidence collection, authorized resolution, accounting adjustment, and closure."
Key point:
- Disputes require explicit ownership, evidence, deadlines, and approved resolution.
- A disputed status must not be removed solely to meet a reporting or payment deadline.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
29. Q: What controls should apply when a supplier requests a change to bank or remittance details?
A: Show answer
Treat supplier remittance changes as a distinct, high-risk master-data event. Verify the change through a contact method independently obtained from previously approved records or another trusted source. Do not rely solely on the invoice, an email reply, a new telephone number provided in the request, or urgency asserted by the supplier. Require documented evidence of the request, independent verification, review by an authorized person, separate approval, effective date, and an audit record of the old and new information. Where possible, restrict the person who changes the master record from approving the related invoice or releasing its payment. A mismatch, unusual change, suspected compromise, or conflict of interest must trigger a hold and the designated fraud-escalation route. Preserve evidence securely and do not disclose restricted investigation details more widely than necessary. Payment processing should resume only after the change and affected transactions have been independently cleared.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate a bank-detail change request and demonstrate independent verification, authorization, evidence preservation, and release criteria."
Key point:
- Supplier remittance changes require independent verification and separate authorization.
- Email familiarity and payment urgency are not proof of authenticity.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
30. Q: How should the AP subledger be reconciled to the general-ledger control account?
A: Show answer
Reconcile the complete AP subledger population to the relevant GL control account for the same entity, currency basis, and period. Compare opening balances, additions, credits, settlements, adjustments, reclassifications, and closing balances. Obtain report identifiers, extraction timestamps, and control totals to establish which records were included. Investigate reconciling items individually, including missing postings, duplicate postings, unsupported manual journals, wrong supplier accounts, unrecognized credits, period differences, and interface errors. A balancing total does not prove that the underlying population is complete or accurately classified. Each difference should have a reason, an owner, a resolution deadline, and supporting evidence. Manual correcting journals must be independently reviewed, authorized, posted to the correct period, and linked to the issue they resolve. Retain the signed reconciliation and evidence of follow-up so the close reviewer can distinguish resolved differences from those still outstanding.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an AP-to-GL reconciliation using full populations and explain every reconciling item through to its approved resolution."
Key point:
- Subledger and GL balances must reconcile using comparable populations and periods.
- An unsupported manual journal must not be used to conceal an unexplained difference.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
31. Q: How should supplier statements be reconciled to AP records and outstanding balances?
A: Show answer
Compare each supplier-statement line with the invoice register, posted liabilities, credit notes, authorized payments, and known timing differences. Reconcile opening balances, invoices, credits, cash settlements, and ending balances where a complete statement is available. Investigate invoices appearing on the statement but missing internally, AP items absent from the supplier statement, unexplained credits, duplicate billings, payment-allocation differences, and unresolved disputed items. Obtain missing invoices or supporting records from the appropriate owner and confirm that any supplier-statement discrepancy is not already explained by a payment in transit or another legitimate timing difference. Supplier confirmation may help evidence a balance but does not by itself validate every invoice's authorization, receipt, classification, or tax treatment. Each unreconciled line should have a responsible owner, next action, and due date. Escalate material or recurring differences and preserve correspondence and final reconciliation evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a supplier statement line by line and classify each difference as a supported timing item, a missing record, a credit, or an unresolved exception."
Key point:
- Supplier statements are an important external reconciliation source, not a substitute for internal transaction evidence.
- Every unmatched line needs a documented explanation or follow-up action.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
32. Q: How should outstanding payables be aged and escalated without losing sight of disputes or accounting errors?
A: Show answer
Develop aging from the correct approved due-date basis and the outstanding posted amount, rather than relying on invoice receipt date alone when it is not the contractual measure. Define consistent aging bands, identify amounts approaching due dates, and distinguish approved but not yet due balances from overdue, disputed, held, partially settled, credited, or unverified items. Investigate negative supplier balances, long-outstanding credits, unmatched deposits, and apparent overdue items whose supporting records are incomplete. Aging information should identify supplier, entity, currency, due date, contract reference, posting status, dispute status, responsible owner, and required action. Do not change invoice or receipt dates to make an old balance appear current. Review large, unusual, long-outstanding, or repeatedly disputed balances with management, procurement, and the relevant contract owner. Aging reports should reconcile to the AP subledger and explain their treatment of excluded or held transactions.
Study instruction:
- "Build an AP aging report and investigate overdue invoices, long-outstanding credits, disputed balances, and records with altered or unsupported dates."
Key point:
- Aging depends on accurate dates, outstanding amounts, and clear status definitions.
- Overdue status must trigger follow-up, not automatic approval or alteration of evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
33. Q: How should journal corrections, reclassifications, reversals, and prior-period errors be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Determine whether the proposed entry corrects a posting error, changes an accounting estimate, reclassifies an appropriately recognized balance, reverses a temporary accrual, or addresses an issue from a previously reported period. These events are not interchangeable, and their treatment may differ under the applicable accounting framework. Trace the proposed entry to the original document, account mapping, recognition period, approval, and reconciliation. A reclassification should not be used to conceal an omitted liability; an accrual reversal should not erase a valid obligation; and a prior-period error should not be labelled a current-period estimate change without a basis. Each journal should identify the debit and credit, amounts, currency, entity, dimensions, period, preparer, independent reviewer, explanation, and linked original posting where applicable. Material corrections should be assessed for their impact on financial statements, donor reports, close status, and any downstream approvals or hand-offs.
Study instruction:
- "Compare examples of a correcting journal, accrual reversal, estimate change, and reclassification, documenting the accounting basis for each."
Key point:
- Journals must reflect the substance of the issue rather than the easiest available correction route.
- Original entries and approved correction histories must remain traceable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
34. Q: How should AP exceptions be recorded, investigated, and formally closed?
A: Show answer
Record each exception with a unique identifier, affected invoice or accrual, amount and currency, period, risk level, exception type, missing evidence, root cause, containment action, responsible owner, deadline, escalation route, corrective action, and required independent retest. Examples include missing receipts, incomplete supplier identity, tax uncertainty, duplicate candidates, incorrect calculations, failed postings, reconciliation differences, suspicious remittance requests, and policy or authority gaps. Distinguish the cause from the symptom: recurring late invoices may result from upstream receiving delays rather than AP inattention. The closure criteria must specify the objective evidence needed, the person authorized to verify resolution, and whether related records or controls require updating. An email acknowledgment, verbal direction, or managerial awareness alone is not closure. If a case cannot be resolved by the deadline, it must remain open or be escalated under the approved policy. Trend the exceptions to identify control weaknesses that require redesign.
Study instruction:
- "Create an exception register and demonstrate how evidence, root-cause analysis, corrective action, independent retesting, and closure are linked."
Key point:
- Exceptions need owners, deadlines, evidence, and explicit closure criteria.
- Recurring issues should lead to preventive improvements, not repeated manual workarounds.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger controls
35. Q: What audit trail must be retained to demonstrate the integrity of an AP transaction?
A: Show answer
Retain the original invoice, source-channel details, immutable case identifier, supplier verification, purchase order or contract, receipt or service acceptance, matching worksheets, duplicate disposition, calculations, tax and foreign-exchange decisions, account coding, reviews, approvals, posting reference, status changes, adjustment history, reconciliation, and downstream hand-off. Record the responsible actor, timestamp, action, reason, before-and-after values or status, system reference, and evidence location for material changes. Preserve failed posting attempts and superseded documents where they explain what happened. File naming and versions should make the authoritative document obvious without destroying earlier evidence. The trail should support two-way tracing: from the GL balance to individual documents and from a source document to its ultimate accounting and workflow result. Documentation alone does not make a control effective; the underlying control must actually have been performed and independently evidenced.
Study instruction:
- "Select a posted liability and reconstruct its full history from original source document through approval, posting, reconciliation, and hand-off."
Key point:
- A complete audit trail shows what happened, who acted, when, why, and under which authority.
- Evidence should preserve both the final result and material changes or failed attempts.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
36. Q: How should supplier records and AP evidence be protected through access restrictions and retention controls?
A: Show answer
Limit access to supplier identity, bank details, tax information, personal data, contracts, and fraud-investigation evidence according to legitimate job responsibilities. Maintain an access register and review users, delegated roles, external advisers, and permissions following staff changes or role reassignment. Supplier data should be stored in approved repositories, and sensitive information should not be copied into general-purpose workbooks or shared folders without authorization. Retention periods must be derived from applicable laws, donor agreements, contracts, audit requirements, and approved organizational policy; do not invent a universal retention period. Apply legal or donor holds where required and document secure disposal when retention ends. Test backups and retrieval procedures periodically to establish that records can actually be restored and read. The SOP example explicitly treats actual retention and privacy requirements as unverified, so adoption requires authoritative review of the relevant obligations and evidence of approval.
Study instruction:
- "Review the AP evidence repository's access matrix, retention authority, backup procedures, and secure-disposal controls."
Key point:
- Access and retention controls must be tied to verified requirements and accountable owners.
- A backup is reliable only when retrieval and restoration have been tested.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
37. Q: Which management indicators can demonstrate whether AP controls are operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Useful measures include invoice volume, intake-to-review time, approval cycle time, proportion of invoices with complete supporting evidence, non-PO frequency, duplicate alerts and confirmed duplicates, matching exceptions, tax and coding holds, failed postings, accrual age, overdue balances, unresolved reconciliation differences, exception recurrence, unauthorized overrides, segregation-of-duties conflicts, and the proportion of obligations posted in the correct period. Every metric requires a defined population, calculation method, data source, reporting frequency, owner, threshold, and follow-up action. For example, an on-time processing rate should use a defined population of eligible invoices, not only invoices that completed successfully. Metrics should be reconciled to source systems and accompanied by explanations for material changes. A lower exception count is not automatically evidence of better control if users have been bypassing controls or failing to log issues. Management should review trends, root causes, and corrective actions rather than focusing exclusively on speed.
Study instruction:
- "Define a monthly AP dashboard with reproducible KPI calculations and test the results against the invoice register and exception records."
Key point:
- Effective KPI reporting requires consistent definitions and reliable source populations.
- Speed measures must not reward bypassing evidence, approval, or reconciliation controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
38. Q: How should workflow statuses and transition rules prevent premature approval, posting, or hand-off?
A: Show answer
Define each status precisely, including its entry criteria, permitted actions, responsible role, required evidence, next eligible states, and closure conditions. For example, RECEIVED means the source document has arrived, not that authenticity is established. INCOMPLETE requires a missing-item list and an owner; MATCH_EXCEPTION requires investigation and resolution; POSTED requires authoritative posting evidence; and a hand-off-ready status requires all AP gates to have passed. The workflow should prohibit protected transitions when prerequisites are missing, including moving a held invoice to approved, posted, or hand-off-ready merely by editing a status field. Retain transition history and record the person or system responsible, event time, supporting evidence, and reason. Exception routes need documented failure behavior and an authorized return path. Test both successful transitions and negative paths in which a missing approval, document, or system result must block further progress.
Study instruction:
- "Create a workflow status matrix and test that incomplete, duplicate, unapproved, and ambiguously posted invoices cannot reach a protected terminal state."
Key point:
- Status changes must represent evidence-backed business events.
- Negative-path tests must prove that mandatory gates cannot be bypassed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
39. Q: Which controlled forms and registers should support the AP process?
A: Show answer
The minimum working set typically includes an invoice intake and validation checklist, supplier identity and authenticity record, supplier-master change form, PO and receipt-matching worksheet, duplicate and credit-note register, accounting and tax decision record, approval and posting log, payment-term calculation, accrual and GRNI register, cut-off review sheet, reconciliation workpapers, exception and fraud register, hand-off record, and retention/access register. Each form should have a stable identifier, purpose, trigger, required fields, owner, reviewer, approval authority, permitted statuses, applicable source references, completion dates, and closure evidence. Fields should be standardized so that documents can be linked across processes without manual guesswork. Do not create a form simply to reproduce information already reliably captured elsewhere; establish the authoritative record and references to avoid duplicate administration. All controlled forms must be versioned, approved, and tested before release, while draft forms must remain visibly distinct from operative procedures.
Study instruction:
- "Inventory the existing AP forms and compare their required fields, owners, approvals, status definitions, and evidence against the actual workflow."
Key point:
- Controlled records make ownership, evidence, and cross-process traceability explicit.
- Forms must be approved and maintained as part of the operational procedure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
40. Q: How can Excel tables and Power Query improve invoice matching and AP reporting without changing accounting rules?
A: Show answer
Excel tables provide a structured data format for invoice registers, supplier master data, receiving records, duplicate candidates, accruals, and reconciliation outputs. Power Query can import and normalize data from approved sources, standardize comparison fields, merge invoice records with supplier and receipt data, and produce candidate exception lists. The transformations should preserve original identifiers and important source fields alongside any normalized values. Exact or fuzzy matching identifies records that need investigation; it does not determine by itself whether an invoice is genuine, authorized, or a duplicate. Before refreshing or publishing outputs, reconcile row counts and control totals, document the source timestamps, and investigate unexpected changes. Maintain a separate approved mapping and accounting decision layer so technical transformations do not become an undocumented source of accounting rules. Refreshes, query changes, and output generation should be logged and tested against representative cases before use in a live close process.
Study instruction:
- "Build a query that combines invoice, supplier, and receipt tables, then reconcile row counts and test both matching and exception cases."
Key point:
- Data transformation can improve consistency and review efficiency.
- Accounting recognition, authorization, and tax decisions must remain governed by approved requirements.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Merge queries overview in Power Query
- Microsoft Support — Create and format tables in Excel
41. Q: How should automated duplicate detection avoid deleting valid invoice history or corrupting reporting totals?
A: Show answer
Keep original invoice identifiers unchanged and calculate a separate normalized key with a documented transformation version. The duplicate process should examine supplier and entity, invoice number, currency, amount, dates, purchase order, receipt references, prior posting and payment records, credit notes, replacement history, and related accruals. Exact matches can be flagged using approved keys, while near-duplicate candidates may require multiple attributes or fuzzy matching. A matching score is a screening signal, not proof of duplication. Avoid automatically deleting records or assuming that a particular record will always be preserved when a query removes duplicates; the query output must be tested for deterministic and business-appropriate behavior. Excluded items should remain traceable in an exception register with their disposition and reviewer. Reconcile source and output populations, explain removals or exclusions, and verify that legitimate records remain represented in the approved totals.
Study instruction:
- "Create a duplicate test set with repeated keys and legitimate similar invoices, then confirm that every exclusion has an evidence-backed disposition."
Key point:
- Automated matching identifies candidates; independent review determines the final disposition.
- Traceable exception handling protects valid records and reporting completeness.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Working with duplicate values in Power Query
42. Q: What should the AP team do when goods have been received but the invoice is missing at month-end?
A: Show answer
First, obtain the approved purchase order or contract, goods receipt or inspection evidence, acceptance date, quantity, applicable price, and evidence that the obligation belongs to the reporting period. Search the invoice register and subsequent correspondence to confirm that the supplier invoice has not already been recorded elsewhere. If the applicable accounting policy requires recognition and the amount can be supported, prepare an accrued-liability entry using the approved account classification and a documented measurement basis. Have the entry independently reviewed, approved, posted, and assigned a unique accrual reference. Record the expected invoice, responsible owner, reversal or release date, and follow-up plan. An open purchase order without evidence of delivery is insufficient. When the invoice arrives, link it to the accrual, reconcile the actual amount, and reverse, release, or adjust the estimate under the approved policy. Escalate uncertain receipt, quantity, or contractual obligations rather than manufacturing a supportable-looking estimate.
Study instruction:
- "Work through a received-not-invoiced case from receipt evidence and estimation to posting, subsequent invoicing, and final accrual settlement."
Key point:
- Accruals must reflect supported obligations incurred by the reporting cut-off.
- Subsequent invoicing must settle the linked accrual without duplicating the cost.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
43. Q: How should AP handle an invoice that exceeds the accepted quantity or authorized contract value?
A: Show answer
Place the affected transaction on hold and identify the nature of the difference. Compare the invoice with the approved order or contract, accepted quantity, delivery or service records, prior invoicing, returns, credit notes, milestones, and cumulative contract ceiling. Determine whether the difference is a legitimate authorized variation, a billing error, an unaccepted delivery, a possible duplicate, or an unsupported charge. The person responsible for the source contract must provide approved evidence of any amendment or variation; an invoice itself cannot authorize an increased contract value. Recalculate the payable amount and rematch the invoice after the supporting record has been updated through the approved process. Retain the original and amended contract references, approval, variance analysis, reviewer decision, and final calculation. Where the difference remains unresolved, maintain the hold and communicate the expected resolution date to the owner and relevant management.
Study instruction:
- "Investigate a supplier invoice exceeding accepted quantity and contract value, documenting the approved source of any permitted change."
Key point:
- Supplier billing cannot independently authorize a contract variation.
- Cumulative billing and accepted quantities must be verified before release.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
44. Q: How should the organization prevent reimbursement, payroll, travel advance, card, partner advance, or tax transactions from bypassing their controlling process?
A: Show answer
Identify the transaction's substance and routing requirement before creating an AP record. Employee reimbursements, payroll items, travel settlements, corporate-card charges, project advances, partner advances, and tax filings may have dedicated procedures that define evidence, approval, accounting, settlement, and reporting. Refer them to the relevant process and retain the referral identifier and receiving owner's acknowledgment. Do not convert a transaction into a generic supplier invoice merely because the source process is delayed or the AP workflow is easier to use. Such routing can bypass payroll controls, duplicate a reimbursement, misclassify an advance, or make the related balance difficult to reconcile. If ownership is disputed or the relevant SOP is missing, hold the case and obtain an authorized process decision. After referral, verify that the originating AP register records the appropriate status so the same cost or liability is not accidentally processed twice through parallel systems.
Study instruction:
- "Classify sample reimbursement, payroll, travel, corporate-card, and partner-advance cases, then identify the controlling process and required referral evidence."
Key point:
- Transaction routing depends on the nature of the process, not only on who submitted the document.
- Referrals must prevent both control bypass and duplicate recognition.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
45. Q: What should be verified before an AP SOP is adopted and released for organizational use?
A: Show answer
Treat adoption as a controlled release decision, not merely a document-formatting exercise. Verify the actual legal entity, accounting framework, applicable policies, supplier and procurement arrangements, delegated authorities, tax requirements, donor and contractual conditions, account mappings, payment terms, related SOPs, system capabilities, role assignments, access permissions, evidence retention requirements, and escalation routes. Confirm that source references are current and applicable, that every required form and decision table exists, and that status transitions have defined success and failure behavior. Test representative invoices, duplicate scenarios, disputed items, credits, accruals, currency and tax calculations, posting failures, reconciliation differences, and hand-offs using clearly identified test data. Perform independent review of accounting treatment and control effectiveness. Record test outcomes, unresolved defects, approver, effective date, training, version, and rollback or remediation plan. A successful file save or structural check does not prove that the SOP works operationally or that it is authorized. The supplied DOCX expressly marks itself as fictional and not adopted; actual release therefore requires the organization's own evidence and approval.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption checklist and demonstrate that all mandatory authority, configuration, training, testing, and approval gates have objective supporting evidence."
Key point:
- An SOP becomes operative only through verified applicability, successful testing, and authorized adoption.
- Fictional examples and automated test results must not be represented as proof of actual organizational compliance.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
