Table 2
Payment Processing and Disbursement Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of an integrated payment processing and bank disbursement procedure?
A: Show answer
An integrated payment procedure establishes how an outgoing payment moves from an authorized obligation to verified payee details, supporting evidence, accurate calculation, independent review, delegated approval, bank execution, confirmation of payment status, accounting updates, and reconciliation hand-off. Its objective is to ensure that every disbursement is valid, accurate, authorized, traceable, and supported by evidence. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an adopted policy or proof of an actual payment. An organization must verify its legal identity, bank mandate, delegation of authority (DoA), source procedures, donor conditions, applicable laws, and retention rules before operational adoption. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides useful reference procedures for accounts payable, cash management, and general ledger processing, but its Umoja-specific instructions should not automatically be treated as mandatory procedures for another NGO.

Study instruction:
- "Map one payment from the original obligation through bank-status confirmation and reconciliation hand-off, identifying the evidence required at every stage."

Key point:
- A payment requires valid authorization, independent controls, and sufficient evidence.
- A demonstration SOP must be verified and formally approved before operational use.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
2. Q: Which payment categories should be covered by a comprehensive NGO payment procedure?
A: Show answer
The procedure should cover supplier payments, employee reimbursements, payroll disbursements, tax and statutory payments, partner or subgrant payments, staff advances, beneficiary payments, interoffice transfers, foreign-currency payments, recurring instructions, bank charges, returned payments, failed transactions, cancellations, and recalls. Each category should have a defined source process, evidence checklist, approval route, payment method, accounting interface, and exception process. A payment category should not automatically bypass controls because its amount is small, the recipient is familiar, or the transaction occurs frequently. The procedure should identify which activities it controls directly and which remain the responsibility of procurement, payroll, grant management, program operations, tax, or formal bank reconciliation. Requirements that depend on the actual organization, donor agreement, or local law must be verified separately.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory all payment types processed during a representative month and map each type to its source procedure, approval route, and required evidence."

Key point:
- Different payment types may require different evidence and specialist review.
- Scope boundaries must be explicit so upstream authorization is not confused with payment execution.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
3. Q: How should payment authorization be distinguished from payment processing and bank disbursement?
A: Show answer
Payment authorization establishes that the underlying obligation is legitimate, eligible, properly supported, and approved by the appropriate authority. Payment processing verifies the payee, amount, supporting documents, deductions, payment method, funding conditions, and other transaction-specific requirements. Bank disbursement is the separate execution of the authorized instruction through a permitted bank account and channel. These stages are connected but do not replace one another. An approved purchase does not prove that its invoice is correct, and an approved payment batch does not automatically establish that the bank executed it. Similarly, a bank transaction does not demonstrate that the original expenditure was eligible under the donor agreement. The procedure should preserve evidence for each stage and define the responsible owner and status transition.

Study instruction:
- "Take one supplier invoice and distinguish the evidence supporting the purchase, invoice validation, payment approval, bank authorization, and actual settlement."

Key point:
- Upstream approval, payment approval, and bank authorization are distinct controls.
- Each stage needs its own accountable owner and evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
4. Q: How should the organization determine which policies and legal requirements govern a payment?
A: Show answer
Establish a controlled authority register covering applicable legislation, executed bank mandates, donor or partner agreements, organizational policies, approved delegations, payment-channel instructions, and the relevant accounting framework. For each source, record its owner, exact reference, scope, effective date, review date, applicability decision, reviewer, and any unresolved uncertainty. Do not infer that a law governing banks or payment-service providers automatically imposes identical obligations on every payer. Likewise, do not assume a UN system procedure applies directly to an independent NGO. When requirements conflict or applicability is unclear, hold the affected transaction and obtain a decision from the designated legal, compliance, treasury, or other competent reviewer. Record the decision before releasing the payment.

Study instruction:
- "Review a sample authority register and identify any missing mandates, expired delegations, unverified legal references, or unresolved applicability questions."

Key point:
- Authority must be current, relevant, and applicable to the actual transaction.
- Unresolved legal or policy conflicts must not be settled through informal assumptions.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 1 — Introduction and Finance Manual structure
5. Q: What evidence must be verified before the SOP is formally adopted?
A: Show answer
Before adoption, confirm the legal entity's actual name and status, bank accounts and mandates, authorized signatories, individual bank users, approval thresholds, current delegation matrix, source SOPs, accounting framework, donor terms, tax responsibilities, retention rules, and records-management policies. Map each requirement to an authoritative document or independently verified record. Identify missing information as an explicit implementation blocker rather than filling gaps with invented names, thresholds, account details, or signatures. Assign an owner and target date for every outstanding item. After the required evidence is reviewed, obtain approval from the authorized governance or policy owner, issue a controlled version, communicate the effective date, and withdraw obsolete drafts from operational use.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption checklist comparing every mandatory organizational parameter with the controlled evidence available and record unresolved gaps."

Key point:
- A well-written document is not evidence that the organization has approved or implemented it.
- Actual entity-specific documents and approvals must support operational release.
6. Q: Which roles should participate in a controlled payment lifecycle?
A: Show answer
A controlled lifecycle may involve the requester, source-document owner, procurement or payroll processor, payee verifier, payment preparer, independent reviewer, delegated approver, bank maker, bank checker or signatories, specialist reviewers, source-system operator, and reconciliation owner. The actual role names depend on organizational structure and banking arrangements. Responsibilities should identify who prepares the transaction, verifies source evidence, confirms payee details, validates calculations, approves the payment, executes the bank instruction, verifies settlement, records accounting entries, and performs formal reconciliation. The role matrix must also identify deputies, approval limits, incompatible duties, escalation routes, and access permissions. No job title alone proves authority; actual permissions must match current mandates and approved delegations.

Study instruction:
- "Build a responsibility matrix for each payment stage and compare it with actual staff assignments, system permissions, and bank authorization records."

Key point:
- Every stage needs a clearly accountable role.
- Responsibilities must match actual authority and system access.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
7. Q: What is segregation of duties, and why is it essential in payment processing?
A: Show answer
Segregation of duties separates activities that could otherwise allow one individual to initiate an improper payment, conceal an error, or manipulate records without independent detection. Where practical, the requester should not independently verify a payee change, prepare the payment, approve it, execute it, and reconcile the resulting bank transaction. Different people should perform incompatible activities, particularly payee verification, payment preparation, approval, bank execution, and independent reconciliation. Review the actual combination of permissions rather than relying only on job descriptions. Where staffing constraints prevent full separation, document compensating controls such as independent transaction review, restricted bank permissions, post-payment verification, and periodic access review. These controls must be demonstrably performed, not simply stated in policy.

Study instruction:
- "Identify incompatible duties in a sample payment workflow and propose a documented compensating control for each unavoidable conflict."

Key point:
- Independent control reduces the ability to create and conceal unauthorized payments.
- Compensating controls require evidence of actual operation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
8. Q: How should maker, checker, and approver responsibilities be distinguished?
A: Show answer
The maker prepares the payment instruction or transaction, the checker independently validates the relevant details, and the approver confirms that the transaction falls within the required authority and is ready for release. A bank maker/checker arrangement is not automatically equivalent to the organization's internal payment approval. For example, a manager may approve a payment internally while the bank still requires two authorized signatories. The procedure should define which fields and evidence each role examines, what limits apply, which combinations are prohibited, and how rejections or amendments return for review. A checker must independently inspect the evidence rather than merely confirm that someone else has completed the task. The system or audit record should retain each person's identity, timestamp, decision, and relevant transaction version.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one payment through internal preparation, checking, approval, bank submission, and signatory authorization; identify every distinct decision."

Key point:
- Internal approval and bank authorization are separate control layers.
- A checker must perform an independent review of the actual transaction.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
9. Q: What should happen when a payment request is incomplete or inconsistent?
A: Show answer
Stop the affected request before it enters an executable payment batch. Record which field or document is missing, the responsible person, the date identified, the payment deadline, the risk of proceeding, and the evidence required to resolve the issue. Return the request to the originating unit or route it to the designated specialist where classification or authority is uncertain. Do not infer a missing approval, use an unverified account number, or substitute a similar transaction as evidence. If only one item in a batch is defective, exclude it from the releasable population while retaining control over the remaining items, provided batch integrity can be maintained and the approved policy permits partial processing. Preserve the original request and the resolution history.

Study instruction:
- "Test an incomplete payment request and document the hold reason, owner, remediation, approval, and evidence needed before release."

Key point:
- Missing or contradictory evidence must create a visible hold.
- A deadline does not convert an incomplete request into an authorized payment.
10. Q: What information should a standardized payment intake form contain?
A: Show answer
A payment intake form should identify the legal payer, payment category, source transaction, payee, requested amount, currency, due date, purpose, project or grant code, proposed payment channel, supporting documents, upstream approval, requester, and responsible source-data owner. Add fields for payee verification, duplicate screening, calculation review, funding or compliance gates, approval status, bank execution reference, settlement evidence, and accounting update where applicable. Use unique transaction identifiers and controlled status values so the same record can be followed throughout the process. Required fields should depend on payment type, with conditional checks for payroll, advances, partner payments, taxes, or foreign currency. Do not collect passwords, authentication codes, or unnecessary sensitive personal information in the form.

Study instruction:
- "Design a payment intake form and test it with supplier invoices, reimbursements, advances, and statutory payments to identify missing mandatory fields."

Key point:
- Standardized intake improves completeness, traceability, and consistent processing.
- Sensitive information should be limited to what the process genuinely requires.
11. Q: How should the organization verify that the payer is using the correct legal entity and bank account?
A: Show answer
Compare the payer named in the approved obligation and payment instruction with the legal entity associated with the selected bank account, the applicable mandate, and the permitted payment purpose. Verify the account's ownership or authorized use through controlled bank or organizational evidence. Confirm that the account is active, the payment channel is permitted, the currency is supported, and the transaction falls within applicable limits. Do not assume that an account can be used merely because it appears in an old spreadsheet or was used for a previous payment. Cross-entity transactions, interoffice transfers, and payments made on behalf of another organization require documented authority and appropriate accounting treatment. Any mismatch should block release until the responsible reviewer resolves it.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a sample payment request, source obligation, legal-entity record, and bank mandate to verify the payer-account relationship."

Key point:
- The payer, obligation, and account must be consistent with authorized arrangements.
- Historical usage does not establish current account authority.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
12. Q: What checks are needed when onboarding a new payee?
A: Show answer
Payee onboarding should establish the recipient's identity, legal or individual status, relevant registration or tax details, authorized representatives where applicable, payment purpose, and bank details. Use reliable documents and approved verification channels appropriate to the recipient and risk. Compare payee names and account-holder information, investigate material mismatches, and record the reviewer, date, source evidence, and decision. Apply additional checks for international recipients, implementing partners, politically or legally sensitive cases, or other higher-risk transactions when required by law or organizational policy. An invoice by itself does not necessarily establish that the bank account belongs to the intended recipient. New payees should remain on hold until the mandatory verification steps are completed and the approved master-data record is established.

Study instruction:
- "Review a new-payee file and determine whether identity, bank ownership, payment purpose, and required approvals are supported by reliable evidence."

Key point:
- Payee onboarding is an independent control, not merely data entry.
- Unverified recipients must not be released for payment.
13. Q: How should changes to payee bank details be controlled?
A: Show answer
Treat bank-detail changes as high-risk master-data events because a fraudulent change can redirect a legitimate payment. Authenticate the request through a trusted contact channel already held in approved records, rather than relying exclusively on a phone number or email address included in the change request itself. Have a separate person verify the new details independently, obtain any required approval, document effective dates, and retain masked before-and-after values and verification evidence. Review whether pending payment batches contain the old or new details and determine whether they must be revalidated. Restrict who can edit bank master data and periodically inspect change logs. If a request appears suspicious, maintain a hold, preserve evidence, and follow the incident-response procedure before making further changes or payments.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a bank-detail change received by email and identify how trusted-channel verification, independent approval, and batch revalidation should operate."

Key point:
- Bank-detail changes require verification independent of the change requester.
- An apparently urgent request must not bypass identity and authority controls.
14. Q: How should payment evidence be checked for completeness and authenticity?
A: Show answer
Evidence should establish the underlying obligation, the recipient's entitlement, the amount due, the approval, and the appropriateness of the payment. Depending on the transaction, records may include contracts, purchase orders, goods receipts, service-acceptance records, invoices, expense claims, payroll approvals, grant agreements, payment schedules, tax calculations, or beneficiary authorization. Verify that the records relate to the correct entity, payee, amount, currency, project, and period. Check signatures or electronic approvals through the organization's recognized procedure, and investigate altered documents, inconsistent dates, duplicate attachments, or suspicious instructions. A file being present in a folder does not establish its authenticity. Preserve the evidence reviewed, the review outcome, and the basis for resolving any material discrepancy.

Study instruction:
- "Select representative payments and trace each one from original obligation through acceptance evidence, invoice or claim, approval, and payment instruction."

Key point:
- Evidence must substantiate both the transaction and the recipient's entitlement.
- Completeness checks alone do not establish authenticity or validity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
15. Q: How does three-way matching reduce the risk of paying an invalid supplier invoice?
A: Show answer
For purchases governed by a purchase order, three-way matching compares the approved purchase order, evidence of goods receipt or service acceptance, and the supplier invoice. The reviewer checks quantities, description, price, terms, tax treatment where relevant, and any authorized changes. Differences outside permitted tolerances should be investigated before payment. An invoice that matches the purchase order but lacks proof of delivery may still be unsupported. Likewise, a valid delivery record does not justify paying an invoice for an amount exceeding the approved obligation. Organizations without an integrated purchasing system can apply the same principle through a documented manual checklist, provided the comparison is independent and evidence is retained. Non-PO payments require an appropriate alternative approval and evidence route rather than an invented purchase order.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a three-way match on one invoice and document the treatment of quantity, price, acceptance, and authorization discrepancies."

Key point:
- The purchase order, receipt or acceptance, and invoice substantiate different aspects of the obligation.
- Material mismatches should be resolved before payment authorization.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
16. Q: How should payments without a purchase order be reviewed?
A: Show answer
A non-PO payment should follow an approved alternative route that identifies the nature of the obligation, the authority for the expenditure, the recipient, the evidence of goods or services received, the calculation, and the designated approver. Depending on the transaction, the supporting records may be a signed contract, approved staff claim, payroll instruction, tax assessment, grant agreement, or another valid source record. The absence of a purchase order must not be used to bypass approval or evidence requirements. Conversely, staff should not invent a purchase order simply to force a transaction through a workflow designed for procurement. The procedure should specify the permitted non-PO categories, required approvals, supporting documents, and circumstances that require escalation.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a PO-backed invoice with a non-PO claim and identify the alternative evidence and approval requirements for the latter."

Key point:
- Non-PO payments require a controlled alternative authorization route.
- Lack of a purchase order does not remove the need to substantiate the obligation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
17. Q: How should duplicate invoices and repeated payment instructions be detected?
A: Show answer
Duplicate detection should compare several attributes, including legal entity, payee identifier, invoice number, invoice date, amount, currency, purchase-order reference, source-document identifier, and payment history. Exact duplicates should normally be blocked for investigation, while possible matches should be flagged for independent review. A repeated invoice number is not conclusive when different suppliers use similar numbering, and the same obligation may appear in several systems under different references. Screening should therefore distinguish exact duplicates from potential duplicates and legitimate installments. Check whether a prior payment is pending, rejected, returned, cancelled, or confirmed as settled before permitting another instruction. Retain the screening result, reviewer decision, and reason for resolving each candidate.

Study instruction:
- "Test exact duplicates, similar invoice numbers from different suppliers, valid installment payments, and items with unresolved earlier bank status."

Key point:
- Duplicate screening requires multiple attributes and payment-history checks.
- Potential matches must be resolved before they are paid again.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
18. Q: How should the payable amount, deductions, withholding, and net transfer be recalculated?
A: Show answer
The payment calculation should begin with the approved gross obligation and separately identify authorized deductions, withholding taxes, credits, advances applied, and other valid adjustments. Calculate the net transfer using the approved transaction logic and reconcile the result to the supporting invoice or claim. For example, a hypothetical gross amount of IDR 42,000,000 less an authorized deduction of IDR 1,500,000 and withholding of IDR 1,500,000 produces a net payment of IDR 39,000,000. The example is arithmetic only; actual tax treatment and deductions must be verified under applicable law, contract terms, and policy. Independently recompute material values and investigate differences before approval. Do not allow a macro or spreadsheet formula to establish a tax rate or deduction that has not been properly authorized.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate a sample payment from gross obligation to net transfer and independently verify every deduction, tax amount, and adjustment."

Key point:
- Gross obligation, deductions, withholding, and net payment must remain separately traceable.
- Tax and adjustment rules require appropriate authority; arithmetic alone does not establish validity.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
19. Q: How should foreign-currency payments be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain the original transaction currency, approved payment currency, foreign-currency amount, applicable exchange-rate basis, rate source, relevant date, resulting local-currency amount, and any realized or unrealized differences required by the applicable accounting policy. Check whether the bank account supports the intended currency and whether conversion or intermediary-bank arrangements are authorized. Keep transaction conversion separate from fees and other deductions so that the final amount can be explained. The correct exchange-rate source and timing depend on the transaction, accounting framework, and any applicable local requirements. If the rate or currency amount changes after approval, assess the effect on the approved value and determine whether recalculation and renewed authorization are required.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a foreign-currency payment from the original obligation through the approved rate, bank instruction, local-currency accounting value, and settlement evidence."

Key point:
- Currency, rate, date, fees, and accounting value must be independently traceable.
- A rate used for bank execution must not automatically be assumed appropriate for every accounting purpose.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and financial liabilities
20. Q: How should bank charges and other payment fees be treated?
A: Show answer
Distinguish the amount owed to the beneficiary from bank charges, intermediary fees, foreign-exchange costs, and any deductions that are separately authorized. The payment instruction should make clear who bears the charges under the applicable agreement and payment arrangement. Record the gross obligation, beneficiary transfer, fees, and any resulting balance separately enough to support accounting and reconciliation. For international transactions, investigate unexpected deductions or differences between the instructed and credited amounts. Do not reduce a supplier's payable merely because the bank transfers less than expected unless the treatment is supported by evidence and accounting policy. The actual chart of accounts and accounting treatment should be confirmed against the organization's approved framework and procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one payment involving bank fees and compare the approved obligation, instructed amount, bank debit, and beneficiary credit."

Key point:
- Bank charges should not be confused with reductions in the underlying payable.
- Fee allocation and accounting treatment need documented support.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
21. Q: How should payment batches be constructed and controlled?
A: Show answer
Group payments only when the items are compatible with the permitted payer entity, bank account, currency, payment method, execution date, and bank-file requirements. Assign a unique batch identifier and version, then maintain a manifest listing included transactions, payees, amounts, deductions, currency, and source references. Independently recalculate item totals and batch control totals before approval. Identify excluded, held, returned, or otherwise non-routine items so that they do not silently enter the releasable population. The batch should be built from a controlled source population and exported through an approved process. If items are added, removed, or altered after review, determine which controls must be repeated and obtain authorization for the new version before submission.

Study instruction:
- "Build a sample batch manifest and reconcile the included payment IDs, item totals, currency, and batch total to the source population."

Key point:
- Batch controls establish which specific transactions were approved for execution.
- A balanced total does not prove the individual payments are valid.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
22. Q: How can file integrity and version control prevent an unauthorized payment batch from being submitted?
A: Show answer
The approved batch must be tied to a specific, identifiable instruction file and a defined transaction population. Record the file name, version, creation date, batch reference, item count, control totals, currency, preparer, reviewer, approval status, and submission reference. Where supported, calculate a cryptographic hash from the exact exported file bytes and retain it in the manifest. The hash can help demonstrate whether the file changed, but it does not prove the payment contents are legitimate or that the file was executed by the bank. If any material field changes after approval, invalidate the affected approval and repeat the required review. The submitted file must match the approved version and the actual bank instruction.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a reviewed payment export with a modified copy and verify that the version, control totals, and file-integrity checks identify the change."

Key point:
- Approval must be linked to the exact transaction population and file version.
- File-integrity evidence supplements rather than replaces substantive review.
23. Q: What should an independent reviewer verify before approving a payment batch?
A: Show answer
The independent reviewer should examine source authorization, supporting evidence, payee identity, bank-detail verification, duplicate screening, approved amounts, deductions, withholding, currency, funding conditions, payment method, and delegated authority. Recalculate or independently test material figures, review unusual items, and confirm that exceptions have either been resolved or appropriately excluded. The review should compare the actual batch with the manifest and establish that all required gates have been completed for that version. Record the reviewer, date, evidence examined, questions raised, corrections made, and final decision. An approval statement without evidence of what was reviewed is weak support, particularly when the batch subsequently changes.

Study instruction:
- "Use an independent-review checklist on a mixed batch and record which items are approved, held, excluded, or referred for specialist review."

Key point:
- Review must test transaction validity, calculations, evidence, and batch integrity.
- The reviewer must approve the actual batch version rather than an earlier draft.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
24. Q: How should delegated authority limits be applied to payment approval?
A: Show answer
Each approval must be checked against the current delegation of authority, including authorized person, transaction category, legal entity, currency, amount limit, effective dates, and any required joint approval. An approver may be authorized for routine expenses but not for a large commitment, restricted grant disbursement, unusual international payment, or payment outside a specified budget. Splitting a transaction to fall below an approval limit must not be permitted. Verify that the approver's actual system rights and bank permissions are consistent with the approved delegation, while recognizing that internal delegation and bank signing authority are separate. If authority is expired, missing, revoked, or outside scope, block the payment until an appropriate authorized decision is documented.

Study instruction:
- "Test payments immediately below and above a hypothetical approval threshold and include an expired delegation and a joint-signature requirement."

Key point:
- Approval authority must be current and specific to the transaction.
- Threshold splitting and unsupported substitutions undermine delegated controls.
25. Q: When should a payment be referred for donor, tax, legal, compliance, or safeguarding review?
A: Show answer
Specialist review is needed when a payment triggers a requirement that cannot be resolved solely through ordinary payment checks. Examples include donor eligibility restrictions, partner disbursement conditions, uncertain withholding tax, cross-border legal questions, sanctions or other applicable screening concerns, conflicts of interest, safeguarding issues, and unusual payment structures. The reviewer should identify the specific requirement, obtain reliable evidence, document the conclusion, and confirm any necessary approval before the payment proceeds. Not every control applies to every payment; applicability must be determined from the actual facts, jurisdiction, donor conditions, and organization policy. An unresolved mandatory gate should create a hold and escalation rather than be treated as complete because an approval deadline is approaching.

Study instruction:
- "Select several payments with different specialist triggers and determine which reviews are applicable, what evidence is needed, and who may clear each hold."

Key point:
- Specialist review must be risk-based and tied to applicable requirements.
- Unresolved mandatory conditions must block release.
26. Q: How should donor-funded payments be checked against grant and partner agreements?
A: Show answer
For donor- or partner-funded payments, confirm that the recipient, payment purpose, amount, currency, milestone, and expenditure category are consistent with the executed agreement and any approved amendment. Check eligibility conditions, reporting requirements, supporting evidence, prior approvals, and restrictions on advances or onward transfers. Distinguish a payment that is authorized under the grant from one that is merely within the overall budget. A program manager's request should not replace evidence required under the agreement. Where the agreement is unclear, refer the issue to grants management or another authorized specialist and document the resulting decision. Record the source agreement version used so that future reviewers can reconstruct the basis for release.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a proposed partner disbursement to the executed agreement, approved budget, milestone evidence, payment authorization, and required post-payment reporting."

Key point:
- Donor eligibility and payment authority must be verified separately.
- Agreement-specific conditions can restrict timing, purpose, amount, or recipient.
27. Q: How should tax and statutory payments be controlled before bank execution?
A: Show answer
A tax payment should be linked to a verified taxpayer, payment category, tax period, approved calculation, applicable filing or assessment, correct payment reference, and valid authorization. Where withholding is involved, separately track the amount owed to the payee and the amount payable to the tax authority. Compare the approved tax amount with the payment instruction and ensure any required filing or calculation review has been completed. The responsible tax owner must verify current legal requirements and deadlines; the payment-processing procedure should not invent tax rates or determine tax liability from an account label alone. Store the payment evidence and reconcile the resulting liability according to the applicable accounting procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Choose a statutory payment and trace its calculation, tax period, approval, payment reference, bank evidence, and accounting entry."

Key point:
- The tax obligation and payment instruction must agree with verified source evidence.
- Tax classification and rates require current authoritative support.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
28. Q: Why must bank authorization remain separate from internal payment approval?
A: Show answer
Internal approval establishes that an expenditure or payment is permitted under organizational policy. Bank authorization establishes that the submitted instruction was approved through the actual account mandate and permitted execution channel. A bank mandate may require two signatories, defined maker/checker permissions, amount limits, or restrictions by account and currency. Internal approval does not override those requirements. Conversely, an authorized bank signer should not execute a payment that has not passed the organization's internal controls. The procedure must identify the valid account, permitted users, authorization sequence, applicable limits, and evidence of bank acceptance or execution. A mismatch between internal approval and bank authorization should stop release until resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an internally approved batch with the applicable bank mandate and identify any missing signer, unauthorized user, or limit violation."

Key point:
- Internal approval and bank execution authority are independent control requirements.
- Actual mandates and user permissions must be current and verified.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
29. Q: What controls should apply to urgent, manual, offline, or emergency payments?
A: Show answer
Define which circumstances justify the exceptional route, which role may authorize it, what alternative evidence is acceptable, and which normal controls remain mandatory. Urgency should accelerate escalation and review rather than authorize informal account substitutions, unverified recipients, missing authority, or self-approval. If an approved emergency method is used, document the reason, supporting evidence, independent authorization, bank execution, communication, and subsequent review. Where the bank channel is unavailable, follow verified bank and organizational instructions rather than improvising a new transfer route. A retrospective review should verify that the transaction was legitimate and identify any process weakness requiring corrective action. The procedure must distinguish permissible emergency processing from an attempt to evade routine controls.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a time-critical payment and identify which controls can be adapted, which remain mandatory, and what evidence must be retained afterward."

Key point:
- Urgency does not waive essential authorization, payee, or bank-mandate controls.
- Exceptions must be justified, approved, documented, and reviewed.
30. Q: How should payment instructions be submitted and tracked through the bank?
A: Show answer
Submit the approved instruction through the organization's authorized bank channel, ensuring the submitted file or transaction details match the approved version. Record the submitting user, date and time, bank reference, batch identifier, control totals, and acknowledgment. Monitor the bank-provided status according to the transaction type and relevant service process. A submission acknowledgment may establish receipt by the bank system but does not necessarily prove successful execution or beneficiary credit. If the bank reports pending, rejected, failed, or ambiguous status, retain the original response and route the item to the status-resolution procedure. Never assume that the absence of an error message means the payment is complete.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a batch from submission through bank acknowledgment and compare the submitted version with the approved manifest and bank response."

Key point:
- Submission, acceptance, execution, and settlement are distinct status points.
- Each status must be supported by appropriate bank evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
31. Q: What evidence is sufficient to classify an outgoing payment as settled?
A: Show answer
Settlement should be established using reliable evidence appropriate to the bank channel and transaction type, such as a confirmed bank transaction record, a relevant bank statement entry, or other official execution evidence. The evidence must identify the transaction or provide a reliable link to the payment reference, amount, currency, date, account, and beneficiary where available. A prepared instruction, internal approval, or submission acknowledgment is not necessarily proof of settlement. If the bank provides a technical status code, document how that code maps to the organization's controlled status definition and retain the raw response. If evidence is incomplete or contradictory, classify the payment as unresolved and investigate it rather than prematurely marking it as settled.

Study instruction:
- "Compare proof of payment, bank acknowledgment, and bank-statement evidence and determine what each record actually proves."

Key point:
- A settled status must be based on sufficient transaction-specific evidence.
- Technical status labels require validated interpretation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
32. Q: How should pending, unknown, or contradictory bank statuses be handled?
A: Show answer
Keep the transaction in an unresolved status until the relevant bank evidence establishes the outcome. Record the original submission, time of inquiry, bank response, affected account and amount, responsible investigator, and next action. Contact the bank through a verified channel and follow the approved status-inquiry procedure. Do not create a replacement payment merely because the original instruction has not produced a clear confirmation. The original may still be executing, which creates a risk of duplicate disbursement. Once the status is confirmed, update the register and determine whether the original instruction can be completed, cancelled, or replaced under the applicable process. Retain the evidence and approval supporting the final decision.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a transaction with unknown bank status and demonstrate how to investigate it without prematurely issuing a replacement payment."

Key point:
- Unknown status is an exception, not evidence of failure or settlement.
- Replacement instructions must wait until the original transaction's state is sufficiently established.
33. Q: How should rejected, failed, or returned payments be processed?
A: Show answer
Record the original payment identifier, bank reference, amount, currency, status, reason supplied by the bank, date identified, and required corrective action. Investigate whether the issue arises from incorrect bank details, account restrictions, technical failure, compliance review, insufficient funds, or another cause. Verify the actual reason before changing master data or creating another instruction. Determine whether the original payable remains outstanding, the amount was returned, or only part of the transfer completed. Correct the relevant record through the controlled workflow, obtain any required renewed approval, and link the new attempt to the original payment. A returned or failed transfer must not silently disappear from the payment register or accounting records.

Study instruction:
- "Review a failed transfer and trace the investigation, correction, revised authorization, bank outcome, and accounting consequences."

Key point:
- A failed or returned payment needs a documented resolution and transaction linkage.
- Do not reissue funds until duplicate-execution risk has been addressed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
34. Q: What controls are required before retrying a payment or creating a replacement instruction?
A: Show answer
First determine the terminal outcome of the original instruction using sufficient bank evidence. Verify whether any money was transferred, returned, reversed, or remains in process. Link the replacement to the original payment and record the failure reason, corrective action, changed payee or bank details if any, revised amount, and required approvals. Run duplicate screening again and revalidate any material change to the transaction. If the original outcome cannot be confirmed, keep the replacement on hold and escalate the investigation. The procedure should prevent a retry from being treated as an unrelated new payment, because this can hide duplicate obligations or cause two transfers for the same liability.

Study instruction:
- "Create a payment-retry decision tree that distinguishes confirmed failure, confirmed return, pending status, and confirmed settlement."

Key point:
- A retry requires established status, linkage to the original, and renewed controls as needed.
- An unresolved original payment must not trigger an automatic replacement.
35. Q: How should payment cancellations, stop instructions, and recall requests be controlled?
A: Show answer
Record the original payment reference, the reason for cancellation or recall, the authority requesting it, the time of the request, and the bank channel used. Verify whether the transaction was still pending or had already executed before deciding what action is possible. A recall request is not proof that a payment has been recalled successfully, and a stop instruction may be subject to bank-specific conditions. Retain the bank's confirmation or final response and track any amount returned or still outstanding. The accounting and payable status must reflect the evidence and applicable policy rather than the intention to stop a payment. Do not close the case or issue replacement funds until the original transaction's outcome and the remaining liability are established.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a recall request from internal authorization through the bank's final response and verify that the payment is not closed on the basis of the request alone."

Key point:
- A cancellation or recall request is not the same as a confirmed outcome.
- Outstanding obligations and returned funds must remain traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
36. Q: How should completed bank payments update the accounting or source system?
A: Show answer
Update the source system only when the relevant status and accounting evidence are sufficient under the organization's approved process. Link the source obligation, payable or advance, payment instruction, bank reference, amount, currency, date, fees, and resulting status. Where a bank response or statement is imported automatically, validate the source data, transaction mapping, duplicate handling, and status interpretation before relying on the update. A technical import failure must create a visible exception rather than silently leaving the payable or cash record inaccurate. Likewise, a successful update must not imply that formal bank reconciliation has already occurred unless that separate control was actually completed. Keep evidence of the user or system process, time, and result of the update.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one completed payment from the source-system record through the payment document, bank evidence, and resulting accounting status."

Key point:
- Source-system status must reflect evidence, not an assumed result.
- Payment processing and formal reconciliation should remain distinguishable controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
37. Q: What is the difference between payment reconciliation hand-off and formal bank reconciliation?
A: Show answer
A payment-processing hand-off confirms that the relevant payment evidence, references, accounting updates, and unresolved exceptions have been provided to the designated reconciliation owner. Formal bank reconciliation is a distinct control that compares bank-statement activity with accounting records, identifies unmatched transactions, and resolves timing differences or errors. Handing over a payment record does not prove the bank statement has been reconciled, and a completed payment instruction does not establish that every related accounting record is correct. Define the reconciliation owner's responsibilities, hand-off fields, acceptance criteria, supporting documents, and exception escalation. The process should preserve clear ownership so that outstanding items do not fall between payment processing and period-end reconciliation.

Study instruction:
- "Follow a payment through the hand-off record and confirm which checks belong to the payment processor and which remain with the independent reconciliation owner."

Key point:
- Payment completion and formal bank reconciliation are separate milestones.
- Handoffs require clear ownership, evidence, and unresolved-item tracking.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
38. Q: How does the accounts payable document flow support accurate financial records?
A: Show answer
The accounting process should preserve the natural relationship among the document that recognizes an obligation, the payment document, the bank-statement evidence, and any subsequent clearing or reconciliation records. In the UN IPSAS Finance Manual's Umoja environment, accounts payable postings integrate with the general ledger through controlled reconciliation accounts, and payment processing links payable balances to payment records. An NGO should apply its own chart of accounts and approved accounting system configuration rather than copying Umoja account codes or transaction codes. The key control principle is that each stage must be traceable and consistent with the applicable accounting policy. Manual journal entries or system overrides should be supported, independently reviewed, and reconciled to the subsidiary records.

Study instruction:
- "Draw the accounting-document flow for an invoice and subsequent payment, identifying which records create, settle, or reconcile the liability."

Key point:
- Payment records should reconcile to the underlying payable and general ledger.
- System-specific accounting instructions must be adapted to the organization's approved framework.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
39. Q: How should an accrued liability be distinguished from a payment already executed?
A: Show answer
An accrued liability represents an obligation recognized under the applicable accounting framework for goods or services received but not yet paid, while payment execution records the settlement of an obligation through an authorized disbursement. The two events may occur in different reporting periods. Payment processing should not be used to delay or replace the recognition of liabilities where the accounting criteria have already been met. Conversely, an invoice or accrual entry does not prove that a bank transfer occurred. The finance team should maintain a clear link between the source obligation, payable or accrual, payment document, bank evidence, and clearing record. Recognition and settlement timing should follow the applicable accounting policy and transaction facts.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a month-end unpaid invoice with an invoice paid the following month and identify the liability-recognition and settlement evidence for each period."

Key point:
- Liability recognition and cash settlement represent different accounting events.
- A payment instruction does not replace the evidence needed to recognize or clear an obligation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and accrued expenses
40. Q: How should supplier advances and down-payment requests be controlled?
A: Show answer
An advance should be linked to an authorized contractual or operational purpose, approved recipient, amount, due date, payment terms, and subsequent evidence requirements. Track the outstanding balance until it is applied against an eligible invoice, supported by an approved settlement, or recovered. Payment of an advance must not automatically be treated as evidence that the related goods or services have been received or that the amount qualifies as an expense. Where an integrated accounting system uses a payment block or separate down-payment workflow, verify that the actual organization has approved and configured the relevant control before relying on it. Review aged advances, incomplete delivery evidence, unapplied amounts, and recoveries as part of the close process.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a supplier advance from request and approval through disbursement, delivery, application against an invoice, and clearance of the remaining balance."

Key point:
- Advances require separate authorization and subsequent settlement evidence.
- An advance is not automatically an expense or proof of completed delivery.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and down-payment procedures
41. Q: How should supplier credit notes, overpayments, and recoverable balances be handled?
A: Show answer
Verify the reason for the credit, overpayment, or recovery and link it to the original supplier transaction. A valid credit note may reduce the payable or affect a subsequent payment, while an overpayment may require a refund or approved offset. The finance team should not silently net a disputed credit against a payment without an authorized and supported accounting basis. Record the original invoice, credit or adjustment document, amount, supplier confirmation where relevant, approval, and settlement or recovery status. Investigate old balances and establish who is responsible for obtaining refunds or resolving disputes. Any correction to the accounting record must preserve the original transaction history and show the basis for the change.

Study instruction:
- "Review an overpayment and a supplier credit note, then trace how each affects the outstanding payable, subsequent payment, and recovery record."

Key point:
- Credits and recoveries must be supported and linked to the original transaction.
- Unresolved balances require an owner and documented follow-up.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and credit memos
42. Q: What should be included in a centralized payment-exception register?
A: Show answer
The register should capture a unique case identifier, payment or batch reference, payee, entity, amount and currency, exception type, date identified, risk level, owner, due date, status, actions taken, escalation history, evidence references, and closure approval. Exceptions may include missing documents, invalid bank details, duplicate candidates, calculation differences, expired delegation, failed payments, unresolved bank status, returned funds, incomplete system updates, or suspicious activity. Define the criteria for opening, escalating, and closing each category. A transaction should not be marked resolved merely because it has been carried forward to the next month or assigned to another employee. Closure should require evidence of the actual outcome and any necessary accounting correction or preventive action.

Study instruction:
- "Design an exception register and verify that every case has an owner, deadline, risk classification, evidence trail, and documented closure criteria."

Key point:
- Exception registers make unresolved risk visible and actionable.
- Closure requires evidence and an authorized decision, not a status change alone.
43. Q: How should suspected payment fraud or social engineering be handled?
A: Show answer
Stop the affected instruction or batch where safely possible, restrict further unauthorized changes, preserve original emails and documents, record bank and system references, and notify the designated incident-response and management contacts through verified channels. Do not continue an unverified bank-detail change merely to meet a deadline. If a transaction may already have executed, contact the bank through an independently verified channel and follow the organization's incident and legal-reporting requirements. Preserve timestamps, user actions, approvals, system logs, and communication records. Limit access to sensitive evidence and avoid altering originals. The investigation should determine the incident scope, affected payments, control weaknesses, and required corrective actions before the case is formally closed.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a fraudulent bank-detail-change request and document the containment, evidence-preservation, escalation, bank-contact, and corrective-action steps."

Key point:
- Suspected fraud requires immediate containment and preservation of evidence.
- Investigation and recovery must follow approved incident procedures and applicable law.
44. Q: How should payment records be retained while protecting personal and banking information?
A: Show answer
Retain the records needed to establish the source obligation, payee verification, calculation, approvals, bank authorization, execution status, accounting update, reconciliation hand-off, and exception resolution. Organize the archive by financial year, period, entity, payment type, and unique reference, with controlled naming and version conventions. Restrict access according to role and need, particularly for payroll, beneficiary, identity, and bank information. Encryption, secure transfer, access logging, backup, and recovery arrangements should follow the organization's approved information-security and privacy requirements. Retention periods and legal holds must be verified against applicable law, donor or contract requirements, and internal policy before records are deleted. A backup should be tested for restoration rather than assumed effective because a copy exists.

Study instruction:
- "Retrieve a sample payment file from the archive and verify access restrictions, version history, supporting evidence, retention rules, and recovery arrangements."

Key point:
- An effective archive preserves traceability while restricting sensitive information.
- Retention and disposal must follow verified obligations and applicable holds.
45. Q: How should management monitor whether payment-processing controls are effective?
A: Show answer
Management reporting should show transaction volumes, total disbursements, processing time, overdue requests, duplicate candidates, failed and returned payments, unresolved bank statuses, payment exceptions by age and risk, incomplete evidence, rejected approvals, and outstanding source-system updates. Each indicator needs a defined calculation, source, reporting frequency, owner, target, and escalation threshold. Interpret results alongside risk and payment type rather than relying exclusively on aggregate totals. For example, a low number of exceptions may reflect weak detection rather than strong controls. Test automated dashboard results against transaction records and bank evidence. Management should review recurring trends, assign corrective actions, and monitor whether those actions reduce the underlying causes of error or delay.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a monthly payment-control dashboard and reconcile a sample of its indicators to the underlying payment and exception registers."

Key point:
- Useful KPIs have consistent definitions, reliable source data, and clear ownership.
- Management review should drive evidence-based corrective action.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger reporting
46. Q: How should bank users, signing mandates, and delegated permissions be reviewed periodically?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled roster of bank users, signatories, roles, permitted accounts and currencies, transaction limits, effective dates, expiry dates, and authorizing records. Compare the roster against the actual bank mandate and the organization's current delegation matrix. Remove access promptly when employees leave, change roles, or no longer require it, and retain evidence of revocation. Independently test whether maker/checker and signatory combinations work as approved and whether unauthorized or shared credentials exist. Record the review date, reviewer, differences, remediation owner, and retest outcome. Access review should also consider external advisers and temporary users. An unverified user, stale mandate, or unresolved permission mismatch should create an appropriate restriction until the issue is corrected.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a sample access recertification and compare current permissions with the bank mandate, staff roster, delegation, and recorded revocations."

Key point:
- Bank access must match current authorized responsibilities.
- Periodic review must result in documented remediation and independent verification.
47. Q: How should Excel, Power Query, or VBA support payment controls without replacing human authorization?
A: Show answer
Excel can provide standardized intake forms, controlled tables, validation lists, calculation checks, batch manifests, exception registers, and management reports. Power Query can combine approved source files, normalize fields, identify potential duplicates, and reconcile record counts or control totals. VBA may generate reports, create controlled exports, maintain workflow logs, and enforce predefined procedural checks. The rules must be designed around the organization's approved procedures and tested before deployment. Software should not invent missing authority, automatically decide a complex legal or donor question, or mark an unknown bank status as settled. Use independent test cases, controlled configuration, change approval, access restrictions, error handling, audit logging, and a recoverable source-data process. All material output should remain traceable to source records and review decisions.

Study instruction:
- "Prototype an automated payment checklist and test missing evidence, duplicate invoices, changed bank details, invalid approval limits, and unresolved execution status."

Key point:
- Automation supports consistent controls but does not establish legal or accounting validity.
- Rules and outputs require testing, authorization, and traceability.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and journal workflows
48. Q: How should workflow automation represent payment states and enforce mandatory gates?
A: Show answer
Define controlled states such as received, incomplete, under review, on hold, approved, ready for bank submission, submitted, pending confirmation, settled, rejected, returned, cancelled, and closed. Each state transition should specify the authorized actor, required evidence, conditions, and permitted next states. For example, a payment must not move from pending to settled merely because a service-level deadline has passed. A hold should remain active while a mandatory payee, authority, funding, or bank-status requirement is unresolved. Workflow design should also prevent transitions that refer to nonexistent stages or bypass required controls. Test both valid paths and deliberately invalid sequences, then preserve the ruleset version and the results of the tests.

Study instruction:
- "Draw a payment-state diagram and test that no successful route can bypass required evidence, independent approval, bank authorization, or final-status verification."

Key point:
- Controlled workflow states make decisions and restrictions explicit.
- Timeouts and automation cannot substitute for missing evidence or authorization.
49. Q: How should payment-processing changes, training, and business continuity be governed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a change register covering revised procedures, bank integrations, approval limits, master-data rules, workflow logic, user permissions, and report calculations. Each change should include its reason, owner, affected controls, risk assessment, required testing, independent review, approval, effective date, and communication plan. Test material changes using representative scenarios, including rejected payments, duplicate candidates, bank-detail changes, and uncertain execution status. Train affected staff before the change takes effect and retain the training evidence. Establish deputies and recovery arrangements for unavailable personnel, systems, and bank channels. Preserve prior approved procedure versions so the organization can explain which control rules applied to a historical payment and why a process changed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one hypothetical procedure change from request and risk assessment through testing, approval, training, implementation, and post-implementation review."

Key point:
- Payment-control changes require controlled approval and testing.
- Training, deputies, and recoverable records support operational continuity.
50. Q: How should payment controls be tested before a procedure or automated system is approved for use?
A: Show answer
Test the complete payment lifecycle with representative transactions and negative test cases. Examples include missing source approvals, unverified bank details, duplicate invoices, amount discrepancies, expired delegation, unauthorized signatories, mixed-currency batches, altered exports, unresolved bank statuses, failed source updates, and exceptions closed without evidence. Specify the expected outcome for each case, such as proceed, block, hold, refer, or escalate, based on the approved policy. Independently recompute calculation examples and compare workflow results with expected outcomes. Validate document integrity and review all pages of the final procedure for missing content or unreadable tables. Technical tests, application compatibility, and operational approval should be recorded separately; a successful document-generation check does not establish that the procedure is legally applicable or approved by the organization.

Study instruction:
- "Create a negative-test inventory with expected outcomes, execution evidence, failed-test remediation, and independent retest results."

Key point:
- Testing must demonstrate both correct processing and effective blocking of unsafe transactions.
- Technical validation and organizational adoption are separate acceptance decisions.
