Table 3
Reimbursement Claim Controls and Accounting Workflow
1. Q: What is the purpose of an NGO reimbursement claim SOP, and what controls should it establish from submission to closure?
A: Show answer
A reimbursement claim SOP establishes the controlled process for receiving, validating, reviewing, approving, accounting for, handing off, reconciling, and closing expenses personally paid by an eligible claimant on behalf of the organization. It should protect organizational funds, support timely reimbursement, establish consistent eligibility decisions, prevent duplicate payments, preserve evidence, and make every decision traceable. The process should distinguish claim approval from payment execution and actual payment confirmation. A submitted receipt, available budget, managerial instruction, or balanced journal is not, by itself, proof that the claim is eligible or paid. Before adoption, the organization must validate its legal entity, applicable accounting framework, internal policies, employment or contractual arrangements, donor agreements, tax requirements, delegated authorities, and system capabilities. The supplied SOP is a fictional demonstration rather than adopted policy or evidence of a real payment. Its structure must therefore be treated as a design reference requiring authorized review and configuration.

Study instruction:
- "Map the complete reimbursement lifecycle and identify the evidence required before each stage can be marked complete."
Key point:
- A reimbursement SOP governs the claim lifecycle, not merely the transfer of cash.
- Fictional examples must not be treated as adopted policy or proof of an actual transaction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
2. Q: How should an NGO determine which rules govern a reimbursement claim when internal policies, donor agreements, contracts, and legal requirements overlap?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying mandatory legal requirements and other applicable external obligations. Then examine executed employment or contractor terms, donor agreements and amendments, approved expense policies, delegation instruments, and the source-process controls relevant to the transaction. Each source should be recorded with its owner, version, effective date, applicability, and relevant provision. A policy cannot waive a mandatory legal requirement, and a manager cannot approve expenditure that falls outside the manager's delegated authority. If two sources conflict or their applicability is uncertain, record the conflict, hold the affected decision, and refer it to the competent legal, tax, grants, human resources, or accounting reviewer. Maintain a source register that distinguishes a source's existence from proof that it applies to the particular organization or transaction. Deadlines, expense caps, required documents, tax rules, and exchange-rate methods must be activated only after verification and formal approval where required.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one reimbursement rule through the applicable law, donor agreement, internal policy, approval authority, and evidence of applicability."
Key point:
- The existence of a rule does not establish its applicability to every claim.
- Unresolved legal, contractual, or policy conflicts must be escalated rather than silently overridden.
3. Q: How should reimbursement claims be distinguished from supplier invoices, travel claims, advances, corporate-card transactions, payroll benefits, and partner expenditure?
A: Show answer
Classification should follow the substance of the transaction and the organization's approved process boundaries. A reimbursement generally concerns an authorized claimant seeking repayment for an eligible expense personally paid on behalf of the organization. A supplier invoice belongs to accounts payable and procurement controls; a travel claim may require a dedicated travel and advance-liquidation process; an outstanding advance is accounted for and settled under the applicable advance procedure; and a corporate-card transaction requires card-statement reconciliation and verification of the underlying expenditure. Payroll benefits, petty cash, partner expenditure, and project advances may have their own authorization and supporting-documentation requirements. These categories must not be forced into reimbursement merely because the same employee or amount is involved. An out-of-scope transaction should receive a referral record identifying the correct destination, reason for referral, responsible owner, outstanding information, and status. The referral remains open until the receiving process has acknowledged it and the necessary information has been returned or the handoff has otherwise been resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample supplier invoices, staff-paid purchases, travel settlements, corporate-card expenses, and advances by source process."
Key point:
- Similar cash flows can belong to different accounting and authorization processes.
- Referral to another process must not bypass the controls of that process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses and travel-related processes
4. Q: What information should a standard reimbursement submission contain before the claim enters formal review?
A: Show answer
The submission should identify a unique claim ID, version ID, claimant reference, claimant category, legal entity, office, business purpose, expenditure date, submission date, transaction currency, proposed payment currency, project reference where relevant, and the applicable preapproval reference. It should include item-level line IDs, expense categories, gross amounts, credits or refunds, calculated net amounts, receipt references, evidence of payment, and the claimant's required declarations concerning business purpose, conflicts, or other reimbursement requests. Unknown information should be marked for resolution rather than invented. Required fields should be defined in an approved submission specification and should distinguish mandatory from conditional data. The system should freeze or preserve the submitted version and record its timestamp, attachments, and claimant declaration. Validation should identify missing information before a claim progresses to eligibility review. Where an omitted field could affect payment, tax, donor eligibility, or accounting classification, the affected decision remains on hold until the issue is resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Build a submission checklist and test it against claims with missing identities, dates, purposes, currency, and line-item evidence."
Key point:
- Complete structured submissions reduce rework and preserve traceability.
- Required information must be validated before the claim progresses.
5. Q: What supporting evidence is needed to establish that an expense was genuinely incurred and personally paid by the claimant?
A: Show answer
Evidence normally starts with a receipt or invoice identifying the merchant, date, items or services, amount, and currency, together with proof of payment where the approved policy requires it. Depending on the expense, the file may also need a contract, purchase authorization, travel authorization, delivery record, attendance record, event documentation, or another source record demonstrating the organizational purpose. The evidence must support both the existence of the expenditure and the claimant's assertion that reimbursement is due. A card statement can show a payment but may not identify the business purpose or establish that the claimant has not already been reimbursed. Conversely, a receipt may establish a purchase but not prove settlement of the amount. The reviewer should compare these records, identify inconsistencies, and document any alternative evidence permitted by policy. Missing mandatory evidence should result in a request for information or an appropriate hold, not an unsupported assumption that the transaction is valid.

Study instruction:
- "For a sample claim, identify which documents prove occurrence, business purpose, personal payment, amount, and authorization."
Key point:
- Receipts and payment evidence establish different facts and may both be necessary.
- Missing evidence must be handled under an approved exception procedure.
6. Q: How should reviewers verify receipts and investigate inconsistent or potentially altered evidence?
A: Show answer
Review the document's date, merchant, amount, currency, description, payment method, transaction reference, and relationship to the claimed activity. Compare the receipt with proof of payment and other available records, such as supplier confirmation or an original digital receipt. Check for inconsistent totals, repeated metadata, implausible date sequences, altered-looking fields, duplicate attachments, or unexplained discrepancies. These indicators justify investigation but do not automatically prove fraud. Preserve the original evidence, record the concern and the source of the discrepancy, restrict unnecessary access, and assign an independent reviewer. Where an integrity concern is credible, place the affected claim or line on hold and refer it through the organization's approved investigation process. Do not overwrite the original record or close the case solely because the claimant provides an unsupported verbal explanation. Any conclusion should be based on evidence, documented authority, and an appropriately independent review.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a receipt, card or bank evidence, and transaction record, then document inconsistencies without prematurely concluding that fraud occurred."
Key point:
- An anomaly is a trigger for investigation, not a definitive fraud finding.
- Preserve evidence and maintain independent review of integrity concerns.
7. Q: Why should reimbursement eligibility be assessed separately for each expense line rather than treating the entire claim as one indivisible amount?
A: Show answer
One claim can contain a mixture of allowable, unsupported, restricted, duplicate, or ineligible expenses. Item-level assessment makes it possible to approve an eligible portion while keeping disputed or ineligible amounts separate. For every line, verify the claimant's authority, business purpose, evidence, personal payment, relationship to an approved activity, applicable expense policy, and any donor, tax, or coding conditions. Record the amount submitted, any supported credits, the eligibility outcome, the amount held, the amount ineligible, and the rationale. Aggregating the claim without line-level decisions can cause prohibited costs to be reimbursed simply because they appear beside valid expenditure. Equally, automatically rejecting an entire claim may be unnecessary when policy permits partial treatment. The organization must define which requirements are non-waivable and which exceptions can be considered by an authorized decision-maker. Every decision must be reproducible from the evidence and applicable rules.

Study instruction:
- "Review a mixed claim containing an eligible purchase and an ineligible item, and calculate the amount that can proceed subject to all remaining approval gates."
Key point:
- Eligibility decisions should be traceable to individual expense lines.
- Partial eligibility does not automatically authorize payment of the eligible portion.
8. Q: How should an NGO distinguish eligible, partially eligible, ineligible, and held claims in its workflow?
A: Show answer
These statuses communicate different decisions and should have formally documented entry criteria. An eligible claim has passed the applicable eligibility checks, but may still require approval and payment controls. A partially eligible claim has a supported portion that can be considered separately from amounts held or rejected. An ineligible claim has a documented basis for excluding the affected expenditure under an applicable rule. A held claim is unresolved and must not progress through the affected control gates. Reasons for a hold may include missing evidence, a duplicate alert, uncertainty about claimant authority, unresolved tax treatment, an unverified donor restriction, invalid coding, or an integrity concern. Each status should define who may assign it, which actions are blocked, what evidence is required to change it, and how the claimant is notified. Do not treat held amounts as eligible, and do not describe an eligibility decision as final approval if delegated approval remains outstanding.

Study instruction:
- "Define the entry and exit criteria for eligibility statuses and test each status against a representative claim."
Key point:
- Eligibility, approval, and payment are separate control decisions.
- A hold remains active until its underlying issue has been resolved and recorded.
9. Q: How can an organization identify duplicate claims, repeated receipts, or expenses already paid directly by the organization?
A: Show answer
Use multiple attributes to identify possible duplicates, including claimant, merchant, receipt number, transaction date, amount, currency, description, payment reference, and expense line ID. Matching should account for formatting differences, currencies, split payments, and legitimate repeated purchases. A matching receipt number alone is not conclusive because different vendors can use the same reference, and one genuine transaction can appear differently in different systems. Compare reimbursement records with accounts payable, corporate-card transactions, previous claims, travel settlements, advance liquidation, and other applicable payment records. Exact or near matches should generate an alert and an independent review rather than automatic deletion or rejection. Record whether the alert was confirmed, dismissed with evidence, or remains unresolved. Prevent payment while a material duplicate concern remains open. Keep the matching evidence and reviewer decision so the same issue is not repeatedly reopened without new information.

Study instruction:
- "Test a duplicate-detection register with exact matches, near matches, valid repeat purchases, and transactions already paid through another process."
Key point:
- Duplicate detection should combine multiple attributes and preserve legitimate transactions.
- An unresolved duplicate alert should block the affected claim from payment.
10. Q: How should an NGO handle late reimbursement claims when no approved submission deadline or exception rule has been verified?
A: Show answer
First, determine which deadline actually applies by reviewing approved organizational policy, contractual terms, applicable law, and any relevant donor requirement. A system default or date copied from a sample document is not sufficient authority. Record the expenditure date, submission date, applicable policy version, and the evidence supporting the deadline assessment. If the claim is late and the applicable policy requires an exception, hold it for review by the authorized decision-maker. The reviewer should document the reason for lateness, whether an exception is permitted, its scope, its approval, and any remaining non-waivable requirements. A late claim should not be automatically approved merely because the amount is small or the expenditure appears genuine. If no applicable deadline can be established, obtain an authorized policy or legal interpretation before enforcing or waiving one. Monitor repeated late submissions to determine whether the underlying problem is policy awareness, manager approval delay, missing documentation, or an impractical submission process.

Study instruction:
- "Assess a late submission against the actual governing policy and distinguish a valid exception from an unverified template default."
Key point:
- Deadlines and exception authority must come from verified, applicable sources.
- Approval of lateness does not waive other mandatory eligibility and evidence requirements.
11. Q: How should responsibilities be divided among the claimant, intake officer, preparer, reviewer, approver, payment operator, reconciler, and records custodian?
A: Show answer
Each role should have a defined responsibility and access scope. The claimant supplies the claim and declarations; intake checks submission completeness and routing; the preparer organizes data and calculations; the reviewer evaluates evidence, eligibility, coding, and exceptions; the delegated approver authorizes the claim within established limits; the payment operator handles the separate payment process; the reconciler compares approved amounts with actual disbursement and accounting records; and the records custodian maintains controlled evidence and retention. Specialist roles such as Tax, Grants, Human Resources, Accounting, and Integrity should be involved when their expertise or authority is required. The system should record who performed, reviewed, approved, or changed each relevant action. Staffing constraints may prevent full separation, but compensating controls such as independent post-payment review and periodic access checks must be explicitly designed. A role title alone does not prove that a person is authorized for a specific entity, amount, expense type, or exception.

Study instruction:
- "Build a responsibility matrix and identify where one person could initiate, approve, pay, and reconcile the same claim."
Key point:
- Duties must be separated according to actual risk and authority.
- Where full segregation is impractical, compensating controls must be documented and tested.
12. Q: What controls prevent self-approval, conflicted approval, or use of an approver whose delegated authority has expired?
A: Show answer
Maintain an effective-dated delegation register that identifies the approver, entity, role, approval scope, applicable limits, start and end dates, conditions, and evidence of authorization. Compare each approval against that register at the time the decision is made. The claimant must not approve their own claim, clear their own duplicate alert, or serve as the sole approver of their own exception or payment. A person requesting an exception should not be the only person to approve and close it. Where relevant, assess conflicts of interest, changes in employment or position, and restrictions on approving particular expense categories. If authority is expired, insufficient, conflicted, or unverified, block approval and route the claim to a competent independent authority. Preserve the rejected attempt and the resolution. Do not repair a failed authorization by retrospectively entering a name without verifying the approval that was actually given.

Study instruction:
- "Test approval records against an effective-dated delegation matrix, including self-approval, expired authority, and conflicts of interest."
Key point:
- Approval must be valid for the person, transaction, amount, and date.
- Unauthorized or conflicted approvals cannot clear mandatory control gates.
13. Q: Why must budget availability and donor eligibility be checked separately from general expense eligibility?
A: Show answer
An expense may be genuine and properly supported but still be prohibited by the relevant project budget, donor agreement, restricted funding conditions, or internal authorization. Budget availability demonstrates a financial capacity or allocation; it does not prove that the nature, timing, purpose, or documentation of the expense is allowable. Review the applicable project, grant, cost category, period, donor restrictions, and any required preapproval. Identify the exact agreement clause or approved internal rule that supports the conclusion. If donor eligibility or coding is uncertain, hold the affected amount and obtain the designated Grants or Finance decision-maker's review. Do not transfer an expense to another project merely because it has a remaining budget. If an allocation is necessary, document its basis, authorization, and consistency with donor terms and accounting policy. Reconcile the claim allocation to the source expense so that the same cost is not charged to multiple grants or reported twice.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a supported expense against a project budget and an executed donor agreement, and explain why the two checks produce different conclusions."
Key point:
- A valid expense is not automatically donor-allowable or budget-authorized.
- Cost allocation must have a defensible basis and prevent double charging.
14. Q: How should tax-sensitive reimbursement items be classified without assuming that every reimbursement is taxable or tax-free?
A: Show answer
Tax treatment depends on the nature of the benefit or expense, the recipient's relationship with the organization, the supporting facts, and the applicable current tax rules. A genuine business expense reimbursement should not automatically be treated the same as a personal benefit, allowance, gift, or other payment. Equally, calling a payment a reimbursement does not automatically establish tax exemption. Identify the transaction, recipient category, purpose, contractual context, amount, supporting evidence, and relevant tax rule. Refer uncertain cases to the authorized tax specialist, who should record the applicable source, analysis, conclusion, effective date, and approval where required. Keep the tax determination separate from the eligibility and accounting decisions because each addresses a different question. Do not hard-code a tax outcome into a template until the governing rule and its applicability have been verified. Where data required for assessment is missing or the treatment remains disputed, place the affected line on a tax hold.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a supported business expense with a possible personal benefit and list the facts required for a tax decision."
Key point:
- Transaction labels alone do not determine tax treatment.
- Uncertain tax classifications require competent, documented review before clearance.
15. Q: How should an NGO assign the correct chart-of-accounts code and dimensions to an approved reimbursement?
A: Show answer
The accounting classification should reflect the nature of the expense under the organization's applicable accounting framework and approved chart of accounts. Required dimensions may include legal entity, office, project, grant, donor, cost centre, natural account, currency, and tax code. A reimbursement category is not, by itself, proof that a particular account or project is correct. Verify the purpose, supporting documents, coding rules, donor conditions, and any capitalization or tax implications that may alter treatment. Use an approved mapping table with effective dates and defined exception handling. Missing or invalid mappings should block final journal preparation until corrected. Changes to a previously reviewed classification should be recorded as a new version, with an explanation of the change and renewed review where material. Reconcile all project allocations to the approved claim total and verify that credits and ineligible amounts are not accidentally included in expense postings.

Study instruction:
- "Map sample claim lines to natural accounts and organizational dimensions using an approved mapping table."
Key point:
- Coding must represent the actual transaction and applicable accounting policy.
- Invalid or changed mappings require documented review before posting.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger and journal workflows
16. Q: How should foreign-currency reimbursement claims be calculated and reviewed when the approved exchange-rate source has not been established?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original currency, original amount, supported credits, net amount, proposed payment currency, exchange-rate source, rate date or selection basis, rate value, conversion result, and applicable rounding method. Determine the appropriate method from verified organizational policy and any relevant contractual, donor, accounting, or local requirements. Do not substitute a convenient spreadsheet rate for an approved source. The rate and date used for operational reimbursement may not necessarily be identical to the exchange rate appropriate for financial-statement measurement. Reperform the calculation independently, check the direction of conversion, and test rounding for consistency. If the approved source, date, or method is unverified, hold the converted amount and route it to the accounting owner for resolution. Store the original transaction values so the calculation can be reproduced without replacing historical inputs when rates are updated.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate an illustrative foreign-currency claim and verify source, rate date, conversion, rounding, and approval."
Key point:
- A correct arithmetic result does not validate an unapproved exchange-rate method.
- Original currency data and the complete conversion basis must remain traceable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger processing
17. Q: How should the reimbursable amount be calculated when a claim contains gross expenses, credits, held amounts, and ineligible items?
A: Show answer
Establish a transparent calculation at line level. Start with the supported gross amount, subtract valid refunds, discounts, or other credits, and calculate the net amount according to the approved claim rules. Then identify which net amounts are eligible to proceed, which must remain held, and which are ineligible. The result should reconcile to the submitted claim and must not include unresolved or prohibited items in the amount eligible for approval. For illustration, suppose a fictional claim has IDR 300,000 of supported training supplies and IDR 75,000 of an ineligible personal penalty. The gross claim is IDR 375,000, but only IDR 300,000 may proceed for consideration before remaining approval and payment controls. This example is arithmetic only; it does not establish any organization's eligibility policy. Formula changes must be controlled and independently tested, including credits, partial eligibility, foreign currency, and rounding cases.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate gross, credits, net, eligible, held, and ineligible amounts for a mixed claim and reconcile every total."
Key point:
- Submitted, eligible, approved, and payable amounts are different measures.
- Held and ineligible amounts must not leak into the approved payment calculation.
18. Q: How should reimbursement status fields be designed so that a status does not imply more progress than the evidence supports?
A: Show answer
Define separate status domains for the claim, individual evidence checks, exception handling, payment, reconciliation, and final closure. For example, a claim may be submitted, under review, held, eligible, partially eligible, or rejected, while a payment may be pending, unknown, partial, successful, or failed. An evidence status may indicate that a document has not been checked, has been verified, is missing, is inconsistent, or raises an integrity concern. Each status must have a precise meaning, an assigned owner, and a documented condition for transition. Never use a single generic “complete” flag to represent eligibility, approval, payment, reconciliation, and closure simultaneously. In particular, approval must not set payment status to paid, and payment handoff must not set reconciliation status to closed. Status definitions should be approved, tested, and presented consistently in reports, registers, and user interfaces.

Study instruction:
- "Design separate claim, evidence, payment, exception, and closure status fields and define the proof required for each transition."
Key point:
- A status is a control assertion and needs supporting evidence.
- Claim approval, actual payment, reconciliation, and closure must remain distinct.
19. Q: What evidence should be required when a claim moves from submission through review and delegated approval?
A: Show answer
Each transition should identify its triggering action, authorized actor, required evidence, permitted next states, and actions that must be blocked. Submission should preserve the received version and claimant declarations. Intake should record completeness and routing. Review should capture item-level decisions, evidence checks, calculations, coding, and unresolved exceptions. Approval should record the approved amount, approver identity, time, delegation basis, version, and decision rationale. A held claim must not progress merely because an operator changes the status field. If a workflow cannot establish that mandatory checks have passed, it must stop and request a corrective action or authorized decision. Build and test a controlled transition matrix that rejects unsafe transitions, such as approving a version that was not reviewed or handing off a claim before the required approval. Logs should preserve unsuccessful transition attempts as well as successful decisions when they are relevant to control monitoring.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a claim through every workflow transition and identify the evidence proving each action was authorized."
Key point:
- Workflow states should be evidence-backed, not manually assumed.
- Mandatory control failures must stop progression until corrected and retested.
20. Q: When should an NGO return, reject, amend, or cancel a reimbursement claim?
A: Show answer
Return a claim when necessary information or evidence is missing and the issue can reasonably be corrected by the claimant. Reject the relevant claim or line when an established policy or authorization rule makes it ineligible, recording the grounds and notification. Amend a claim when a supported correction is permitted, preserving the prior version and identifying changed fields, amounts, evidence, and approvals. Cancel a claim when an authorized cancellation is appropriate, while retaining the record of the request and decision. These outcomes should not be interchangeable. A return does not establish eligibility, and a cancelled record should not disappear from the audit trail. Material changes to the amount, payee, currency, coding, donor allocation, or supporting evidence may invalidate the scope of the previous approval and require revalidation or fresh approval. The organization should define when resubmission creates a new version or a new claim ID and how prior decisions are linked to the final record.

Study instruction:
- "Classify five claim problems as return, rejection, amendment, or cancellation and document the reason and permitted next state."
Key point:
- Each disposition has a distinct purpose and evidence requirement.
- Prior versions and decisions must remain available after a change.
21. Q: Why must payment handoff be distinguished from proof that a reimbursement has actually been paid?
A: Show answer
Approval authorizes the claim within the scope of the approver's authority, while handoff passes an approved payment instruction to the designated payment process. Neither action proves that cash was transferred successfully or received by the correct payee. The payment operator should receive an instruction tied to the approved claim version, payee, amount, currency, coding, and relevant reference. The actual payment process must then provide reliable evidence of the outcome, such as a confirmed transaction record or bank statement reference. Only after verifying the authoritative payment result should the organization update payment status and begin the related reconciliation. A queued instruction, prepared bank file, or screenshot of an unfinished process is not sufficient proof of completed disbursement. Preserve the handoff timestamp, receiving operator or system, transaction reference, and actual payment evidence so that the claim's full path can be reconstructed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one claim from approval through payment instruction, bank evidence, and final accounting reconciliation."
Key point:
- Approval and payment handoff do not establish successful disbursement.
- Paid status must be supported by reliable evidence of the actual payment outcome.
22. Q: How should failed, partial, duplicate, or unknown payment results be handled without creating a second unauthorized payment?
A: Show answer
Record the actual payment status, amount, reference, date, payee, currency, and supporting evidence before deciding on the next action. For a failed payment, establish whether the failure is final and whether any debit occurred. For an unknown result, contact the authoritative payment source and investigate the existing instruction before initiating another transaction. For a partial payment, identify the amount paid and the amount outstanding separately. For a suspected duplicate payment, block further release of the affected balance and investigate both transactions. Do not treat an attempted or queued transfer as proof of payment. The payment operator and reconciler should document their independent conclusions and maintain links between any retry, recovery, or correction and the original transaction. Where a payment status remains uncertain, preserve the unresolved state and escalate it according to the approved service level. An automatic retry is unsafe unless the control design can establish that the prior instruction cannot result in another disbursement.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate failed, partial, duplicate, and unknown payment results and test the controls that prevent unauthorized retries."
Key point:
- Verify the earlier payment's actual state before reissuing an instruction.
- Partial and unresolved transactions must remain visible until reconciled.
23. Q: What accounting treatment should be considered when an NGO receives an approved reimbursement claim that has not yet been paid?
A: Show answer
Accounting recognition and payment processing are different events. Where an expense has been incurred and the applicable recognition criteria are satisfied, the organization may need to recognize the expense and a corresponding liability before making the reimbursement payment. The precise treatment depends on the applicable accounting framework, the transaction facts, organizational policy, and any material donor or contractual requirements. Approval is important evidence, but it does not necessarily determine the original date of expense recognition. The team should identify when the goods or services were received, when the organization became obligated, whether the claimant personally paid the cost, and whether any existing advance or other settlement already covers it. A generic entry might debit an appropriate expense or asset account and credit an appropriate liability, but the account selection and recognition date must be established under the applicable rules. The liability is subsequently reduced when a supported payment is made and properly recorded. Avoid recording a second expense merely because reimbursement is processed after the original activity.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an illustrative journal for an incurred eligible expense and subsequent reimbursement, then explain how the recognition date is established."
Key point:
- Expense recognition and cash settlement are separate accounting questions.
- The approved accounting framework and transaction facts determine the correct entry and timing.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
24. Q: How should reimbursement expenses be recognized at month-end when the claim is submitted or approved after the reporting cut-off?
A: Show answer
Month-end procedures should identify expenses incurred before the reporting date that have not yet entered the normal claim workflow. Useful sources include employee submissions, pending approvals, event records, delivery confirmations, expense-card data, and other transaction evidence. The accounting team should evaluate whether the underlying expense and liability satisfy the applicable recognition criteria for the reporting period. The later submission date or reimbursement date does not, on its own, determine the accounting period. Estimates or accruals should be supported by reasonable evidence, approved where required, and reconciled when the final claim is received. The organization should avoid double-counting an accrual when posting the eventual claim and should follow the approved policy for reversals or adjustments. Differences between estimated and actual amounts should be investigated and documented. The applicable accounting framework determines the precise recognition, measurement, and reversal method; an internal reimbursement deadline must not override the relevant reporting requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Review claims submitted after month-end and determine whether any expense or liability belongs to the closed period."
Key point:
- Financial reporting cut-off follows the applicable recognition criteria, not merely the claim-processing date.
- Subsequent claim processing must be reconciled to avoid duplicate expense recognition.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Accrual and general-ledger workflows
25. Q: How should a reimbursement liability be reconciled from claim approval through payment and final closure?
A: Show answer
The reconciliation should begin with the approved amount and version, then compare the payment instruction, actual payment evidence, posted journal, and applicable general-ledger balance. Record the amount approved, amount paid, outstanding balance, any deduction or adjustment, reference numbers, dates, and explanation of every difference. Confirm that the transaction was posted to the correct entity, liability or clearing account, expense category, currency, and project dimensions. Where a payment has been reversed, reissued, or only partially completed, link the related transactions without erasing the original history. Outstanding balances should have an assigned owner, a due date, an age, and a defined follow-up action. The reconciler must not simply adjust the difference to an expense account to force the ledger to agree. Closure should occur only when the claim's required review, payment, reconciliation, and record-retention conditions are satisfied. Material unexplained differences must remain open and be escalated.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a claim-to-ledger reconciliation and identify the evidence required to clear each difference."
Key point:
- The approved claim, actual disbursement, and ledger should reconcile with an auditable trail.
- Unexplained balances cannot be cleared by unsupported adjustments.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger and reconciliation workflows
26. Q: When can a reimbursement claim be marked closed, and what unresolved matters prevent closure?
A: Show answer
Closure should follow an explicit checklist covering the final claim version, evidence, eligibility decisions, required approvals, payment outcome, journal posting where applicable, claim-to-payment reconciliation, and required record retention. Any applicable exception must be resolved or left open under an explicitly permitted and authorized status; it must not be silently bypassed. Outstanding actions may include an unresolved duplicate alert, missing evidence, an unverified tax conclusion, pending donor review, unexplained payment difference, open refund, or incomplete integrity investigation. If the organization's policy permits separate administrative completion while a different issue remains open, that distinction must be documented without incorrectly marking the whole matter fully settled. The closure rule should name the responsible role and specify the evidence retained. Reports should distinguish a claim that is approved, paid, reconciled, administratively complete, or closed so management can see what remains outstanding and why.

Study instruction:
- "Create a closure checklist and test whether a claim with an open reconciliation difference can incorrectly pass the final gate."
Key point:
- Closure is a controlled conclusion requiring evidence, not a convenient status change.
- Open mandatory actions must block full closure.
27. Q: What records should be preserved to support a reimbursement claim during an internal or external audit?
A: Show answer
A complete audit file should connect the original submission and supporting documents with the claimant declarations, eligibility decisions, duplicate checks, coding, donor and tax reviews where applicable, approval evidence, payment instructions, actual payment confirmation, ledger posting, reconciliation, exceptions, and final closure. Include the claim ID, version, entity, relevant dates, responsible individuals, approval references, source-policy versions, and links between associated records. Preserve the original evidence and a clear record of subsequent amendments. A report that shows a final amount is not a substitute for the transactions and decisions supporting it. Maintain a controlled index and test whether a reviewer can move in both directions: from a reported amount to the original claim and from the source expense to the final reporting records. Access, retention, and disposal must follow applicable legal, donor, contractual, and organizational requirements. The organization should establish who is authorized to retrieve, amend, archive, or dispose of the records.

Study instruction:
- "Select a completed claim and reconstruct its entire history from initial submission to final ledger and payment evidence."
Key point:
- A defensible audit trail links documents, decisions, transactions, and outcomes.
- Original evidence and previous versions must remain traceable throughout the retention period.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger and journal documentation
28. Q: How should reimbursement records containing personal, banking, or health information be protected?
A: Show answer
Collect only information necessary to process, approve, pay, reconcile, and retain the claim under the applicable rules. Assign role-based access, separate particularly sensitive supporting records where appropriate, and restrict access to bank details and health information. Avoid embedding bank account numbers, medical narratives, authentication credentials, or other unnecessary sensitive fields in widely circulated reports. Determine applicable privacy obligations and retention rules from the organization's legal context and processing activities; the existence of a law or a template statement alone does not establish the organization's compliance. Establish procedures for secure transfer, correction, access requests, incident escalation, and authorized disposal. When evidence integrity is questioned, preserve the relevant material securely and limit access to personnel responsible for the investigation. Review exports and report distribution so a convenience feature does not expose more personal data than the recipient needs.

Study instruction:
- "Review a reimbursement form and identify unnecessary sensitive fields, excessive access, and unsafe evidence-sharing practices."
Key point:
- Data minimization and access control should be built into the workflow.
- Applicable privacy and retention obligations must be independently verified.
29. Q: What should the organization do when evidence suggests tampering, a conflict of interest, or another integrity incident?
A: Show answer
Stop the affected claim action and preserve the original documents, submission version, relevant system records, and communication history. Create a restricted incident record stating the concern, detection date, affected claim and lines, evidence available, immediate containment, responsible owner, and required escalation. Avoid unsupported accusations or conclusions. Refer the matter to the authorized integrity, compliance, legal, or investigation function under the organization's procedures. Ensure that the person potentially involved does not control the investigation or decide alone whether the hold can be removed. Record the permitted disposition and the evidence required to resume processing, reject the claim, or take another authorized action. Any subsequent claim correction should preserve the previous version and link to the incident record. After resolution, identify the control weakness and implement a corrective action with a named owner, deadline, and independent retest where appropriate.

Study instruction:
- "Work through a fictional suspected-alteration case and document containment, evidence preservation, referral, and authorized resolution."
Key point:
- An integrity concern needs controlled investigation rather than automatic approval or unsupported accusations.
- Evidence must be preserved and the decision to resume processing independently authorized.
30. Q: How should an NGO investigate a recurring reimbursement error and prevent it from happening again?
A: Show answer
Record the specific error, affected claims, dates, amounts where relevant, detection method, impact, immediate response, responsible owner, and required follow-up. Investigate the root cause instead of stopping at descriptions such as “carelessness” or “incorrect entry.” Possible causes include incomplete instructions, inconsistent source templates, an outdated mapping, missing validation, unclear approval authority, weak evidence checks, incorrect formula logic, or a broken handoff between functions. Determine whether other claims may be affected, quantify the scope where feasible, and correct the underlying records under controlled procedures. Design preventive or detective controls that address the cause, update the SOP or training if needed, and test the correction with representative transactions. Keep the original incident record and evidence of the retest. A case should not be closed merely because the immediate claim has been processed; the wider process weakness must also be addressed when the risk requires it.

Study instruction:
- "Document the root cause of a recurring claim error and define a corrective action, owner, due date, and retest criterion."
Key point:
- Corrective action should address the underlying process weakness.
- Repeated errors require both transaction correction and control improvement.
31. Q: Why should approval be tied to a specific claim version, and what happens if the amount or payee changes after approval?
A: Show answer
Approval applies to the content the authorized approver actually reviewed. A controlled version should identify the claim, line items, amount, currency, payee reference, coding, supporting evidence, decision, and approval timestamp. If a material field changes afterward, the prior approval may no longer cover the revised transaction. The workflow should compare the current version with the approved version, classify the change, assess its impact, and require renewed review or approval according to policy. Material changes to amount, payee, currency, project, donor allocation, or supporting evidence should not silently inherit the earlier decision. Preserve the original approved record, the amendment request, changed fields, reason, reviewer decision, and new approval when needed. Payment handoff must use the version that is validly approved. A file name that still says “approved” is not sufficient evidence that the underlying content remains approved.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a change in amount and payee after approval, then identify which control gates must be repeated."
Key point:
- Approval is version-specific and does not automatically extend to materially changed content.
- Revised claims need controlled comparison and renewed authorization when required.
32. Q: Which controlled forms and registers should support a complete reimbursement process?
A: Show answer
The controlled package may include the claim submission form, claimant declaration, evidence checklist, expense-category and evidence matrix, claimant and delegation register, segregation-of-duties matrix, chart-of-accounts mapping, donor and tax review records, foreign-exchange parameter register, approval matrix, workflow and status register, exception register, payment-handoff log, payment-outcome register, reconciliation and aging register, incident record, retention index, KPI dashboard, and acceptance-test inventory. Each record should have an owner, defined fields, a purpose, effective version, access rules, and a retention basis. Conditional forms should be used only when the corresponding transaction or risk requires them. The organization should distinguish mandatory records from optional or context-dependent documents. A blank form does not prove that the control was performed, and a completed form should not automatically be accepted without checking the supporting evidence. The package must be reviewed for consistency so the same transaction ID and version can connect records without relying on manually retyped descriptions.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory the required forms and registers, identify each owner, and trace how one claim ID links the records."
Key point:
- Controlled records make responsibilities and evidence requirements explicit.
- A form's existence does not prove that its control was executed effectively.
33. Q: How can Excel tables and validation lists improve the quality of reimbursement claim data?
A: Show answer
Store claim headers, claim lines, evidence references, approvals, payment outcomes, and exceptions in structured tables with stable identifiers and defined column names. Use validation lists for approved categories, currencies, status values, legal entities, project codes, and other controlled master data. Require appropriate fields and apply type checks, such as valid dates and numeric amounts. Use formulas that clearly separate gross, credits, net, held, eligible, and paid amounts. Avoid mixing user input with formula outputs or placing final approval fields within uncontrolled working areas. Keep mapping tables in a controlled location and maintain ownership of configuration changes. Data validation improves consistency but cannot prove that a receipt is genuine, a donor permits an expense, or an approval is authorized. Those matters still require evidence and review. Test what happens when a code is missing, a new category is added, the table grows, or an invalid value is pasted into a validated range.

Study instruction:
- "Design a structured claim table with validated codes and test missing fields, invalid categories, expanding rows, and formula integrity."
Key point:
- Standardized tables and validation reduce preventable input errors.
- Spreadsheet controls support, but do not replace, substantive review and authorization.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support — Create and format tables
- Microsoft Learn — Excel Validation object
34. Q: How can Power Query help consolidate reimbursement data without weakening evidence and approval controls?
A: Show answer
Power Query can import information from claim forms, finance registers, payment files, expense-card statements, and controlled master-data tables. Repeatable transformations can standardize column names and data types, normalize dates and currency fields, append monthly files, and create candidate duplicate or missing-data lists. Store a reference to the source file, extraction date, processing batch, and query version so a reviewer can identify the origin of the output. Reconcile row counts and control totals before and after transformations, investigate records that fail mapping, and keep the exception output visible. Do not let a query silently remove transactions simply because a value is missing or duplicated. Nor should a query automatically approve claims or decide complex donor, tax, or accounting questions without authorized rules and appropriate human review. Refreshable data is useful only when source changes and transformation effects are monitored and the final numbers remain traceable to evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Create a query that combines claim data from two sources, then reconcile totals and investigate excluded or unmatched records."
Key point:
- Power Query supports repeatable data preparation and exception identification.
- Transformations must preserve traceability and must not silently remove valid records.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
35. Q: What reimbursement tasks can safely be automated with VBA, and which decisions should remain under authorized human control?
A: Show answer
VBA can assist with repetitive tasks such as generating claim IDs, creating controlled output files, producing review checklists, flagging overdue items, refreshing reporting views, exporting approved reports, and writing processing events to a log. Automation should not remove independent judgment from eligibility, conflict-of-interest, donor, tax, integrity, or unusual accounting decisions unless a validated and authorized rule explicitly governs the decision. Every automated action should check required inputs and workflow prerequisites before proceeding. Error handling should stop safely when a worksheet, source file, mapping, or required value is missing. Log the time, action, batch, output reference, and outcome where appropriate. Restrict macro distribution and access, retain approved versions, and test the code against realistic positive and negative cases. A generated journal, PDF, or payment file must not be treated as proof that a reviewer approved it or that a bank transaction succeeded.

Study instruction:
- "Design an automation boundary that separates routine preparation from specialist review and payment authorization."
Key point:
- Automate repetitive processing while preserving independent decisions and mandatory gates.
- Generated outputs need separate approval and outcome evidence.

- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA reference
36. Q: How should negative testing establish that a reimbursement workflow rejects unsafe claims and unauthorized actions?
A: Show answer
Develop test cases that deliberately introduce missing required fields, absent proof of payment, inconsistent receipts, unresolved duplicates, ineligible expenses, unverified tax treatment, invalid coding, unsupported exchange rates, arithmetic discrepancies, self-approval, expired authority, and material changes after approval. Additional tests should cover early payment handoff, claims marked paid without authoritative evidence, unsafe retry attempts, overwritten history, premature closure, and unauthorized configuration changes. Each test must define the starting state, injected defect, expected rejection or hold, actual result, evidence, and tester. Critical negative tests should demonstrate that the real validation path blocks unsafe progression rather than merely displaying a warning that users can ignore. Keep test cases and fictional sample data separate from operational claim records. Document failed tests, correction actions, retest results, and the release decision. A list of planned tests is not evidence that the tests actually ran or passed; only recorded results support an acceptance conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "Run negative cases against the workflow and record the expected and actual result for every critical control."
Key point:
- Control effectiveness must be demonstrated through execution, not inferred from a checklist.
- Failed tests must be corrected and successfully retested before the affected function is released.
37. Q: What management indicators can show whether reimbursement controls are working effectively?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the number and percentage of claims with missing evidence, unresolved duplicate alerts, held lines awaiting specialist review, invalid or incomplete coding, overdue claims, approval exceptions, failed or unknown payment attempts, unreconciled balances, and unresolved integrity incidents. Measure processing time by stage and the percentage of submissions returned for correction. Define each metric precisely, including the source register, time period, denominator, owner, reporting frequency, threshold, and required action. For example, a timely-submission rate should include all claims within the defined population rather than only those completed successfully. Pair volume measures with risk indicators: a falling backlog is not an improvement if claims are being closed without sufficient evidence. Review trends and recurring causes, assign corrective actions, and record management decisions. Validate dashboard measures against the underlying claim and payment records because a stale workbook status can misrepresent actual performance.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a reimbursement-control dashboard and independently verify each KPI against the underlying registers."
Key point:
- KPI definitions must be consistent, reproducible, and tied to action.
- Performance measures must not reward premature closure or bypassed controls.
38. Q: What should an NGO verify before formally adopting a reimbursement SOP drafted from a fictional example?
A: Show answer
The organization should identify the actual legal entity and authorized policy owner, confirm the applicable accounting framework, verify current legal and tax requirements, obtain approved reimbursement and expense policies, confirm donor conditions, and establish legitimate approval delegations. It should define claimant categories, submission deadlines, evidence requirements, expense caps, exchange-rate sources, retention periods, coding dimensions, specialist review triggers, and required payment evidence from authoritative sources rather than illustrative defaults. Relevant personnel should review the workflow and forms, including Finance, Human Resources, Tax, Grants, Operations, Records, and the authorized governing or delegated approval body as applicable. Perform a walkthrough using a completed historical claim and test the negative controls before implementation. Document unresolved matters as blocked or unverified, approve the final version, communicate it to users, and establish a review date. A fictional demonstration document, even when technically well structured, does not establish organizational adoption or legal compliance.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an adoption checklist that separates verified requirements, missing authorities, configuration tasks, approvals, and acceptance tests."
Key point:
- A draft SOP becomes operational only through verified applicability and authorized adoption.
- Unverified parameters must remain blocked until resolved and formally approved.
39. Q: How should the SOP handle an expense that was charged to the wrong project or cost centre after initial review?
A: Show answer
Confirm the original transaction, its approved purpose, actual beneficiary, relevant donor or project restrictions, and the coding decision already recorded. Determine whether the proposed reclassification is permitted under the accounting framework, the donor agreement, and organizational procedures. A correction should preserve the initial coding and document the reason, affected dimensions, proposed destination, supporting evidence, authorization, and impact on donor or management reporting. Recalculate any dependent totals and verify whether approval must be repeated because the revised allocation changes the substance or authorization of the transaction. Do not simply move a cost to a project with available budget. Post the correction through the approved accounting process, link the correcting entry to the original transaction, and reconcile the result to the claim and project records. If the appropriate project or donor allocation remains uncertain, retain a hold and obtain the competent reviewer's decision.

Study instruction:
- "Review an incorrectly coded claim and trace the proposed correction from evidence through authorization and ledger reconciliation."
Key point:
- Coding corrections need substantive justification and an audit trail.
- Available project budget is not sufficient authority to reallocate a cost.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger and journal workflows
40. Q: What should be done if an approved reimbursement is later found to be ineligible, duplicated, or paid in excess?
A: Show answer
Identify the original claim and payment, preserve the approval and supporting evidence, and establish the factual basis of the issue. Determine whether the error affects the claimant's outstanding balance, general ledger, donor charges, tax records, financial reports, or other claims. Calculate the amount affected and decide on the appropriate corrective action under the applicable policy and law. Depending on the facts, actions may include correcting a coding or journal error, seeking a supported refund, initiating an authorized recovery process, correcting a donor allocation, or escalating a potential integrity incident. Do not automatically deduct the amount from future pay or pursue recovery without verifying the lawful authority and appropriate procedure. Maintain a recovery register with the amount, basis, responsible owner, due date, communications, accounting treatment, and evidence of settlement. Reconcile the final recovery or correction to the original transaction and assess whether a systemic control weakness needs remediation.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a fictional overpayment from detection through approved corrective action, recovery or adjustment, and final reconciliation."
Key point:
- Recovery must be supported by evidence and the appropriate legal and organizational authority.
- The original transaction and all subsequent corrections must remain traceable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General ledger and journal workflows
41. Q: How should staff training and handover support consistent application of reimbursement controls?
A: Show answer
Training should explain which expenses qualify, what evidence is needed, how to complete the form, how to disclose duplicate claims or conflicts, and when specialist review is required. Reviewers and approvers also need role-specific guidance on delegation, evidence assessment, version binding, exceptions, and payment controls. Use scenarios to demonstrate incomplete receipts, late submissions, mixed eligibility, uncertain tax treatment, foreign-currency amounts, project restrictions, and unknown payment outcomes. Maintain a record of training version, participants, completion date, assessment results where applicable, and follow-up for recurring errors. Handover materials should identify current policy sources, workflow owners, controlled parameters, escalation paths, open issues, and backup responsibilities. Avoid embedding passwords or other authentication secrets in training files. When policies, forms, or systems change, communicate the approved effective date and provide targeted retraining. Monitor whether the training is working by reviewing error rates and the quality of actual claim evidence rather than counting attendance alone.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a scenario-based training checklist and identify the evidence that users understand each mandatory control."
Key point:
- Role-specific training helps prevent predictable submission and review errors.
- Handover documents should explain current controls without exposing sensitive credentials.
42. Q: How should an NGO monitor changes to reimbursement policy, tax rules, donor agreements, and system configurations over time?
A: Show answer
Assign a responsible owner to monitor authoritative sources and changes to approved organizational policies, donor agreements, legal requirements, accounting guidance, and software configuration. For each change, document the source, publication or approval date, effective date, affected transaction categories, impacted forms and parameters, required system changes, testing, and user communication. Do not update calculation logic based only on an informal email or an unverified summary. Assess the impact on open claims, prior approvals, posted journals, payment instructions, and reporting. Test the updated rules using representative and negative cases, obtain required authorization, and retain the previous version and transition record. Make clear which version applies to each claim based on approved rules and relevant dates. Where requirements conflict or applicability remains unresolved, maintain the affected hold and obtain competent advice before releasing the changed workflow.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a hypothetical policy or tax-rule change through impact assessment, configuration, testing, approval, and effective-date control."
Key point:
- Policy and parameter changes require authoritative sources and controlled implementation.
- Historical claim decisions must remain explainable under the versions that applied at the time.

Table 4
Project Agreement Cash Advance Controls
1. Q: How should the cash advance cycle for a project agreement be controlled, from request through final balance closure?
A: Show answer
The cash advance cycle begins with identifying the activity's funding needs, verifying the applicable project agreement, validating the recipient, confirming budget availability, and assessing cost eligibility. Finance then reviews the transaction classification, risk, outstanding previous advances, and the basis for the requested amount. An authorized officer approves the request before Treasury transfers funds to the verified recipient. After disbursement, the balance must be monitored until the recipient submits an expenditure report, supporting evidence, and a refund of unused funds where applicable. Finance independently assesses the eligibility of claimed expenses, records accepted expenditure, retains unsupported amounts as outstanding balances, processes recoveries, reconciles the general ledger, and closes the advance only when all settlement requirements are satisfied. The register, agreement, approvals, bank evidence, liquidation report, journals, and reconciliations must be linked. The supplied document is a fictional example, so its procedures and authorities must be verified before implementation in an actual organization.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one project advance from the initial request to a zero balance or an officially documented resolution of the remaining balance."

Key point:
- Disbursement is not the end of the process; settlement must be evidenced.
- A balance can be closed only after expenses, refunds, and accounting records have been reconciled.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
2. Q: What is the difference between a cash advance, project expense, reimbursement, and supplier prepayment?
A: Show answer
A cash advance is money provided before final accountability for its use has been established. Depending on the applicable accounting framework and transaction substance, it is generally tracked as an advance asset or receivable until supported expenditure is accepted or the amount repayable is settled. A project expense is recognized when the relevant expense recognition criteria are met. A reimbursement compensates an eligible party for costs already incurred and therefore requires supporting evidence and an approved claim. A supplier prepayment relates to a procurement contract for goods or services and may belong to a separate Accounts Payable process. The transaction's name or payment method is not sufficient to determine its classification. Consider its economic substance, contract, recipient, payment terms, and available evidence. A supplier prepayment must not be classified as a project cash advance merely to use the same form. Similarly, an advance to a staff member or project implementer must not be expensed immediately merely because cash has been disbursed.

Study instruction:
- "Compare four transaction scenarios using their substance, source documents, accountability stage, and appropriate accounting classification."

Key point:
- A cash outflow does not automatically establish an expense.
- Classification must follow transaction substance and the applicable policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
3. Q: How should the meaning of PKS and the identities of the parties be verified before the SOP is used?
A: Show answer
The term PKS must follow the definition actually used in the organization's documents and executed agreement. The supplied example assumes, for fictional purposes, that PKS means Perjanjian Kerja Sama (Cooperation Agreement), but this assumption does not establish the legal meaning or terms of an actual agreement. Finance should obtain the complete agreement, verify the identities and authority of the parties, and review the collaboration's purpose, effective period, scope of activities, funding amount, advance conditions, accountability deadlines, treatment of unused funds, and dispute-resolution mechanisms. Terms such as partner, fund recipient, internal custodian, subgrant recipient, and supplier must not be treated as interchangeable without verification. The legal and accounting roles of each party should be confirmed by the competent functions. If the definition, agreement terms, or signatory authority remain unclear, mark the document as unverified and hold operational steps that depend on that information. The SOP may be adopted only after its definitions, scope, and responsibilities have been confirmed by the organization's authorized officers.

Study instruction:
- "Verify the definition of PKS in the actual agreement and match it to each party's identity, authority, and obligations."

Key point:
- An acronym in an example does not establish the legal meaning of an actual agreement.
- Verified documents and signatory authority are prerequisites for operational use.

Source links:
4. Q: Which transactions belong within the project cash advance SOP, and which should be routed to other procedures?
A: Show answer
The scope should be defined by transaction substance and organizational policy. A project cash advance SOP may cover requests, eligibility reviews, approvals, transfers, fund utilization, liquidation, refunds, recoveries, reconciliation, and balance closure. However, supplier payments, travel advances, petty cash, reimbursements, payroll, subgrant installments, and contract prepayments may require separate procedures. The source document specifically identifies interfaces with procurement/accounts payable, travel advances, and partner-related processes. For each interface, specify the process owner, linking reference number, transferred documents, receiving party, deadline, and evidence that the handover is complete. Do not invent detailed procedures for other SOPs that have not been approved. When a transaction moves between processes, its balance and documentation obligations must remain traceable without duplicate accounting or gaps in accountability. Review the exclusions whenever the organization changes its program structure, recipient categories, or funding arrangements.

Study instruction:
- "Classify examples involving suppliers, field staff, travel, project partners, and reimbursements under the appropriate process."

Key point:
- Clear scope prevents a transaction from being processed under the wrong SOP.
- Transfers between procedures require documented handover and ownership.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
5. Q: How should the hierarchy of requirements governing project cash advances be established?
A: Show answer
Before approving an advance, the organization should identify applicable legislation, its accounting policies, donor or funding agreements, cooperation agreements, approved budgets, delegated authority schedules, and relevant SOPs. These requirements do not all serve the same purpose. An agreement may restrict the permitted purposes and eligibility of expenditure, while accounting policies govern recognition, measurement, presentation, and balance clearance. Legislation may establish contractual, tax, privacy, or record-retention requirements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides examples of accounting and financial processes in the United Nations context, but it does not automatically become binding policy for every NGO. Finance should identify which sources apply to its own entity, record their versions and verification dates, and ask Legal, Compliance, or another competent adviser to assess conflicting requirements. Where the applicable requirements remain uncertain, affected transactions should be held or escalated according to risk rather than decided solely by staff habits or spreadsheet settings.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a register of the governing requirements for one advance and identify the verifier and approval status of each source."

Key point:
- Reference guidance does not replace the actual entity's rules and executed agreement.
- Conflicting or uncertain requirements require verification and an authorized decision.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual
6. Q: How should agreement, project, activity, budget, and recipient eligibility be checked before an advance is issued?
A: Show answer
The assessment should establish that the relevant agreement is effective and that the activity falls within its approved scope. Match the project identifier, activity period, workplan, donor code, approved budget, requested amount, recipient category, and disbursement conditions. Check whether the funds may be used for the intended activities and whether cost limits, procurement requirements, matching contributions, or funding restrictions apply. Review previous advances, outstanding liquidations, and conditions that must be met before another disbursement. Incomplete documents, expired agreements, insufficient budgets, or ineligible recipients should result in an ineligible or pending status, unless an authorized decision is permitted under the applicable rules. Finance must not assume that program approval automatically replaces budget verification or financial authorization. Document the eligibility conclusion, supporting evidence, reviewer, date, decision rationale, and follow-up when a request is rejected or deferred.

Study instruction:
- "Test one request by matching its agreement, workplan, budget, recipient category, and outstanding previous advances."

Key point:
- Approval must be based on verified contractual requirements and budget availability.
- Funds must not be released while material prerequisites remain unmet.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
7. Q: How should recipient categories and due diligence affect cash advance controls?
A: Show answer
Identify whether the recipient is a staff member holding funds, an internal field officer, another project office, or an external party under an agreement. Each category may have different authority, responsibilities, identity documentation, reporting methods, and recovery arrangements. Verify the recipient's identity, destination account, relationship to the activity, person responsible for holding the funds, and any applicable eligibility requirements. For external parties, inspect legal-identity documentation and the due diligence required by the organization or donor. For internal custodians, confirm their assignment, permitted uses, handover arrangements, and personal accountability. Record due diligence results while protecting confidential information through appropriate access restrictions. Conflicts of interest, inconsistent identity details, mismatched bank accounts, or a history of outstanding advances should be addressed before approval. Any exception to normal requirements must be approved by an appropriately authorized officer and must not override mandatory legal or contractual requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Compare due diligence requirements for staff, internal custodians, and external partners, identifying the additional evidence each category needs."

Key point:
- Controls must reflect the recipient's role and risk profile.
- Identity and transfer details must be verified before disbursement.

8. Q: How should the roles of maker, checker, approver, Treasury, and reviewer be separated in the advance process?
A: Show answer
Responsibilities should prevent one person from controlling the entire process without independent review. The requester explains the funding need; the program owner confirms the activity; Finance checks the budget, classification, documentation, and existing balance; the reviewer tests the calculations; the approver authorizes the request under delegated authority; Treasury executes the payment; and an authorized independent officer checks the bank evidence and reconciliation. Journal preparation and posting must also follow approved procedures and have review evidence. In a small organization, complete segregation may not be feasible. In that case, use compensating controls such as independent pre-payment approval, post-payment bank verification, and periodic review of outstanding balances. Prepare a RACI matrix identifying who is responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed. Compare actual system access with this matrix to ensure that a preparer cannot bypass required approvals. External consultants may perform agreed tasks, but internal oversight and decision-making responsibilities must remain clear.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a RACI matrix and identify points where one person could prepare, check, approve, pay, and close the same transaction."

Key point:
- Approval and payment execution require clearly evidenced authority.
- Inadequate segregation requires documented compensating controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
9. Q: What documents should accompany a cash advance request to support a complete review?
A: Show answer
The request package should link the requester and recipient to a unique request number, agreement, project, activity, period, budget, purpose, amount breakdown, activity schedule, and planned liquidation date. Include documents required by policy, such as the workplan, program approval, budget confirmation, cost breakdown, and evidence that contractual conditions have been met. Check whether the recipient has previous advances that remain unsettled or whether the same transaction has already been submitted. Give each request a unique identifier and record the receipt date, completeness assessment, review result, reviewer, status, and reason for returning it for correction. Missing mandatory documents should result in an incomplete status; a request must not pass merely because the activity deadline is approaching. Any revised documentation should have a version, explanation, and approval history. The checklist must be tailored to the actual agreement so that it neither invents unnecessary requirements nor overlooks material obligations.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a request-document checklist and test a sample to confirm that each requirement has supporting evidence or an authorized exception."

Key point:
- Complete documentation enables review but does not replace approval.
- Unique transaction identifiers help prevent duplicate requests and support traceability.
