- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
10. Q: How should budget availability and cost eligibility be verified before approving a cash advance?
A: Show answer
Finance should compare the funding need with the approved project budget, available budget balance, relevant commitments, prior transactions, and funding limits under the agreement. Cost-eligibility checks should address the purpose, period, activity, expense category, amount limits, procurement requirements where applicable, and supporting evidence. An unbudgeted or prohibited cost must not be treated as eligible merely because the activity is urgent. Determine whether a budget amendment or special approval is permitted and identify the officer authorized to make that decision. Where a cost cannot yet be classified, request clarification and hold the affected amount until a decision is received. Document the eligibility rationale so another reviewer can understand why a cost was approved or rejected. Available budget alone does not prove that an expenditure satisfies donor or agreement requirements. Where multiple projects or donors are involved, confirm that costs will not be double-charged or allocated without a verified basis.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the available budget with the advance request and test one eligible cost, one cost requiring clarification, and one prohibited cost."

Key point:
- Budget availability and cost eligibility are separate assessments.
- Budget exceptions must follow the applicable authority and approval requirements.

Source links:
11. Q: How should risk assessment and due diligence influence a cash advance decision?
A: Show answer
Risk assessment should consider the advance amount, type of activity, location, recipient capacity, previous reporting quality, outstanding balances, agreement completeness, procurement exposure, potential conflicts of interest, and ability to trace the funds. The results should determine the level of review, additional evidence required, monitoring frequency, authorization limits, or whether disbursement should be deferred. High risk does not always require rejection, but it does require appropriate mitigation approved by an authorized officer. Risks that cannot be mitigated or that violate mandatory requirements should stop the process. Record the assessment, information sources, indicators identified, decision, additional controls, and action owners. Do not treat an informal risk assessment as a substitute for formal due diligence where law or donor requirements prescribe specific checks. Reassess risks when the amount, recipient, activity, bank account, or period changes. Identified risks should also affect post-disbursement monitoring and recovery priorities when liquidation is overdue.

Study instruction:
- "Build a risk matrix based on amount, settlement history, evidence completeness, and activity complexity, then assign mitigation actions to each risk level."

Key point:
- Risk determines the depth of review and intensity of monitoring.
- Exceptions require documented approval and traceability.

Source links:
12. Q: How should the approval matrix and pre-disbursement conditions be designed?
A: Show answer
The approval matrix should specify authority limits, substitute approvers, review stages, transaction types, mandatory conditions, and approval evidence. Financial thresholds must come from approved organizational policy, not fictional values in the example. Pre-disbursement conditions may include an effective agreement, available budget, eligible recipient, complete documentation, no unresolved prohibitions, and an assessment of previous advances. The system should distinguish statuses such as prepared, under review, approved, rejected, held, and paid. Conditional approval should be used only when policy permits it, the conditions are specific, and the authorized officer has approved them. Changes to the amount, recipient, bank account, period, or purpose after approval should trigger a new review. Verbal approval must not be treated as final evidence unless an approved emergency procedure explicitly allows it and specifies subsequent documentation. Archive the approver's identity, date and time, request version, and decision rationale.

Study instruction:
- "Design an authorization matrix using approved limits and simulate a material change in the amount after approval."

Key point:
- Approval applies only to the transaction and version actually reviewed.
- Material changes require impact assessment and renewed authorization where appropriate.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
13. Q: How can the organization ensure that an advance is paid to the correct recipient in the approved amount?
A: Show answer
Before payment, Treasury should match the request reference, recipient identity, verified destination account, amount, currency, transfer purpose, and approval status. Changes to bank details must undergo an independent verification procedure defined by the organization to reduce fraud risk. Retain the payment instruction, bank authorization, date, transaction reference, actual value, and evidence of settlement. After payment, an independent officer should match the bank evidence with the payment register and advance record. Differences in amount, bank fees, partial transfers, incorrect accounts, or mismatched recipients must be flagged and resolved through the approved procedure. A transfer instruction does not necessarily prove that the recipient received the funds; rely on the actual settlement status available. If payment fails, do not mark the full advance as disbursed. Segregation between preparer, approver, and payment executor must match the organization's actual authorization matrix.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one advance from approval documents to the bank statement, matching the recipient, amount, currency, and settlement date."

Key point:
- Payment must be reconciled to the approval and actual transaction evidence.
- Changes to bank details or amounts require independent verification.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
14. Q: What should happen when a cash advance transfer fails, is partial, or remains unconfirmed?
A: Show answer
Failed, partial, and unconfirmed transfers must have distinct statuses in the register. When settlement is indeterminate, do not assume the funds were received or automatically repeat the transfer, because this could create a duplicate payment. Treasury should investigate with the bank, checking the reference, amount, account, and settlement status, and retain the results. For a partial payment, record the amount that actually succeeded and the amount still outstanding. A retry should occur only after the duplicate-payment risk has been resolved and the required authorization obtained. Reconcile differences among the approved amount, instructed transfer amount, and settled amount. If the bank or system cannot confirm settlement before an activity deadline, escalate to an authorized officer and document the operational decision. The source contains a fictional case involving an approved IDR 5,000,000 transfer whose settlement is uncertain; it must not be included as a confirmed cash outflow until evidence is available.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an unconfirmed transfer and identify the evidence needed before marking it complete or retrying it."

Key point:
- An unconfirmed transfer is not the same as a successful payment.
- Retrying requires independent investigation and duplicate-payment controls.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
15. Q: How should custodian controls ensure that cash advance funds are used only for authorized activities?
A: Show answer
After receiving the funds, the recipient should understand the purpose, permitted uses, activity period, restrictions on transferring funds, documentation requirements, reporting method, and liquidation deadline. For an internal custodian, the transfer of funds or change of custodian must be recorded so responsibility is not lost. Cash should be secured, counted, and reconciled against receipts and payments. Use of a personal account or wallet must be assessed under applicable policy and law; the SOP must not assume such an arrangement is permitted without verification. Recipients must not use advances for personal expenses or redirect funds to other activities without authorization. Lost funds, suspicious transactions, changes in purpose, and inability to provide evidence should be reported promptly. Monitoring may include confirmation of receipt, periodic checks, and expenditure sampling. Handover evidence and a balance confirmation are required when staff change or an activity is transferred.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a custody checklist covering receipt, security, authorized use, handover, and incident reporting."

Key point:
- Recipients are accountable for the funds until their use is substantiated.
- Redirecting funds requires an appropriate basis and authorization.

16. Q: What evidence is required to accept an expenditure submitted against a cash advance?
A: Show answer
Evidence must connect the expenditure to the transaction, advance recipient, project activity, date, amount, supplier or payee, and authorized purpose. Relevant documents may include valid invoices or receipts, payment evidence, contracts or purchase orders, goods-receipt records, service-delivery evidence, participant lists, activity reports, or other documents appropriate to the cost. Review documents for authenticity, legibility, consistency of amounts, party identity, date, and possible duplication. Payment evidence alone does not necessarily establish that goods or services were received or that the cost complies with donor requirements. If a receipt is missing or alternative evidence is proposed, follow the approved procedure for evaluating sufficiency and obtaining any required exception approval. Record why the evidence was accepted or rejected and who made the decision. Actual evidence requirements must come from applicable policies, agreements, and law; the fictional example does not establish binding standards for another organization.

Study instruction:
- "Test one expenditure sample by separating transaction evidence, payment evidence, receipt-of-goods-or-services evidence, and program eligibility evidence."

Key point:
- Evidence must demonstrate that the transaction occurred and met applicable requirements.
- Incomplete documentation requires a documented decision and follow-up.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
17. Q: How should duplicate transactions, fictitious claims, and suspected misuse of funds be identified?
A: Show answer
Testing should compare claims with receipts, invoices, transfer evidence, recipient, date, amount, description, project code, and transactions previously reported. A document number alone is insufficient because different suppliers may use similar numbering, and one transaction can appear under different references in different systems. Use combinations of attributes to identify potential duplicates, then inspect the source evidence and context. Suspected duplicate or unsupported claims should be held pending resolution. Do not automatically delete records or declare fraud solely because data appears similar. If there are indications of manipulation, conflicts of interest, forged receipts, or diversion of funds, follow the organization's investigation, reporting, and evidence-protection procedures. The claimant must not be the only person deciding whether their own claim is acceptable. Conclusions should distinguish administrative errors, missing evidence, ineligible claims, and suspected misconduct requiring formal investigation.

Study instruction:
- "Create test data containing one duplicate, one valid claim, and one suspicious claim, and ensure each produces a different follow-up action."

Key point:
- Potential duplicates require verification before rejection or removal.
- Suspected fraud must be handled through formal procedures with evidence preserved.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
18. Q: How should cash advance liquidation be calculated and independently reviewed?
A: Show answer
Liquidation begins by reconciling the amount actually received with submitted expenditure, accepted eligible costs, unsupported amounts, and refunds actually received. For each claim, the reviewer assesses eligibility, evidence, calculation accuracy, possible duplication, and allocation to the project and activity. Accepted expenditure must be supported and comply with the applicable requirements. The basic calculation is the unsettled advance balance: funds actually received minus accepted expenditure and confirmed refunds, subject to any other valid adjustments under the approved procedure. Ensure that amounts held for review and refunds still expected are not counted twice. The requester must not be the sole reviewer of their own calculation. Retain the transaction listing, amounts accepted or rejected, decision rationale, independent review evidence, dates, and related journals. Differences must be resolved or retained as open balances under the appropriate procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate one liquidation from the actual transfer through accepted expenses, held claims, and the amount refundable."

Key point:
- Only expenditure accepted on the basis of sufficient evidence reduces the advance balance.
- Refunds not yet received remain open items.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
19. Q: How should partially accepted expenditure be recorded and followed up?
A: Show answer
When some claims meet the requirements but others do not, do not automatically accept or reject the entire liquidation. Separate accepted costs, items requiring clarification, and rejected costs under the applicable rules. Record the reason for each category, such as missing documents, costs outside the approved budget, insufficient payment evidence, expenses outside the eligible period, or suspected duplication. Accepted costs may be processed under the accounting policy, while unsupported amounts remain outstanding until additional evidence, a refund, recovery, or another authorized resolution is obtained. Partial acceptance must not be treated as final settlement of the entire advance. Inform the recipient of the accepted amount and the evidence needed to resolve the remaining items. If the liquidation deadline has passed, include unresolved items in the aging register and escalate them. Reconcile the original claim total to all subsequent decisions to ensure that no amount has been omitted or counted twice.

Study instruction:
- "Take one mixed claim and document accepted, held, and rejected amounts, including their effects on the outstanding balance."

Key point:
- Partial acceptance does not settle the recipient's entire accountability obligation.
- Claim decisions must reconcile to the register and related journals.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
20. Q: How should unused cash advance funds be refunded and verified as received by the organization?
A: Show answer
After reviewing the liquidation, calculate the unused amount based on funds actually received and approved expenses. Unused funds must be refunded in accordance with the agreement and organizational policy. Retain the refund instruction, sender identity, receiving account, amount, currency, date, transaction reference, and evidence of receipt in the organization's bank account. Verify actual receipt and match it to the refund obligation. A promise or transfer instruction from the recipient is not proof that the refund is complete. If the amount received differs because of bank charges or exchange differences, investigate the cause and retain the difference as an open item until resolved. Allocate the refund to the correct advance so it does not reduce another recipient's or project's balance. After receipt, Finance updates the register, posts the appropriate entry, and reconciles the balance. Do not remove an outstanding balance before receipt is evidenced and the reconciliation has been reviewed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one refund from the repayment obligation through the organization's bank receipt, transaction allocation, and balance clearance."

Key point:
- A refund is complete only after actual receipt has been verified.
- The repayment must be linked to the correct advance and recipient.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
21. Q: How should overdue cash advances be monitored and recovered?
A: Show answer
Establish liquidation deadlines based on the applicable agreement and policy, then calculate the age of each outstanding balance from the relevant date. The aging register should show the recipient, advance reference, original amount, outstanding balance, due date, days overdue, known reason, communications, recovery owner, next action, and target date. Reminders should begin according to the internal schedule before the problem becomes material. If delays continue, escalate to management and the agreement owner, and consider restricting new advances where policy permits. For disputed balances, distinguish confirmed amounts from disputed amounts and document the basis for each. Writing off a balance must not be used merely to accelerate administrative closure; a write-off requires analysis, appropriate authority, evidence of recovery efforts, and proper accounting treatment. Verify applicable legal, contractual, donor, and organizational requirements before taking collection or recovery actions. Continue monitoring until the balance is paid, legally resolved, or formally decided under the approved procedure.

Study instruction:
- "Build an aging report and define triggers for reminders, escalation, restrictions on new advances, and write-off approval."

Key point:
- Overdue balances need an owner, deadlines, and evidence of recovery actions.
- A write-off does not replace collection efforts or formal approval.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Financial Instruments
22. Q: How should a cash advance be accounted for before the recipient's expenditure report is accepted?
A: Show answer
Based on the transaction's substance and the applicable reporting framework, an advance that has not yet been accounted for will generally be monitored as an advance asset or receivable rather than immediately charged to project expenses. Initial recognition and measurement must follow the organization's approved accounting policy and applicable requirements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual explains the accounting treatment for advances to implementing partners under its funding arrangements, but those arrangements and system processes are specific to the UN context and should not automatically be copied by another NGO. When expenditure satisfies the recognition criteria and the required evidence is accepted, the eligible portion may be reclassified from the advance account to an expense or another appropriate asset. Unsupported portions remain open. Amounts repayable may remain receivables or recovery balances, depending on the applicable policy and assessment. Verify donor agreements, funding conditions, entity characteristics, and the relevant accounting framework before determining the final treatment; cash movement alone does not establish the accounting outcome.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the initial disbursement entry with the accounting entry made after expenditure has been supported and approved."

Key point:
- An advance and an expense are different accounting categories.
- Final treatment depends on substance, evidence, policy, and the applicable accounting framework.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 5 — Expenses
23. Q: How should the journal entries for an advance disbursement, accepted expenses, and refunds be prepared in sequence?
A: Show answer
As a general illustration that must be adapted to the organization's accounting framework, an advance disbursement may be recorded by debiting an advance or related receivable account and crediting cash or bank. Once costs satisfy the recognition criteria and are approved, the appropriate expense or asset account is debited and the advance account is credited. A refund actually received may be recorded by debiting bank and crediting the related advance or receivable balance. If expenditure is only partially accepted, recognize only the authorized portion; do not eliminate unsupported balances through unsupported adjustments. Journal references should link to the transaction and its supporting documents. Check account codes, accounting period, project dimensions, donor dimensions, currency, amounts, and debit-credit equality. After posting, reconcile the journal to the advance register, general ledger, project report, and bank activity. Account codes, tax treatment, interface accounts, and recognition requirements are not universal and must be determined under approved organizational policies.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare illustrative journals for disbursement, accepted expenditure, and refund, then reconcile the advance balance after each step."

Key point:
- Journal entries must reflect the economic event and the available evidence.
- Illustrative journals do not replace an approved chart of accounts and accounting policy.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
24. Q: How should account codes, project codes, donor dimensions, cost centers, foreign currency, and tax be controlled for cash advances?
A: Show answer
Every advance and expenditure should use dimensions consistent with the organization's accounting structure and reporting needs. Project, activity, donor, funding source, cost center, recipient, and currency references should be established at the start of the process and checked again when expenditure is liquidated. Donor allocation must not be based solely on who provided the cash; the funding conditions and cost eligibility rules must be reviewed. For foreign-currency transactions, retain the original currency, applicable exchange rate, rate date or selection basis, converted amount, and any unresolved exchange difference. Tax treatment must be assessed based on transaction substance and applicable law rather than inferred from the account name. If a code, rate, or classification is uncertain, flag it for review. Master data and validation rules support consistency but do not independently determine accounting or legal treatment. Allocations must be reconciled to the source transaction total to prevent missing, overstated, or double-charged expenses.

Study instruction:
- "Test one cross-project or foreign-currency expenditure and document the basis for coding, exchange rate, tax, and donor allocation."

Key point:
- Codes and exchange rates must have a reproducible basis.
- Funding use and cost allocation must follow actual project, donor, and organizational requirements.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
25. Q: How should agreement amendments, activity cancellations, recipient changes, and reopening of closed balances be processed?
A: Show answer
Material changes to the amount, purpose, recipient, bank account, period, activity, or accountability requirements should trigger a review of the required approvals and accounting impacts. Confirm that the change is permitted by the contract and policy. Cancellation of an activity does not automatically extinguish the advance; calculate funds already used appropriately, disputed costs, and amounts to be refunded. Replacing the recipient requires a handover of funds, evidence of approval, updated identity information, and clear documentation of responsibility. If a previously closed balance is reopened because of new evidence or an error, do not overwrite the original record. Record the reason for reopening, value changed, reviewer, approval, any correction journal, and impact on reports already distributed. Closure and reopening should use controlled system statuses so the history remains traceable. If a change affects legal obligations or donor terms, obtain review by the appropriate function before implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate project cancellation after disbursement and identify the refund, accounting, approval, and archival requirements."

Key point:
- Changes do not remove accountability for funds already disbursed.
- The original balance and correction history must remain traceable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
26. Q: How should a cash advance register be designed to support monitoring, reconciliation, and audit?
A: Show answer
The register should have a unique identifier and display at least the request number, advance number, agreement, project, recipient, currency, approved amount, actual amount disbursed, transfer date, liquidation due date, expenditure submitted, expenditure accepted, claims held, refunds received, outstanding balance, aging, status, responsible officer, reviewer, and evidence location. References must be consistent with the general ledger, bank activity, journals, agreement documents, and activity reports. Statuses must not be changed merely to make the report appear complete. Material changes to values or statuses should retain the date and time, user, rationale, and required approval. The register should support filtering for overdue balances, unconfirmed transfers, unsupported claims, and balances ready for reconciliation. Compare record counts and total amounts before and after data consolidation to detect missing or duplicated transactions. The structure may be adapted to the organization's systems provided traceability and effective controls remain intact.

Study instruction:
- "Design a register with unique identifiers, standardized statuses, calculated balances, due dates, and evidence and journal references."

Key point:
- The register must connect operational status with accounting balances.
- Register completeness must be tested against source populations and actual evidence.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
27. Q: How should cash advances be reconciled at month-end to prevent misstatement of balances and project expenses?
A: Show answer
Month-end reconciliation should compare the advance register with the general ledger, relevant subsidiary records, bank activity, journals, and liquidation reports received and reviewed. Prepare a roll-forward showing opening balance, actual disbursements, approved expenditure, refunds actually received, valid corrections, and closing balance. Identify differences by cause, such as unconfirmed transfers, unposted journals, duplicate entries, incorrect classification, exchange-rate errors, claims awaiting review, or unidentified refunds. Do not include expenses that have merely been submitted but not approved as accepted expenditure just to clear a balance. Assign an owner, target date, and corrective action for each material difference. An independent reviewer should check calculations and evidence and ensure the reported balance is consistent with the applicable accounting framework. Unresolved reconciliations must remain visible as exceptions and be escalated according to risk.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a monthly advance roll-forward and trace every movement to bank evidence, journals, and claim decisions."

Key point:
- Every closing balance must be explained by the period's movements.
- Unapproved claims and unreceived refunds cannot be treated as final settlement.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
28. Q: When can a cash advance be considered settled if the project or agreement has ended?
A: Show answer
Completion of project activities or expiry of an agreement does not automatically establish that the advance has been settled. Before closing the project, Finance must confirm that all disbursements are recorded, liquidation reports have been reviewed, accepted expenditure has been posted, refunds have been received, and disputed claims or receivable balances have been resolved or carried forward under the appropriate status. Reconcile the register with the general ledger, bank activity, final budget, and project reports. Disputed, unsupported, unrecovered, or management-dependent balances must not be written off merely to achieve a closed status. Assign an owner to outstanding balances even if the original project officer is no longer active. Retain handover documents, settlement evidence, and closure approval. If a balance must carry into the next period, document its basis, amount, rationale, and action plan. Define closure in organizational policy, distinguishing activity completion, administrative closure, and accounting closure.

Study instruction:
- "Apply a project closure checklist and identify balances that prevent final closure."

Key point:
- Project completion does not prove that all advance obligations have been settled.
- Outstanding balances need a documented basis, owner, and resolution plan.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
29. Q: How should problems, suspected fraud, missing funds, or incomplete evidence be handled?
A: Show answer
The SOP should distinguish ordinary administrative errors, incomplete documentation, violations of funding requirements, and indications of fraud or misuse. Record the date, amount, relevant advance, responsible officer, chronology, initial evidence, risk level, and required follow-up. Protect original documents, restrict access to sensitive information, and do not change or delete source records while the issue is being reviewed. Material concerns must be escalated through the organization's approved reporting channels, following investigation procedures and applicable reporter protections. Further disbursements or acceptance of affected claims may be held where policy and delegated authority permit. Once the assessment is complete, document the conclusion, corrections, recovery, communications, and preventive controls to be updated. Formal investigations should be performed by competent and independent personnel where appropriate; staff involved in an incident must not control the conclusion about their own actions. Case closure requires evidence of the outcome and appropriate approval.

Study instruction:
- "Create an incident register covering issue classification, investigation owner, protected evidence, recovery actions, and closure approval."

Key point:
- Incidents require evidence, clear responsibility, and documented follow-up.
- Suspected misconduct requires escalation and independent review.

30. Q: How should cash advance records and audit trails be maintained for future review?
A: Show answer
Each advance should have an archival index linking the request, agreement, budget assessment, due diligence, approvals, transfer instruction, bank evidence, activity report, expenditure evidence, review, liquidation calculation, refund, journals, reconciliation, exception correspondence, and closure. Use consistent file naming and clear version identifiers. Do not overwrite final evidence with revised files without retaining the change history. Restrict access by role, apply retention periods under applicable law, contracts, donor terms, and organizational policy, and test backup and document recovery. System records should identify who created, changed, approved, or closed transactions where these features are available. Every correction should have a reason and a reference to the prior record. Personal identity and banking information must be protected through suitable access controls. The archive should enable a reviewer to locate the evidence supporting a figure without depending entirely on the memory or availability of the staff member who handled the transaction.

Study instruction:
- "Select one transaction and trace its evidence from the archive index through the bank record, journal, approval, and final decision."

Key point:
- Complete archives connect the process and balance to audit evidence.
- Retention, access, backup, and change history must be controlled.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
31. Q: How should workflow statuses and a decision matrix control the movement of cash advances through the process?
A: Show answer
A workflow should connect each stage with its entry criteria, required actions, responsible person, output evidence, and conditions for moving to the next stage. Statuses may include draft, awaiting documents, under review, pending approval, approved, awaiting transfer, transfer confirmed, awaiting liquidation, partial liquidation, recovery outstanding, held, and closed. Status names and codes should be standardized so users interpret them consistently. A decision matrix can specify outcomes for pass, fail, missing information, specialist review, or partial acceptance. The system must not mark a transaction complete merely because a form was submitted or a button was clicked. Status changes affecting balances, authority, or reporting require supporting evidence and a traceable approval history. Test the normal route, rejection, held status, failed transfer, partial liquidation, and reopening before the SOP is used.

Study instruction:
- "Design a status-transition table showing the conditions, owner, evidence, and exception route for each decision."

Key point:
- System statuses must reflect actual evidence and transaction conditions.
- Every transition requires clear criteria and authority.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
32. Q: Which indicators should management use to assess the effectiveness of project cash advance controls?
A: Show answer
Relevant indicators include the number and value of active advances, percentage of liquidations completed on time, value and age of overdue balances, number of claims held, average settlement time, failed transfers, refunds not yet received, reconciliation differences, and recurring corrections. Every indicator should have a definition, data source, calculation method, frequency, owner, target, and escalation threshold. For example, the on-time settlement rate should use all relevant obligations as the denominator, not only advances already completed. Dashboards must distinguish amounts submitted from amounts accepted and refunds promised from refunds actually confirmed. Results should be compared with the register, general ledger, and bank evidence. Deteriorating trends should trigger root-cause analysis and corrective action rather than simply being reported to management.

Study instruction:
- "Design an aging, status, recovery, and on-time settlement dashboard, then verify its results against the source register."

Key point:
- KPIs must be reproducible from reliable data.
- Accurate dashboards should prompt action on unresolved balances and risks.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
33. Q: How can Excel and Power Query support a cash advance register without replacing accounting controls?
A: Show answer
Excel can support structured registers, validation lists for projects and recipients, balance calculations, due-date schedules, and exception reports. Power Query can combine data from payment registers, bank records, liquidation reports, and general ledger extracts through repeatable transformations. Use stable transaction identifiers, consistent data types, duplicate checks, date and currency validation, and control totals before and after processing. Automated reports should distinguish submitted claims from accepted expenses and planned payments from confirmed transfers. A formula or query must not independently determine contractual eligibility or expense recognition. Changes to calculation logic require an owner, rationale, approval, testing against independently calculated samples, and a reproducible version. Reconcile final outputs to official records before using them for journal posting or reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Build a test register containing advances, claims, and refunds, then validate the automated balances against an independent manual calculation."

Key point:
- Automation speeds up processing and highlights exceptions but does not determine accounting validity.
- Query and formula changes must be tested and traceable.

34. Q: How should the staff advance example of IDR 12,000,000 be calculated and verified?
A: Show answer
In the fictional example in the source document, a staff advance of IDR 12,000,000 was transferred. Accepted expenses comprise IDR 4,200,000 for learning supplies, IDR 2,400,000 for workshop materials and venue costs, and IDR 1,650,000 for approved activity transport. Total accepted expenditure is IDR 8,250,000. The refund due is therefore IDR 12,000,000 minus IDR 8,250,000, or IDR 3,750,000. The example records receipt of the IDR 3,750,000 refund on 12 October 2026, resulting in a zero balance within that fictional scenario. In a real case, Finance would still need to verify the original transfer, eligibility of each expense, refund receipt in the bank, journal entries, and reconciliation. The example's amounts and dates do not establish an actual organizational transaction or mandatory policy. If any expense is later rejected, the balance and closure decision must be recalculated.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the three example expenses, the refund, and the closing balance; distinguish arithmetic verification from supporting-evidence verification."

Key point:
- IDR 12,000,000 minus IDR 8,250,000 produces a refund due of IDR 3,750,000.
- A zero balance is valid only after the actual refund and all related entries are reconciled.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
35. Q: How should an external partner advance of IDR 18,000,000 be handled when only part of the expenditure is accepted and liquidation is overdue?
A: Show answer
In the source's fictional scenario, a partner advance of IDR 18,000,000 has two accepted expenditure amounts: IDR 10,000,000 and IDR 5,500,000, giving total accepted expenditure of IDR 15,500,000. An additional IDR 1,000,000 claim has not been accepted because duplication is suspected or supporting evidence is inadequate. Based on accepted expenses, the outstanding balance is IDR 18,000,000 minus IDR 15,500,000, or IDR 2,500,000. The document separates that balance into IDR 1,000,000 of unsupported expenditure and IDR 1,500,000 of unspent or unconfirmed funds. No refund has been received, and the fictional example describes a three-day delay. The balance must therefore remain open. Finance should keep the disputed claim in the appropriate status, request additional evidence, monitor refund or recovery, and escalate the overdue position to the agreement owner. If a similar situation occurs in practice, the IDR 2,500,000 balance must be reconciled to the actual register, journals, and supporting documents.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the partner balance and explain the difference between accepted expenditure, unsupported claims, and unspent funds."

Key point:
- The held IDR 1,000,000 claim does not reduce the balance already accounted for.
- An unreceived refund means the recovery balance remains outstanding.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Funding Arrangements and Expense Recognition
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
36. Q: How should test cases involving unconfirmed transfers, foreign-currency transactions, and suspected duplicates be treated in a control report?
A: Show answer
The source document contains three test cases that must not be mixed with verified, clean transactions. First, an approved IDR 5,000,000 transfer with unconfirmed settlement must remain indeterminate or on hold until bank tracing is complete. Second, a USD 500 claim with no confirmed exchange rate and tax treatment awaiting specialist review must remain under review and must not be added to the final accepted IDR expenditure total. Third, a suspected duplicate of IDR 2,400,000 must be held for investigation and not counted as accepted expenditure merely because it exists in the test data. Clearly separate verified operational records, unresolved exceptions, and synthetic test transactions. Control totals should include only transactions meeting the defined acceptance criteria. If an exception is resolved, retain the decision basis, final amount, evidence, and date of change so that the result can be reproduced and audited.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a control report containing all three test cases and ensure none enters the final total until the relevant conditions have been satisfied."

Key point:
- Unconfirmed transfers, specialist reviews, and suspected duplicates must remain visible as exceptions.
- Test data must not be mistaken for actual transactions or accepted expenditure.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
37. Q: How should a cash advance SOP be validated, approved, and updated before organizational implementation?
A: Show answer
The SOP should be tested against completed real transactions, not just the ideal process described in the document. A walkthrough should examine the agreement, recipient, budget, approvals, actual transfer, expense evidence, liquidation calculation, refund, journals, register, and closure. It should also test exception routes involving unconfirmed transfers, missing documents, overdue balances, and partial claims. Record every finding with evidence, risk, corrective action, owner, target date, and approving authority. The example identifies several adoption gates that remain unverified, including entity identity, accounting framework, governing sources, delegated authority, and independent validation. Check the consistency of forms, workflow IDs, decision matrices, status transitions, and test results. Following review, the policy owner should approve the version, effective date, training, and periodic review arrangements. Existing procedures must not be informally replaced before the revised version is formally authorized.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an SOP adoption checklist and document the walkthrough evidence, test results, approvals, training, and effective date."

Key point:
- Completing a document does not mean it has been approved or is ready for operational use.
- Adoption requires verification, authorization, testing, and evidence of implementation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 17 — Accounting Policies, Changes in Estimates and Errors
38. Q: How should arithmetic validity, document validity, and control effectiveness be distinguished when evaluating cash advances?
A: Show answer
These are three different forms of testing. Arithmetic validity confirms that additions, deductions, and balances are calculated correctly. Document validity checks whether agreements, approvals, transfer evidence, expenditure reports, and refunds actually exist and support the conclusions. Control effectiveness assesses whether approved procedures were performed by the right people, at the right time, with adequate evidence. Correct arithmetic alone does not prove that costs comply with donor requirements or that payment was properly authorized. Similarly, the existence of completed forms does not prove that controls were actually performed. Testing should connect calculations to source documents, system records, approvals, and independent confirmations. If the documents contain only synthetic data, a successful calculation must not be described as proof of operational effectiveness. Evaluation reports should distinguish calculation results, documentation sufficiency, control execution, and policy-adoption status so that users do not draw conclusions beyond the available evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Classify findings as arithmetic errors, missing evidence, control failures, or limitations arising from synthetic data."

Key point:
- Correct calculations do not automatically establish transaction validity or effective controls.
- Conclusions must be limited to the quality and scope of the evidence tested.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures

Table 5
Staff Travel Advances, Per Diem and Expense Settlement
1. Q: How should an integrated staff travel advance, per diem and travel expense settlement process operate from initial request through final accounting reconciliation?
A: Show answer
The process should begin with a documented business purpose, an approved trip request, a confirmed budget and project code, and a review of the applicable travel policy. The organization then authorizes bookings, calculates any advance or per diem entitlement, verifies payment instructions, records the advance, and retains the authorization. After the trip, the traveller submits a travel expense report, itinerary, receipts where required, and evidence of the actual travel undertaken. Finance checks eligibility, dates, amounts, advance utilization, project allocation and supporting evidence. An independent approver resolves exceptions before the settlement is posted. Any additional amount due to the traveller is reimbursed, while an unused advance or overpayment is recovered through an approved process. Finally, Finance reconciles the advance register to the general ledger, closes the individual balance, updates the budget and donor reports, and archives the evidence. The supplied DOCX could not be retrieved in this session; therefore, this resource is a topic-based reference and its requirements must be checked against the original document and the organization's approved policies before adoption.

Study instruction:
- "Map one complete trip from request and payment through expense verification, settlement, journal posting and closure of the advance balance."

Key point:
- Every trip must have a traceable authorization, payment, expense claim and final settlement.
- An advance is not automatically an expense when cash is disbursed.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel Management
2. Q: What is the purpose of a staff travel advance and how should it be distinguished from a travel expense?
A: Show answer
A travel advance provides an employee with funds before an authorized business trip so that the employee does not have to finance all approved travel costs personally. An advance is a temporary balance to be accounted for after the trip, whereas a travel expense represents a cost incurred in conducting organizational activities. Under an accrual-based accounting framework, the initial payment is generally recorded as an advance or receivable from the traveller, subject to the applicable chart of accounts and policy. When eligible expenses are substantiated and approved, the relevant amount is charged to expense and the advance balance is cleared. The accounting entry should not recognize the entire advance as an expense merely because money has left the bank. The policy should distinguish cash advances, prepaid tickets, accommodation paid directly by the organization, per diem payments, and reimbursements because they may require different evidence and posting procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an advance payment, a supplier prepayment and an approved travel expense, identifying the accounting and settlement consequences of each."

Key point:
- Cash disbursement and expense recognition are different accounting events.
- The applicable accounting framework and chart of accounts determine the final treatment.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel advances and expense reporting
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable, including staff advances
3. Q: Which activities and personnel should be covered by a staff travel advance and settlement SOP?
A: Show answer
The SOP should define the eligible personnel, travel purposes, organizational entities, locations and transactions to which it applies. Depending on the organization's approved rules, users may include employees, temporary personnel, consultants or other authorized travellers. Eligibility should be determined by employment or contractual terms, the business purpose, delegated authority, donor conditions and applicable law. The scope should cover domestic and international travel, overnight and day trips where relevant, official meetings, field visits, training, emergency missions and other approved activities. It should also identify excluded travel, personal extensions, commuting, family travel and costs paid directly by the organization. Where different personnel categories have different entitlements, use separate approved rules rather than assuming that one staff policy applies to everyone. Exceptions must be authorized before commitments are made whenever practicable.

Study instruction:
- "Build an eligibility matrix showing personnel categories, travel purposes, permitted funding sources, applicable policies and approval authorities."

Key point:
- Eligibility must be established before an advance or reimbursement is approved.
- Contractual, donor and statutory requirements may require different treatment.

Source links:
- United Nations Policy Portal — Travel and relocation expenses
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel Management
4. Q: What is per diem and how does it differ from reimbursement of actual travel expenses?
A: Show answer
Per diem is a daily allowance determined under an applicable travel policy, commonly intended to cover specified categories such as meals, accommodation and incidental expenses. Actual-cost reimbursement pays eligible expenditures supported by the evidence required under the policy. Some organizations use a mixed arrangement, such as a daily allowance for meals and actual-cost reimbursement for airfare and accommodation. The SOP must state which categories are covered by the allowance and which may be claimed separately. A traveller must not claim an expense separately when it has already been covered by an allowance, unless the policy expressly permits the additional payment. An allowance is not automatically a universally applicable tax-free payment, and the correct accounting, payroll and tax treatment depends on local law, the nature of the payment and the organization's circumstances. The applicable per diem schedule, currency and effective date must be documented and approved.

Study instruction:
- "Compare an actual-cost trip claim with a per diem claim and identify the evidence, calculation method and duplicate-payment risks for each."

Key point:
- Per diem and actual-cost reimbursement are different payment methods with different controls.
- The governing policy must define which expenses each method covers.

Source links:
- United Nations Policy Portal — Travel entitlements and daily subsistence allowance
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel Management
5. Q: Who should prepare, review, approve, pay and reconcile travel advances and expense settlements?
A: Show answer
Responsibilities should be separated among the traveller, requesting manager, budget holder, travel coordinator, Finance preparer, independent reviewer, authorized approver, payment operator and ledger reconciler. The traveller provides the business purpose, itinerary, declaration of expenses and supporting evidence. The manager confirms that the trip was necessary and completed. The budget holder verifies available funding and appropriate project coding. Finance tests eligibility, calculations, supporting documents and the outstanding advance. An authorized approver resolves exceptions, while an appropriately authorized payment operator releases funds. An independent accountant or reviewer reconciles the transactions and closes the balance. In a small NGO, full separation may not be practical; documented compensating controls, such as independent review of source documents and bank transactions, should address the resulting risks. No employee should approve their own unsupported settlement or alter a reconciled balance without an authorized correction and audit trail.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a responsibility matrix for every stage and test whether any individual can request, approve, pay and reconcile the same transaction without independent oversight."

Key point:
- Clear responsibilities reduce errors and unauthorized payments.
- Small teams need documented compensating controls when full segregation is impracticable.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel roles and approval workflows
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and financial controls
6. Q: What information should a travel request contain before an advance is authorized?
A: Show answer
A request should identify the traveller, organizational unit, purpose, destination, approved travel dates, itinerary, expected costs, funding source, project or grant code and approving authority. It should indicate whether airfare, accommodation, registration, local transport or other services will be paid directly by the organization. The request should separately identify the estimated per diem, cash advance and any other expected reimbursement. The budget owner should verify that the proposed costs are eligible, affordable and consistent with the donor agreement or internal travel policy. Finance should check whether the traveller has overdue advances or another open claim requiring resolution. Material changes to the route, duration, destination or estimated cost should trigger revised approval when required. The authorization must be retained with a unique trip reference so the subsequent payment, expense report and settlement can be linked to the same trip.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a sample travel request and confirm that its dates, purpose, cost estimate, funding allocation and approval agree with the supporting records."

Key point:
- An advance must be supported by an approved trip and an identifiable funding source.
- Changes to authorized travel should be controlled and traceable.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 6 — Travel requests and advance processing
7. Q: How should the organization estimate the appropriate travel advance amount?
A: Show answer
Calculate the requested amount from the approved itinerary and the applicable entitlement or reimbursement rules. Depending on policy, the estimate may include eligible per diem, expected local transport, authorized incidentals and costs that the traveller must pay directly. Deduct expenses the organization will pay directly, allowances already provided and other amounts that should not be advanced twice. Use the approved destination-specific rates and the number of eligible travel days rather than an unsupported flat estimate. Record the assumptions, source of each rate, currency, calculation date and any adjustments for meals or accommodation provided by a host. The amount should remain within the delegated approval limit. If material uncertainty exists, the advance can be limited to the supported estimate while exceptional requirements are reviewed separately. The estimate must not be presented as the final expense claim.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate an advance from an approved itinerary and verify each day, covered expense category, deduction and source rate."

Key point:
- Advance amounts must be calculated from an approved itinerary and applicable rules.
- Estimated costs must remain distinguishable from actual eligible expenditure.

8. Q: How should per diem rates and eligible travel days be determined?
A: Show answer
The calculation should follow the organization's formally approved per diem policy and rate schedule. Relevant factors may include destination, travel dates, length of stay, overnight requirements, the departure and return days, accommodation arrangements, meals supplied by the host and the purpose of travel. The SOP should specify the rule for partial days and any permitted reductions instead of allowing each reviewer to apply personal judgment. Rates must be checked against the approved schedule effective for the travel period; the rate currently displayed on a website may not be the rate applicable to an earlier trip. A UN daily subsistence allowance or another institution's published schedule should not automatically be applied to an independent NGO. If rates change, retain the prior version, effective dates, approval and calculation evidence for claims already being processed.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate per diem for a trip spanning multiple dates and destinations using the organization's approved rates and partial-day rules."

Key point:
- The approved policy determines the eligible days and rates.
- External per diem schedules are not automatically binding on an NGO.

9. Q: How should meals, accommodation or other services supplied by a donor, host or conference organizer affect per diem?
A: Show answer
First determine what the approved allowance covers and whether the policy requires a reduction when meals or accommodation are supplied without charge. Confirm the specific service provided, the relevant date, the person who paid for it and whether the cost was already included in the allowance. Apply only the reduction required by the governing policy. The adjustment should be visible in the calculation rather than hidden in a manually reduced total. A conference registration fee that includes lunch, for example, may affect a meal allowance if the applicable rules require it; it does not automatically justify deducting every meal component. Likewise, accommodation paid directly by the NGO should not also be reimbursed to the traveller. Keep the conference agenda, booking details, invoices or other evidence needed to substantiate the adjustment.

Study instruction:
- "Review a trip with host-provided meals and accommodation and calculate any required adjustment under the actual policy."

Key point:
- Covered and externally provided services must be checked to prevent overpayment.
- Deductions must follow the governing rule, not an arbitrary percentage.

10. Q: Which travel costs should be eligible for reimbursement and which should be excluded or treated as exceptions?
A: Show answer
Eligible costs should be defined by policy, business purpose and funding conditions. They may include authorized transportation, accommodation, registration fees, local business travel and necessary incidental expenditure. Personal entertainment, unsupported purchases, unauthorized route deviations, duplicate claims, expenses for unrelated personal activities and costs prohibited by donor terms should normally be excluded unless an applicable exception permits them. The review should distinguish between an expense that is genuinely ineligible and one that is eligible but lacks documentation. The first may require rejection or recovery; the second may require additional evidence or an approved exception under policy. Finance should document the reason for every deduction or rejection, identify the relevant rule, notify the traveller and preserve the decision. It should not create its own eligibility limits where the governing policy is silent without obtaining an authorized policy decision.

Study instruction:
- "Classify a sample of travel transactions as eligible, ineligible or pending evidence and document the policy basis for each decision."

Key point:
- Eligibility depends on the governing policy and the purpose of expenditure.
- Rejections and exceptions should have documented reasons and consistent treatment.

11. Q: Which supporting documents should accompany an expense report or travel settlement?
A: Show answer
The evidence package should normally include the approved trip request, itinerary, travel dates, expense report, per diem calculation where applicable, available receipts, supplier invoices, booking records and proof of payment where required. Additional evidence may include a meeting agenda, attendance record, mission report, conference registration, explanation of route changes or confirmation of host-provided services. Not every allowance necessarily requires an itemized receipt for every meal; the applicable policy determines the required substantiation. The expense report should identify the transaction or receipt reference, expenditure date, category, currency, amount, business purpose and project allocation. Missing evidence should be logged separately with an owner, due date and resolution. A payment record alone does not prove that an expenditure was authorized, eligible or incurred for organizational purposes.

Study instruction:
