- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash Fund Procedures
14. Q: How should voucher numbering, voids, corrections, and transaction histories be controlled?
A: Show answer
Use a controlled numbering method that provides a unique identifier for each movement and allows the sequence to be reviewed. Each voucher should be linked to its fund, transaction, approval, evidence, custodian, and accounting reference. Voided documents, missing numbers, replaced vouchers, and corrections must remain traceable to the original record. The preparer should document the reason, date, affected values, supporting evidence, and approval for every correction. Do not erase a transaction history, fabricate a missing voucher, backdate an approval, or overwrite a final record without preserving the original state. A correction should be distinguishable from a new payment and from a reversal. If the system does not maintain sufficient change history, the organization should introduce a controlled register or independent evidence trail before relying on it for material cash accountability.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a voucher sequence containing a void, a correction, and a missing number, and determine whether every event remains traceable."
Key point:
- Corrections must preserve both the original record and the reason for change.
- Sequence gaps and duplicate identifiers require investigation rather than silent repair.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
15. Q: How should cash held in an imprest fund be distinguished from expense recognition?
A: Show answer
Physical cash held by an authorized custodian represents cash under organizational control, subject to the applicable reporting framework and the organization's actual circumstances. Establishing or replenishing a cash fund does not, by itself, mean that the same amount is a new expense. When cash is disbursed, the transaction must be recorded according to its substance: a qualifying purchase may create an expense or asset, while a temporary advance generally remains an accountable balance until eligible expenditure or another authorized settlement event is established. Cash accountability records should therefore be linked to, but not confused with, the general ledger classification. The applicable accounting framework and approved accounting manual determine recognition, measurement, presentation, and any required disclosures. The UN Finance Manual's cash account examples are useful operational references for UN procedures, not automatic account-code instructions for another NGO.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the accounting impact of establishing a cash fund, purchasing supplies, and issuing a temporary advance."
Key point:
- A cash movement does not automatically create an expense.
- Recognition and classification must follow the applicable accounting framework and transaction substance.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash and Cash Equivalents
16. Q: How is a fixed imprest fund reconciled at a reporting cutoff?
A: Show answer
Reconcile the authorized float to the physical cash counted, eligible supported approved vouchers not yet replenished, and any permitted timing items that satisfy the organization's approved criteria. The working equation is: physical cash plus eligible unreplenished vouchers plus explicitly permitted supported timing items equals the authorized float, adjusted only for formally approved float changes. Each component must be stated separately and reconciled in its own currency. Do not count a voucher as both unreplenished expenditure and physical cash. Compare the result with the cash log, prior reconciliation, replenishment history, general ledger, and approved fund master. Any unexplained difference must remain an exception and should prevent replenishment or certification until investigated. This equation is a proposed control model; the adopted accounting framework and internal policy must confirm the actual procedure before operational use.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one fixed imprest from an independent physical count to the voucher population, authorized float, and ledger balance."
Key point:
- Reconciliation must account for physical cash and eligible outstanding evidence without double-counting.
- Unexplained differences cannot be treated as routine reconciling items.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
17. Q: Which vouchers are eligible for fixed imprest replenishment?
A: Show answer
Replenishment should include only expenditure vouchers that meet the approved eligibility, evidence, authorization, cutoff, coding, and review requirements and that have not previously been replenished. Exclude duplicate claims, unsupported amounts, voided items, items outside the fund's permitted purpose, transactions awaiting material approvals, and claims that have already been reimbursed or posted as replenished under the controlled process. The preparer should produce a schedule showing each voucher identifier, date, payee, purpose, amount, currency, supporting reference, approval status, previous replenishment status, proposed accounting code, and eligibility conclusion. An independent reviewer should verify the population and total before payment is requested. Replenishment should restore the authorized float as permitted by policy, not automatically equal every voucher submitted by the custodian.

Study instruction:
- "Test a proposed replenishment schedule and identify ineligible, duplicate, previously reimbursed, and insufficiently supported vouchers."
Key point:
- Replenishment is limited to approved eligible unreplenished expenditure.
- The voucher population and replenishment history must be checked before payment.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash and Imprest Account Procedures
18. Q: When may a timing item be included in an imprest reconciliation?
A: Show answer
A timing item may be included only when an adopted policy expressly permits it and the item is independently supported. The record should identify the responsible owner, source, amount, currency, transaction date, dispatch or receipt evidence, expected resolution date, and independent confirmation. The reviewer must establish that the item represents a genuine timing difference rather than an unsupported amount or missing cash. A payment instruction, expected transfer, or verbal explanation does not automatically establish that the money is physically present. Timing items should be monitored to resolution, with aging and escalation when the expected date passes. They must not be used to conceal theft, shortage, duplicate reimbursement, or inaccurate bookkeeping. If no approved policy permits the item, it must remain an open exception rather than being inserted into the reconciliation equation.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed timing item and verify the policy authority, dispatch evidence, independent confirmation, and expected settlement date."
Key point:
- Timing items require explicit policy permission and independent support.
- They are not equivalent to physical cash or a substitute for evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
19. Q: What should Finance do when an imprest reconciliation identifies a shortage or surplus?
A: Show answer
Record the actual physical count, reconcile it to the cutoff cash log and supporting vouchers, and quantify the difference in the relevant currency. The custodian should provide an explanation and supporting evidence, but should not be permitted to clear the difference through an unsupported journal, substitute voucher, or informal IOU. The independent reviewer should identify possible causes, such as omitted transactions, double counting, arithmetic errors, unauthorized payments, cash loss, or unrecorded receipts. Escalate potential theft, fraud, safety incidents, or material irregularities through the designated route. Keep the discrepancy open with a responsible owner, deadline, containment action, required approval, and independent retest. Replenishment and final certification should remain on hold when the unresolved difference affects accountability. Any accounting adjustment must follow the approved framework and documented authorization.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate a cash count difference from original evidence through root-cause analysis, approved correction, and independent retesting."
Key point:
- A discrepancy requires investigation and an authorized disposition, not a balancing entry without evidence.
- Material unresolved differences must remain open and escalated.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
20. Q: What steps should the fixed imprest replenishment process follow?
A: Show answer
At the approved trigger or cutoff, the custodian closes the movement log and submits the voucher population with supporting evidence. The preparer checks completeness, eligibility, duplicate and prior-reimbursement status, coding, arithmetic, and fund limits. An independent reviewer reconciles the physical count and documentation to the authorized float and identifies any unexplained differences. The authorized approver confirms the eligible replenishment amount. Treasury or the designated payment function processes the authorized payment, and the custodian acknowledges receipt and updates the cash log. Finance records the payment and related expense or asset entries under the applicable accounting policy, then reconciles the general ledger and fund balance. Every stage should have an owner, status, date, evidence reference, and escalation path. A failed control should prevent release until the issue is resolved or an explicitly permitted exception is approved.

Study instruction:
- "Map each replenishment stage to its owner, prerequisite evidence, approval, accounting entry, and completion criterion."
Key point:
- Preparation, review, approval, payment, posting, and reconciliation are distinct steps.
- The replenishment process must preserve an auditable record of each stage.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash and Imprest Account Procedures
21. Q: How should a temporary field-site cash advance be requested and issued?
A: Show answer
The request should identify a named recipient, approved purpose, amount, currency, activity dates, settlement deadline, project or funding source, and expected expenditure categories. The budget owner verifies operational need, funding availability, procurement requirements, and donor conditions. The approver confirms the correct delegation, applicable limits, security arrangements, and whether a non-cash alternative is feasible. Before payment, Finance checks whether the recipient has overdue advances or unresolved items that affect eligibility under policy. The disbursement must be recorded under a unique AdvanceID and linked to the request, approval, recipient acknowledgement, and cash movement. The recipient must understand the use restriction and required evidence before accepting the money. Issuance of the advance does not, by itself, prove that the intended expenses have occurred or that the activity has been completed.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a field-site advance from request through approval, issuance, acknowledgement, and entry in the advance register."
Key point:
- Advances must be issued to named recipients for approved purposes and amounts.
- The recipient's accountability begins when the cash is issued and accepted.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and Advance Procedures
22. Q: Why should a temporary cash advance remain an accountable balance until expenditure is substantiated?
A: Show answer
An advance is a transfer of cash to a person for later use and accounting, not automatic evidence that the organization has incurred an expense. Under the applicable accounting framework and approved policy, the amount will generally be tracked as an advance or receivable until eligible expenditure is accepted, unused cash is returned, or another authorized disposition is established. Finance should maintain the recipient, date issued, purpose, original amount and currency, approved deadline, eligible expenditure, cash returned, balance outstanding, and supporting evidence. When settlement is accepted, record the expense or asset classification appropriate to the underlying transaction and clear the advance account to the extent justified. Unexplained residuals must remain open or on an approved hold. Do not clear the balance automatically because a deadline passed, a project closed, or a recipient verbally confirmed that the money was spent.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the accounting consequences of issuing an advance, accepting eligible receipts, returning cash, and retaining an unresolved residual."
Key point:
- Cash issued is not the same as expenditure substantiated.
- Advance clearance requires evidence and an authorized settlement decision.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance and Payment Procedures
23. Q: How should an advance settlement be reconciled from issuance to final closure?
A: Show answer
The settlement must account for the original amount issued by reconciling it to accepted eligible expenditure, cash returned, explicitly authorized and classified adjustments, and any unresolved residual. These components should be recorded separately in the original currency and linked to receipts, transaction evidence, approval, and the return-of-cash acknowledgement. The reviewer should verify arithmetic, duplicate claims, purpose compliance, donor and budget eligibility, cutoff, tax classification, and the absence of prior reimbursement. If the equation does not balance, identify the cause rather than forcing the difference into expense. The advance may be closed only when the unexplained residual is zero or an authorized, documented disposition explicitly permits the remaining item to stay open or on hold. Do not automatically reimburse a shortfall, roll a balance into another advance, deduct it from payroll, or write it off without the applicable authority and evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a full settlement worksheet and verify that the amount issued equals supported spend, returned cash, approved adjustments, and any identified residual."
Key point:
- The settlement equation must reconcile in the relevant currency.
- Closure requires an evidenced disposition of every remaining amount.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance and Payment Procedures
24. Q: What happens when eligible expenditure is equal to, greater than, or less than a field-site advance?
A: Show answer
If expenditure equals the advance, an accepted settlement may clear the accountable balance without a cash refund or additional reimbursement. If expenditure is less than the advance, the recipient returns the unused cash and Finance records the accepted eligible expenditure separately from the refund. If eligible expenditure is greater than the advance, a further reimbursement may be considered only after the excess cost is properly documented, eligible, approved, and processed under the applicable payment rules. For example, the supplied model includes a fictional advance of IDR 1,500,000, accepted eligible expenditure of IDR 1,125,000, and returned cash of IDR 375,000. The arithmetic balances, but the figures are illustrative and do not establish an actual cash limit, accounting policy, or authorization. Each case also requires evidence review and the appropriate accounting entries.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate settlement outcomes for equal, lower, and higher expenditure and identify the evidence and approvals needed for each case."
Key point:
- Unused cash must be returned and excess eligible costs separately reviewed.
- Illustrative amounts are not approved organizational parameters.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance and Payment Procedures
25. Q: How should overdue field-site advances be monitored and escalated?
A: Show answer
Maintain an aging register with the AdvanceID, recipient, site, amount and currency, issue date, approved due date, unsettled balance, last contact, settlement status, and next action. Define reminders and escalation thresholds according to the adopted policy and the type of activity. At each reporting cutoff, identify overdue amounts, broken commitments, missing documents, repeat extensions, and recipients with multiple open advances. The responsible owner should establish why settlement is delayed, whether the activity is still active, what evidence is available, and what action can reasonably resolve the issue. Any restriction on new advances must follow approved rules and be applied consistently. Employment consequences, recovery from salary, or presumed personal liability require appropriate legal, contractual, and HR review; they should never be assumed from the existence of an outstanding balance alone.

Study instruction:
- "Age outstanding advances, assign a responsible owner and deadline, and distinguish an overdue administrative item from an item requiring legal or fraud escalation."
Key point:
- Aging supports prioritization and timely settlement follow-up.
- Recovery decisions must follow applicable law and approved procedures.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance and Payment Procedures
26. Q: Under what circumstances may a field-site advance be extended or remain open beyond its original deadline?
A: Show answer
An extension should be considered only when the activity and remaining accountability are supported and an authorized decision is made before the original deadline, where practicable. The record should state the reason, remaining amount, revised settlement date, continuing business need, funding and donor implications, security circumstances, and responsible approver. The extension must be prospective, limited in scope, and recorded against the original advance; it must not conceal an overdue balance or create an informal rollover into a new advance. A new advance should have its own authorization and tracking identity. If the governing policy does not permit an extension or the relevant authority is unavailable, the item should remain on hold or be escalated. Repeated extensions should prompt root-cause review rather than automatic approval.

Study instruction:
- "Review an extension request and determine whether the timing, amount, authority, rationale, and revised accountability are adequately documented."
Key point:
- An extension changes the approved timeline but does not remove accountability.
- Informal rollover and retrospective approval are not acceptable substitutes for controlled authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance Procedures
27. Q: How should missing receipts, incomplete invoices, or unsupported expenditure be handled?
A: Show answer
Record the missing evidence as a formal exception and identify the transaction, recipient, date, amount, currency, purpose, and original supporting documents available. Ask the recipient to explain what is missing and seek independently corroborating evidence, such as supplier confirmation, proof of delivery, payment records, activity logs, or an approved substitute-document process. Screen the claim for duplicates and test whether the expenditure was genuinely incurred, eligible, authorized, and correctly classified. A substitute document should never be fabricated, backdated, or presented as an original receipt. An authorized independent reviewer must approve any permitted exception under the actual policy, while unresolved tax, donor, or statutory questions remain separately identified. The claim must not be silently treated as eligible expense or cleared solely because the field activity has ended.

Study instruction:
- "Assess a missing-receipt case and determine which facts can be corroborated, what substitute evidence is allowed, and who must authorize the disposition."
Key point:
- Missing evidence requires documented investigation and authorized treatment.
- A substitute-evidence process must not weaken statutory, donor, or fraud controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash and Payment Procedures
28. Q: How should donor, grant, budget, and procurement requirements be checked for cash expenditure?
A: Show answer
Before payment, confirm that the planned expenditure is consistent with the approved project budget, activity purpose, donor agreement, procurement rules, eligible spending period, and required approval levels. Identify restrictions concerning permitted categories, documentation, purchasing method, supplier selection, tax treatment, audit access, refund obligations, or reporting. A grant name or project code does not prove that the cost is eligible. Keep the donor eligibility assessment distinct from the accounting decision about whether a cost is an expense, asset, advance, or another balance. Where an agreement is missing, amended, or unclear, refer the matter to the grants or legal owner before treating the requirement as resolved. Record the applicable clause, decision-maker, evidence, and any special condition in the transaction file.

Study instruction:
- "Test sample payments against the executed donor agreement, approved budget, procurement requirements, and evidence of the funded activity."
Key point:
- Cash payment authorization does not automatically establish donor eligibility.
- Contractual and budget restrictions must be checked against the actual transaction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and Payment Procedures
29. Q: How should tax and general-ledger coding be controlled for imprest transactions and field advances?
A: Show answer
Use a controlled coding matrix that reflects the approved chart of accounts, project and cost-centre dimensions, nature of the transaction, applicable tax requirements, and reporting needs. Determine the accounting treatment from the underlying goods or services and applicable accounting policy, rather than from the payment method alone. Tax treatment should be assessed using the recipient's status, transaction substance, current local requirements, and relevant supporting documents. A transaction with uncertain tax treatment should be identified for specialist review before final classification or reporting. The posting must reconcile with the voucher or settlement worksheet, cash movement record, and general ledger. Operational cash records do not independently establish the recognition of expenditure, tax recoverability, donor eligibility, or the proper period for reporting.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a cash-paid purchase from invoice and tax assessment to the voucher, approved GL coding, and period-end reconciliation."
Key point:
- The payment channel does not determine the accounting or tax treatment.
- Coding and recognition depend on transaction facts, applicable rules, and evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
30. Q: How should cash held or disbursed in foreign currency be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Maintain separate balances for each currency and record the original-currency amount, accounting-currency amount where required, approved exchange-rate source, rate date, conversion basis, fees, and supporting transfer or conversion evidence. Reconcile cash held, cash paid, cash returned, outstanding vouchers, and advances by currency before determining the accounting-currency impact. Do not net a shortage in one currency against a surplus in another. If currency conversion is necessary, the rate and method must follow the applicable accounting and treasury policy. Use explicit decimal calculations and documented currency-specific rounding rules so the results are reproducible. Unexplained differences, unsupported rates, and missing conversion evidence must remain open exceptions until resolved or formally handled under an approved process.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a hypothetical multi-currency fund from original amounts through conversion evidence, fees, rounding, and ledger values."
Key point:
- Reconciliation is currency-specific; conversion and rounding must be controlled.
- A fictional exchange rate is not an approved operational parameter.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
31. Q: How should cash in transit between a bank, headquarters, and a field site be controlled?
A: Show answer
A transfer should have a named owner, authorized amount and currency, dispatch date and time, recipient, approved movement method, expected receipt time, and supporting evidence. Dispatch and receipt should be separately recorded so the organization can identify whether a difference arose before, during, or after the transfer. Any timing item used in reconciliation must satisfy an explicit approved policy and be independently confirmed. The movement method and limits must be based on a current site-risk assessment; detailed safe routes, transport arrangements, and other sensitive information should be protected from broad access. A delayed or disputed transfer must be escalated according to the expected settlement deadline. Do not treat a transfer instruction or dispatch message as proof that the receiving custodian has accepted the cash.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a cash transfer through authorization, dispatch evidence, recipient acknowledgement, reconciliation, and resolution of a delayed receipt."
Key point:
- Dispatch, receipt, and accountability transfer are separate events.
- Cash-in-transit records must protect both financial traceability and physical safety.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
32. Q: How should cash be managed when a field site loses system connectivity or cannot immediately access the finance system?
A: Show answer
The organization should define whether offline transactions are permitted and establish a controlled fallback before an outage occurs. Where approved, use pre-numbered forms, a controlled allocation of unique transaction references, secure local custody, documented authorization, defined expiry or synchronization deadlines, and independent review where practicable. Record the time, site, custodian, currency, amount, purpose, approval, and evidence for every movement. When connectivity returns, enter or upload each transaction, reconcile the offline records with the system, and investigate sequence gaps, duplicate references, conflicting amounts, or delayed entries. The outage log should show when the system became unavailable, which controls were used, who approved the fallback, and when normal processing was restored. If required authority, evidence, or safety conditions cannot be maintained, the transaction must remain on hold unless an explicitly authorized safe procedure applies.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an outage and test transaction numbering, local evidence custody, synchronization, and independent reconciliation after system recovery."
Key point:
- Offline processing requires a pre-approved, time-limited, auditable fallback.
- System recovery does not remove the obligation to reconcile every offline transaction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
33. Q: What is the purpose of independent routine and surprise cash counts?
A: Show answer
An independent cash count tests whether recorded accountability agrees with physical cash and supported outstanding items at a defined cutoff. The counter should not be the sole custodian or the person whose records are being independently certified. The count should record date and time, site, fund, currency, denominations, total physical cash, any sealed or separately controlled holdings relevant to the policy, outstanding voucher population, and identified differences. Risk-based schedules should establish routine frequency, while surprise counts may be triggered by unusual balances, custodian changes, incidents, repeated exceptions, or other risk indicators. Any count should be reconciled with the movement log and accounting records. The custodian may explain discrepancies, but the independent reviewer determines the evidence required for resolution and confirms whether corrective action has been retested.

Study instruction:
- "Perform a mock surprise count and reconcile the physical balance to the cash log, approved vouchers, and authorized float."
Key point:
- Independent counts provide evidence that cash exists and accountability records are complete.
- The counter must document differences rather than adjust the records informally.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
34. Q: What should happen when a custodian is absent, replaced, or unable to complete a scheduled handover?
A: Show answer
The fund owner should activate the approved alternate-custodian or emergency-handover procedure. The responsible parties should establish the physical amount available, outstanding vouchers, unresolved differences, advances, cash transfers, keys, access, and pending approvals as far as safely possible. A documented handover record should identify the outgoing and incoming custodian, date and time, evidence, open items, approval, and required access changes. If the outgoing custodian is unavailable or a safety event prevents the usual count, do not fabricate a confirmation or assume that the recorded balance is correct. Use the approved contingency, document unavailable evidence and the reason, arrange an independent follow-up, and retain unresolved items as open. The incoming custodian should acknowledge only the responsibility actually transferred and supported by the evidence available.

Study instruction:
- "Review a planned and an emergency custodian handover and determine what can be certified, what remains unresolved, and who must follow up."
Key point:
- Custody can change only through a documented and appropriately authorized process.
- Missing handover evidence must remain visible and be independently followed up.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
35. Q: When should a cash fund or outstanding advance be suspended or closed?
A: Show answer
Suspension or closure may be necessary when the operational need ends, authorization expires, a site becomes unsafe, cash exposure is no longer justified, repeated discrepancies occur, required controls fail, or the organization changes its operating model. The decision should identify the fund or advance, effective date, reason, approving authority, outstanding balances, and restrictions on further transactions. Closure requires a final count or accepted accountability confirmation, reconciliation, settlement of eligible vouchers and advances, resolution or formal disposition of remaining differences, recovery of unused cash, updates to access and keys, and complete archival. Closing a system record without clearing physical and accounting accountability is not sufficient. If unresolved items cannot be settled immediately, retain them under a documented open or hold status with an accountable owner and deadline rather than representing the entire process as complete.

Study instruction:
- "Build a closure checklist and test whether cash, advances, access, accounting entries, exceptions, and archives have all been addressed."
Key point:
- Formal closure covers both operational authorization and outstanding financial accountability.
- Unresolved balances cannot disappear merely because a fund or site is closed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
36. Q: How should a theft, robbery, suspected fraud, counterfeit note, or other cash-security incident be handled?
A: Show answer
Prioritize personal safety and follow the approved local emergency and security instructions. Staff must not resist, pursue, threaten, or confront an offender to recover cash. Notify the designated security, finance, management, and investigation contacts according to the approved incident protocol. Record the detection time, site and fund, estimated amount and currency, incident type, immediate safety action, notifications and times, containment actions, evidence-preservation steps, responsible owner, specialist referrals, deadlines, and corrective actions. Clearly distinguish verified facts from allegations or estimates. Preserve relevant records and access history without exposing sensitive security information through general registers. Keep the incident open until the appropriate authority determines the disposition and an independent reviewer verifies that the required actions have been completed. Accounting adjustments must follow the applicable framework and should not substitute for incident investigation.

Study instruction:
- "Create an incident response record for a hypothetical cash loss and identify the safety actions, required notifications, preserved evidence, and closure authority."
Key point:
- Staff safety takes priority over cash or property recovery.
- Investigation, accounting treatment, and incident closure require distinct evidence and approvals.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
37. Q: Which fraud and error risks should the cash-control process detect?
A: Show answer
Relevant risks include fictitious expenditure, duplicate vouchers, repeated reimbursement, unauthorized personal use, split transactions intended to bypass limits, self-approval, altered or fabricated receipts, unrecorded cash transfers, concealed shortages, incorrect currency conversion, and advances that remain unsettled indefinitely. Preventive controls include clear limits, independent approval, named custodians, standardized evidence, unique identifiers, payee checks, and restricted access. Detective controls include duplicate testing, sequence-gap reviews, surprise counts, aging analysis, comparisons with the general ledger, and repeated-exception monitoring. Automated flags should identify suspicious or incomplete transactions without making unsupported accusations or deleting legitimate records automatically. Suspected misconduct requires the designated investigation procedure, evidence preservation, and controlled communications. Corrective actions should address the root cause and be independently retested before the relevant control is considered effective.

Study instruction:
- "Test a sample cash log for duplicates, split payments, unusual amounts, sequence gaps, self-approval, and overdue advances."
Key point:
- Fraud prevention requires preventive, detective, and corrective controls.
- A warning indicator requires review and evidence, not an automatic conclusion of misconduct.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Payment and Petty Cash Procedures
38. Q: How should imprest and advance activity be reconciled to the general ledger at month-end?
A: Show answer
Prepare a reconciliation by fund, site, account, currency, and reporting period. Compare the cash log and physical count with the relevant cash balance, eligible unreplenished vouchers, outstanding accountable advances, replenishments, returned cash, approved adjustments, and recorded journal entries. Investigate differences arising from cutoff, omitted postings, duplicate entries, classification errors, timing items, foreign exchange, or incomplete settlement evidence. Confirm that journal entries agree to accepted source documents and the applicable accounting policy. Reconcile opening balances to closing balances and distinguish transactions processed after the period cutoff from adjustments belonging to the period. Any unsupported or unexplained balance should be assigned to a named owner and remain open until properly resolved or formally escalated. A reconciliation signed without review evidence is not sufficient to demonstrate that the underlying control operated effectively.

Study instruction:
- "Trace each closing cash and advance balance to the movement register, supporting documents, journal entries, and reconciliation approval."
Key point:
- Operational cash records and general-ledger balances must be linked and reconciled.
- Cutoff and classification differences need documented explanations and approved corrections.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
39. Q: Which forms and registers are needed to operate the SOP consistently?
A: Show answer
The control framework should provide controlled forms and registers for document approval and revisions, source and applicability verification, entity and fund parameters, custodian appointments, site-risk assessments, disbursement requests, numbered vouchers, cash movements, replenishment calculations, field advance issuance, settlements, aging and reminders, independent cash counts, reconciliation differences, handovers, currency transfers, security incidents, corrective actions, financial reporting interfaces, implementation acknowledgements, management KPIs, records retention, and quality testing. Each form should have an owner, trigger, required fields, status definitions, source references, approval requirements, and audit fields. Avoid duplicating the same information in multiple unmanaged lists. Link records through unique identifiers so the same transaction can be traced from authorization to final accounting and reporting. Where forms collect sensitive information, access must be limited to authorized roles.

Study instruction:
- "Inventory the existing cash forms and map each to its purpose, owner, required fields, approval, evidence, and downstream process."
Key point:
- Forms should support actual control activities rather than create paperwork without an owner.
- Unique identifiers and consistent data fields improve traceability across registers.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
40. Q: How should audit trails and evidence retention be designed for cash controls?
A: Show answer
Retain approved requests, authorizations, voucher and movement records, supporting receipts, acknowledgements, reconciliations, replenishment and settlement calculations, journal references, exception records, approvals, and evidence of resolution. The archive should be organized by entity, year, period, site, fund, transaction or advance identifier, and document type, using a consistent naming and version-control convention. The organization must establish retention periods from applicable legal obligations, donor agreements, contracts, approved internal policy, and any relevant legal hold. The example's source register does not establish a universal retention period. Maintain evidence of who created, changed, reviewed, approved, transferred, or disposed of material records, and preserve the original and corrected versions when changes occur. Access should be role-based, backups tested, and recovery arrangements verified so that the evidence remains available for audit and follow-up.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one historical cash transaction from the archive index to its authorization, source evidence, accounting entry, approval, and final reconciliation."
Key point:
- Retention periods depend on applicable law, contracts, donor requirements, and organizational policy.
- An audit trail must preserve both transaction evidence and material changes.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
41. Q: How should personal information in payee, recipient, and incident records be protected?
A: Show answer
Collect only information necessary for the transaction, accountability, investigation, or legally required reporting. Restrict access to identity documents, contact details, allegations, incident evidence, and other sensitive information to authorized users with a legitimate business need. Do not put passwords, authentication tokens, safe combinations, or other security secrets in the cash register or evidence archive. Establish secure storage, sharing, transmission, backup, disposal, and incident-response procedures under the applicable privacy and records policy. If information is shared with a donor, auditor, legal adviser, or external authority, verify that the disclosure is authorized and appropriately scoped. Legal requirements and retention periods must be checked for the actual jurisdiction and organization. A generic reference to a privacy law or the existence of a record is not, by itself, proof that a particular disclosure or retention period is permissible.

Study instruction:
- "Review a sample register and identify unnecessary personal data, excessive access, unsafe document storage, and disclosure risks."
Key point:
- Cash-control evidence should be sufficient without exposing unnecessary sensitive information.
- Privacy and retention requirements must be verified for the actual organization and jurisdiction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
42. Q: How should unresolved exceptions and corrective actions be monitored?
A: Show answer
Maintain an exception register for failed authorization gates, missing receipts, sequence gaps, duplicate claims, shortages, surpluses, overdue advances, unapproved parameters, unresolved transfers, failed system imports, and unexplained general-ledger balances. Each record should identify the issue, relevant fund or transaction, estimated amount, risk, responsible owner, deadline, containment action, evidence required, escalation level, and closure authority. Separate verified facts from unconfirmed allegations. A corrective action should explain the root cause, proposed change, implementation date, responsible person, and independent retest. An exception must not be closed merely because it has been transferred to a new month or assigned to another employee. If a mandatory control cannot be satisfied, the affected activity should remain on hold until the authorized decision-maker establishes a permitted and safe resolution.

Study instruction:
- "Select recurring exceptions and confirm whether each has a root cause, accountable owner, deadline, containment action, and independent closure evidence."
Key point:
- Exceptions need ownership, deadlines, escalation, and documented closure criteria.
- Corrective action is effective only when the control improvement has been tested.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
43. Q: Which indicators should management use to assess whether cash controls are operating effectively?
A: Show answer
Useful indicators include the percentage of active funds with complete approved master data, the number of missing or duplicate vouchers, the value and age of overdue advances, the percentage of scheduled counts completed, the number and age of cash differences, unreconciled ledger balances, unconfirmed transfers, repeated evidence exceptions, and overdue source or parameter reviews. Define the numerator, denominator, source system, reporting frequency, owner, target, escalation threshold, and calculation method for each KPI. The proportion of overdue advances, for example, should use the full relevant advance population rather than only cases already raised as exceptions. Management should review trends and recurring causes rather than treating a single month's indicator as proof of effectiveness. Automated metrics must be compared with actual evidence to ensure that delayed updates or missing records have not made performance appear better than it is.

Study instruction:
- "Build a cash-control dashboard with reproducible KPI definitions and compare its output with the underlying registers and source evidence."
Key point:
- Indicators need clear definitions, reliable source data, and accountable owners.
- A favorable dashboard does not replace independent review or investigation.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
44. Q: How can Excel, Power Query, or VBA support cash registers and reconciliations without taking over accounting judgment?
A: Show answer
A controlled workbook can maintain fund masters, custodians, movement logs, vouchers, advance aging, settlement worksheets, count records, and exceptions. Power Query can import and standardize approved source files, combine consistent register structures, and flag missing fields or potential duplicates. Excel formulas can calculate approved reconciliation equations and aging, while VBA can create reports, reminders, controlled exports, and workflow logs. The source evidence should remain identifiable and protected from accidental alteration. Automated results must be tested against independently calculated examples, and material exceptions must be reviewed rather than automatically cleared. Changes to calculations, mapping, limits, or business rules require approval and documented testing before release. These tools improve repeatability but do not establish whether an expense is allowable, an accounting treatment is valid, a donor condition has been met, or a cash movement is safe.

Study instruction:
- "Prototype a cash movement register and automated reconciliation, then test duplicate detection, missing evidence, limit breaches, and unresolved differences."
Key point:
- Automation should standardize records and surface exceptions, not replace professional judgment.
- Accounting rules and authorization parameters must come from approved sources and policies.

45. Q: How should changes to the SOP, fund parameters, and system configuration be approved and tested?
A: Show answer
The document owner should maintain a controlled revision register containing the version, source hash or identifier where used, status, owner, reviewer, approver, effective date, rationale, and change history. A change to limits, roles, accounting mappings, settlement deadlines, eligibility rules, or system configuration should trigger an impact assessment covering active funds, open advances, current transactions, reports, training, and evidence requirements. Test the revised rules against representative valid and invalid cases, including limit breaches, expired approvals, missing evidence, duplicate claims, and unsettled balances. Material changes should invalidate obsolete approvals or test results where they no longer support the revised procedure. Release should occur only after current authority, required tests, rollback or contingency arrangements, communication, and independent verification are complete. Preserve prior approved versions for historical traceability.

Study instruction:

- An approved change must be tested before it becomes operational.
- Historical versions and the reasons for changes must remain traceable.

46. Q: How should a fictional fixed-imprest reconciliation example be checked mathematically?
A: Show answer
The example specification uses a fictional authorized float of IDR 2,000,000, physical cash of IDR 750,000, eligible unreplenished vouchers of IDR 1,250,000, and permitted timing items of IDR 0. The arithmetic is IDR 750,000 plus IDR 1,250,000 plus IDR 0, which equals IDR 2,000,000. On those assumptions, the difference is zero and the illustrated eligible replenishment is IDR 1,250,000, restoring the float to IDR 2,000,000 when replenishment is completed. The reviewer must still verify physical cash, voucher eligibility, approvals, prior reimbursement status, and cutoff before accepting the result. Mathematical equality alone does not prove that transactions occurred or that supporting evidence is authentic. These figures demonstrate the equation and must not be copied as actual fund limits or operating balances.

Study instruction:
- "Recalculate the fictional IDR 2,000,000 float and identify which source documents are needed to validate each component."
Key point:
- A balanced equation is necessary but not sufficient evidence of cash accountability.
- Illustrative values are not organizational authorization or proof of actual cash.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
47. Q: How should a fictional field-site advance settlement example be checked?
A: Show answer
The example uses a fictional advance issued of IDR 1,500,000, accepted eligible expenditure of IDR 1,125,000, and returned cash of IDR 375,000. The calculation is IDR 1,125,000 plus IDR 375,000, which equals the IDR 1,500,000 issued. The residual is therefore zero in the illustration. To establish an actual settlement, Finance must inspect the original authorization, cash receipt acknowledgement, individual expenditure documents, eligibility and coding decisions, proof of returned cash, review approval, and accounting entries. If expenditure is unsupported or the returned amount has not been confirmed, the equation cannot be treated as satisfied merely because the numbers add up. The remaining advance should stay open until the evidence supports an approved settlement or other authorized disposition. These values illustrate calculation only and must not be treated as a real transaction.

Study instruction:
- "Validate the illustrative settlement using source evidence and identify what prevents closure when the refund or any expenditure line is unsupported."
Key point:
- A zero residual must be supported by accepted expenditure and confirmed cash return.
- Mathematical agreement does not replace evidence, review, or approval.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Advance and Payment Procedures
48. Q: How should management investigate a cash-count shortage when all vouchers appear to be present?
A: Show answer
Begin by securing the count evidence and confirming the cutoff, fund identity, authorized float, voucher population, and currency. Independently recalculate the reconciliation and compare it with the cash movement log, replenishments, returns, bank or transfer records, and ledger postings. Check for duplicate entries, missing movements, voided vouchers, unauthorized payments, unrecorded receipts, arithmetic errors, or possible theft. Record facts separately from explanations and allegations. The responsible investigator should document procedures performed, persons interviewed where appropriate, evidence obtained, possible financial and donor implications, and recommended corrective action. Finance should not insert an unsupported timing item or post an unexplained adjustment merely to balance the register. The incident remains open until the appropriate authority approves the resolution and an independent reviewer confirms the correction and any necessary control improvements.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an investigation workpaper for a fictional shortage and link each proposed explanation to supporting or contradictory evidence."
Key point:
- A shortage must be investigated even when the voucher register appears complete.
- Balancing adjustments cannot substitute for an evidence-based investigation.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
49. Q: What is required before the example SOP can be approved and released for actual use?
A: Show answer
The organization must resolve the applicable framework and legal entity, obtain and review relevant approved internal policies and donor requirements, validate the actual fund and transaction parameters, approve the delegations and segregation of duties, complete the current site-security assessment, establish evidence-retention and privacy requirements, and confirm the accounting and system mappings. The document must be reviewed by the appropriate finance, legal, grants, security, system, and records owners where those areas are relevant. Required forms, procedures, acceptance criteria, and negative tests should be verified against the final controlled document. Management must ensure that fictional names, sites, balances, dates, approvals, incidents, and exchange rates are clearly distinguished from actual organizational records. The final version should state its approval status, effective date, owner, reviewer, authorized approver, version, and review cycle. Until required gates are complete, the example remains a draft rather than operative authority.

Study instruction:

Key point:
- A complete-looking document is not equivalent to an approved and applicable policy.
- Actual parameters and evidence must be validated before operational release.

50. Q: How should an NGO implement, train staff on, and continuously improve its cash imprest and field-site advance controls?
A: Show answer
Implement the procedure in controlled stages. First, approve the applicable policy and parameters; establish fund and custodian masters; configure forms, accounting mappings, system roles, and evidence storage; and communicate responsibilities. Train requesters, approvers, custodians, reviewers, accountants, and incident owners using realistic examples of payments, reconciliations, overdue advances, missing evidence, and security events. Pilot the process with an independently reviewed sample before wider rollout. Monitor completeness, reconciliation differences, overdue balances, count coverage, repeat exceptions, and timeliness. Review findings regularly with management and assign corrective actions with owners and deadlines. Reassess the SOP when laws, donor agreements, site risks, systems, currency arrangements, or organizational responsibilities change. Preserve previous versions and evidence of training, approval, implementation testing, and independent verification so that the organization can demonstrate not only that a procedure exists, but that it operates as intended.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a staged implementation plan with approved parameters, staff training, pilot testing, monitoring indicators, and independent review."
Key point:
- Operational effectiveness must be demonstrated through testing and ongoing monitoring.
- Continuous improvement should address documented risks and recurring control failures.
