Table 7
Corporate Credit Card Governance and Controls
1. Q: What is the purpose of a corporate credit card standard operating procedure, and why must the example remain on hold until formally adopted?
A: Show answer
A corporate credit card SOP establishes the controlled process for requesting, approving, issuing, activating, using, reconciling, securing, suspending, replacing, and cancelling organizational payment cards. Its purpose is to ensure that expenditure serves an authorized business purpose, follows applicable procurement and donor requirements, has adequate supporting evidence, and is recorded accurately. The SOP should define responsibilities, spending permissions, approval rules, exception handling, accounting interfaces, and evidence requirements. However, the supplied document is explicitly a fictional demonstration, not an approved organizational policy. Its named organization, issuer, personnel, cards, transactions, limits, approvals, and test records do not establish actual facts. Before implementation, the organization must verify its legal circumstances, issuer agreement, internal policies, delegated authority, applicable donor conditions, tax requirements, security arrangements, and operational configuration. Formal adoption requires documented review, authorized approval, implementation testing, and evidence that the controls work in the actual environment.

Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between a demonstration SOP and an approved operating procedure, and identify the evidence required before implementation."

Key point:
- A fictional example is a design reference, not evidence of actual authority or approval.
- Organizational adoption requires verified requirements, documented approval, and tested controls.
2. Q: Which activities should fall within the scope of a corporate credit card SOP?
A: Show answer
The scope should cover the complete card lifecycle, from the initial business request to final cancellation and closure of outstanding transactions. Activities normally include holder eligibility, authorization, card-profile configuration, permitted expenditure, transaction limits, supporting documents, statement processing, independent reconciliation, disputes, fraud response, refunds, security incidents, and offboarding. The SOP should identify which card types and organizational entities are covered and whether virtual cards, equivalent payment instruments, or specialized purchasing arrangements require separate rules. It must also define process boundaries. Procurement continues to govern sourcing and purchasing approvals; travel procedures govern travel authorization and claims; accounts payable governs issuer settlement; the accounting manual governs journal entries and close; tax personnel determine tax treatment; and Grants Finance assesses donor eligibility. These activities should connect through explicit handoffs rather than being duplicated or ambiguously assigned.

Study instruction:
- "Map the corporate card lifecycle and identify the responsible SOP or process owner for every handoff."

Key point:
- The card SOP controls card-related authorization, evidence, and lifecycle activities without replacing adjacent procedures.
- Every interface needs a defined owner, required input, expected output, and escalation route.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
3. Q: How should an organization determine which laws, issuer terms, donor conditions, and internal policies govern its corporate card program?
A: Show answer
Establish a source and applicability register that identifies each requirement, the authority behind it, its effective period, its scope, the responsible reviewer, and the conclusion reached. Applicable legislation and regulatory orders take priority within their legal scope. The executed issuer agreement governs the contractual relationship and issuer-specific operating rules. Donor agreements determine award-specific expenditure eligibility and reporting conditions. Approved organizational policies and valid delegated authority determine internal permissions. Technical settings must implement these requirements rather than override them. For an Indonesian organization, potentially relevant sources include payment-system regulation, personal data protection law, tax legislation, and applicable tax-administration rules. Their application must be assessed against actual entity, transaction, issuer, and contractual facts. Where a rule or parameter remains unknown, the register should show HOLD or UNVERIFIED and identify the evidence required to resolve it. The existence of a regulation alone does not establish that every provision applies to the card program.

Study instruction:
- "Create a source register showing the governing document, applicability decision, effective date, reviewer, and unresolved issues."

Key point:
- Source existence and applicability are separate determinations.
- Unknown legal, contractual, or policy requirements must remain unresolved until verified.

Source links:
- Bank Indonesia — Regulation No. 23/6/PBI/2021 on Payment Service Providers
4. Q: What information should appear on the corporate card SOP document control and approval record?
A: Show answer
The control record should identify the applicable legal entity, document owner, independent reviewer, authorized approver, document identifier, version, generation or revision date, effective date, next review date, and approval reference. Issuer and product details should be recorded only after they have been verified against actual documentation. Each field should have an accountable owner and a defined completion requirement. An approval reference should identify a genuine authorization record rather than a placeholder or invented reference. Draft documents should remain clearly marked as drafts, and the fictional status of training materials should not be confused with an actual release state. Changes to material policy requirements should trigger controlled revision, review, approval, and communication. The organization should retain previous approved versions so historical transactions can be assessed against the rules applicable at that time.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect a sample document control record and identify missing ownership, authorization, effective-date, and version evidence."

Key point:
- Document status must reflect actual approval evidence.
- Version history preserves the basis for historical decisions and audit review.
5. Q: How should responsibilities and delegated authority be separated across the corporate card lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Assign distinct responsibilities to the requester, business owner, line manager, budget owner, authorized approver, card program owner, issuer administrator, cardholder, independent Finance reviewer, HR, Security, Tax, Grants Finance, and Internal Audit. A requester explains the business need, while the approver authorizes it within documented delegation. The issuer administrator implements approved settings but does not determine whether the purchase is organizationally permissible. Cardholders document their own transaction facts but cannot be the sole approver of their expenditure or reconciliation. Finance reviewers assess evidence and accounting classification. Tax and Grants Finance resolve their respective specialist questions. Security manages incidents, while HR supplies employment-status changes that trigger card reviews. Delegation must be verified for the legal entity, organizational scope, currency, permitted activity, monetary threshold, and effective dates. Where staffing limitations prevent full segregation, introduce independently evidenced compensating controls and explicitly assess the remaining risk.

Study instruction:
- "Build a responsibility matrix for requesting, approving, implementing, using, reconciling, and cancelling a corporate card."

Key point:
- Responsibility and authority must be explicitly assigned and independently reviewable.
- A technical administrator cannot replace a delegated business approver.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and journal workflows
6. Q: Why are segregation of duties and independent reconciliation essential controls in a corporate card program?
A: Show answer
Segregation of duties reduces the ability of one person to initiate, authorize, execute, conceal, and certify an improper transaction. The cardholder should not be the sole approver of their own spending, an administrator should not independently authorize their own privileged configuration change, and the reconciler should not certify their own transactions without independent review. The same principle applies to card issuance, limit changes, emergency exceptions, and cancellation. Independent reconciliation should compare the issuer statement population to supporting evidence, approvals, refunds, fees, disputes, and accounting records. If the organization has too few employees to separate every task, an authorized manager or independent reviewer should examine the activity and preserve evidence of that review. The compensating control must address the specific conflict rather than exist only as a general policy statement.

Study instruction:
- "Identify incompatible duties in a sample card workflow and design an independently evidenced compensating control for each unavoidable conflict."

Key point:
- A preparer cannot provide sufficient independent assurance over every action they perform.
- Compensating controls must be designed around the actual segregation limitation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation workflows
7. Q: How should card profiles, transaction limits, merchant restrictions, and other program settings be governed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a controlled register of card profiles and effective parameters. Depending on actual issuer capabilities and approved policy, settings may include transaction, daily, monthly or billing-cycle, online, foreign-use, cash-access, and merchant-category restrictions. Each setting should be linked to an authoritative source, an approved business requirement, an authorized approver, an implementation record, and a verification test. The register should distinguish proposed, approved, configured, verified, and active states. Do not infer a permissible spending limit from an example, a previous cardholder's settings, or an issuer's general product information. If an actual limit or permission cannot be verified, activation or the affected transaction should remain on HOLD. Material changes should require fresh authorization, testing, and a record of the previous and new values. Configuration changes must not be treated as evidence that the underlying purchase is eligible.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one proposed card limit from its policy basis through approval, system configuration, verification, and periodic review."

Key point:
- Card parameters require authoritative sources and controlled change management.
- An unknown setting is unresolved, not automatically compliant.

Source links:
- Bank Indonesia — Regulation on Payment Service Providers
8. Q: What eligibility checks should be completed before a corporate card is issued or activated?
A: Show answer
Verify that the proposed cardholder has an organizational role requiring a card, current authorization, an appropriate budget or purchasing need, completed required training, accepted cardholder terms, and a valid employment or engagement status. Confirm that the approved card profile matches the person's role and authorized use. Determine whether the cardholder, requesting manager, and approving official have satisfied all required approvals and whether the actual issuer onboarding requirements have been met. The inventory should link the card identifier to the holder, profile, organizational entity, approval record, and current lifecycle status. An approval request or issuer application is not proof that the card has been activated correctly. Missing eligibility, training, delegated approval, or configuration evidence should prevent activation until resolved. Reassess eligibility after changes in role, project, employment status, or assigned responsibilities.

Study instruction:
- "Apply an activation checklist to a new card request and document the result of each mandatory eligibility gate."

Key point:
- Issuance requires verified eligibility, authorization, training, and configuration.
- Incomplete prerequisites must prevent activation rather than be completed retrospectively by assumption.
9. Q: What information should a corporate card inventory contain, and how should it be reconciled with the issuer's records?
A: Show answer
The inventory should record a stable card identifier, masked card reference, holder identifier, profile, organizational entity, lifecycle status, relevant approval reference, issue and expiry details where needed, and the source of verified issuer status. It should also record assigned ownership for unresolved transactions or recurring payments. The inventory must distinguish active, pending approval, pending issuer setup, suspended, replacement pending, cancellation pending, cancelled, expired, and rejected states. Periodically reconcile internal records with the issuer's card roster and system data. Investigate cards that are missing from either population, cards assigned to former personnel, duplicate inventory records, and status conflicts. Protect sensitive payment information by never storing full card numbers, CVVs, PINs, passwords, or authentication secrets in the inventory. Only masked identifiers and approved secure-system references should appear in reporting artifacts.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a card inventory to the issuer roster and document how missing, duplicate, or status-inconsistent cards are resolved."

Key point:
- The internal inventory must agree with independently sourced issuer status.
- Only masked payment-card identifiers should appear in the register.

Source links:
- Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection — official Indonesian legal text
10. Q: How should permitted, conditional, restricted, and prohibited expenditures be distinguished?
A: Show answer
The expenditure matrix should identify the business purpose, conditions of use, approval requirements, supporting evidence, budget restrictions, and applicable system settings for each relevant category. Ordinary organizational supplies may be permissible when approved and documented. Online subscriptions, foreign-currency purchases, travel expenditure, equipment, cash-like transactions, and unusual merchant categories may require additional review under applicable policy or issuer terms. Personal purchases, card sharing, unauthorized cash access, concealment, and transaction splitting to evade controls should be prohibited. A transaction should not become permissible merely because the issuer authorizes it or the amount is below a system limit. If eligibility or permission is unclear, hold the transaction pending a documented decision. The matrix should also explain who can make that decision and whether the issue belongs to Procurement, Travel, Tax, Grants Finance, Security, or another specialist owner.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample transactions into permitted, conditional, restricted, prohibited, and unresolved categories using documented criteria."

Key point:
- Issuer approval and internal spending permission are separate decisions.
- Uncertain expenditure requires review rather than automatic acceptance.
11. Q: How should pre-authorization be linked to budgets, procurement requirements, donor restrictions, and issuer authorization?
A: Show answer
Before committing expenditure, identify the business need, the budget or funding source, the required internal approver, the applicable procurement procedure, and any relevant donor restrictions. Record the authorization reference, requested amount and currency, intended supplier, purpose, project or cost center, and any required conditions. Issuer authorization confirms only that the issuer has permitted or processed the payment under its own controls. It does not establish that internal approval was obtained, a procurement threshold was respected, the budget is available, or donor funding is eligible. The organization should therefore retain separate evidence of each required decision. If a transaction exceeds its authority or a required approval is missing, apply the appropriate hold and escalation process. A retroactive explanation should not be treated as automatically curing a prohibited purchase or unauthorized commitment.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a purchase from business request to internal authorization, budget confirmation, procurement checks, and issuer authorization."

Key point:
- Pre-authorization must address every relevant organizational control, not just the bank's payment decision.
- A card transaction's technical success does not prove policy compliance.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and supporting purchasing documents
12. Q: What information should be included in a corporate card purchase request and approval record?
A: Show answer
A request record should capture the requester, proposed cardholder, organizational entity, business purpose, supplier, expected amount and currency, purchase category, project or cost center, budget reference, and required date. It should also identify the applicable policy check, procurement approval, donor review where relevant, authorized approver, delegation basis, decision date, and approval conditions. The approved amount should be clearly distinguishable from the actual posted transaction amount. Changes to supplier, purpose, value, or scope should trigger reassessment when required by policy. Records should link to contracts, purchase orders, travel approvals, or other supporting evidence where applicable. A simple approval email may be useful evidence, but the organization should ensure that it clearly demonstrates the decision, authority, scope, and timing. An incomplete approval record should be returned for clarification or held according to risk.

Study instruction:
- "Create a purchase authorization record and identify the evidence needed to support a later independent review."

Key point:
- Approval evidence must establish who authorized what, when, and under which authority.
- Approved scope and actual expenditure should remain separately traceable.
13. Q: What supporting evidence is required to substantiate an individual corporate card transaction?
A: Show answer
Each transaction should normally be supported by an itemized receipt or invoice, a clear organizational business purpose, the approval reference, supplier identity, transaction date, amount, currency, and relevant delivery or service confirmation. Additional evidence may include a contract, purchase order, travel authorization, attendee record, subscription approval, or project coding instruction. The evidence requirements should reflect the nature and risk of the purchase rather than relying on a single document for every transaction. A card statement or merchant slip alone usually shows that a charge occurred, but it may not explain what was purchased, whether it was received, or whether it was authorized. Where a legally acceptable alternative document is permitted, the applicable requirements should be verified and recorded. Reviewers should be able to trace each transaction from the statement to its underlying business activity and supporting documentation.

Study instruction:
- "Select several card transactions and assess whether each has sufficient evidence of purpose, authorization, supplier, value, and receipt of goods or services."

Key point:
- A statement line proves a charge was recorded but does not necessarily substantiate its business purpose or eligibility.
- Evidence requirements should be defined and tested against actual transaction types.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
14. Q: How should missing receipts, incomplete invoices, and late cardholder submissions be handled?
A: Show answer
Record the affected transaction in an exception register with its card and statement references, amount, date, missing evidence, responsible person, risk, and resolution deadline. Contact the cardholder and request the missing documentation through an approved channel. If the receipt cannot be recovered, evaluate whether the policy and applicable requirements permit alternative evidence, a formal exception, reimbursement, or another corrective action. An alternative explanation should not automatically establish tax deductibility, donor eligibility, or compliance with procurement rules. The item should remain held or open while the required review is incomplete. Repeated delays should be analyzed for root causes, such as unclear deadlines, inadequate reminders, weak training, or an impractical evidence process. The control owner should escalate overdue high-risk items and retain evidence of the final resolution.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a missing-evidence register and distinguish acceptable documentary alternatives from unresolved policy exceptions."

Key point:
- Missing documentation must have an owner, deadline, and documented resolution.
- An exception does not automatically establish that the expenditure is compliant or recoverable.
15. Q: How should the organization distinguish a missing receipt from a prohibited or potentially fraudulent transaction?
A: Show answer
Missing evidence means the transaction is not yet sufficiently supported; it does not, by itself, prove fraud. A prohibited transaction is inconsistent with applicable rules, while a suspected fraudulent transaction involves indicators of potentially unauthorized or deceptive activity that require appropriate investigation. These categories may overlap, but they require different decisions. A missing receipt for an otherwise plausible business purchase may lead to a recovery request or an approved exception process. A personal purchase, card-sharing incident, altered document, or unexplained charge may require restrictions, incident escalation, and investigation. The reviewer should record the facts, preserve evidence, assess whether immediate containment is needed, and avoid unsupported accusations. Any disciplinary, legal, recovery, or fraud conclusion should follow the organization's authorized investigation procedure and applicable law.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a legitimate purchase with missing evidence, a known prohibited purchase, and an unrecognized charge, and determine the required response for each."

Key point:
- Evidence gaps, policy breaches, and suspected fraud are different classifications.
- Investigation and corrective action should follow verified facts and authorized procedures.
16. Q: How should statements be received, checked for completeness, and certified by cardholders?
A: Show answer
The statement process should establish the expected statement population for each card and billing period, the person responsible for receipt, the submission deadline, and the approved storage location. Reconcile the cards expected from the issuer roster with the statements received, including any replacement, suspended, or cancelled cards that may still have residual activity. The cardholder should review each transaction, confirm the business purpose and accuracy of known details, identify unfamiliar transactions, and provide the required supporting evidence. Certification should identify the relevant statement and period, the declarant, and the date. It should not replace independent Finance review or imply that every transaction is approved for accounting, tax, and donor purposes. Missing statements and late certifications should be recorded as exceptions with owners and deadlines. Completion requires evidence of receipt, review, certification, and follow-up where necessary.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one monthly statement from expected receipt through completeness checks, cardholder certification, and independent review."

Key point:
- Statement completeness must be checked against the expected card population.
- Cardholder certification is not a substitute for independent reconciliation or specialist review.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation workflows
17. Q: How should statement transactions be matched to approvals, receipts, refunds, and accounting records?
A: Show answer
Match each statement line using multiple attributes, including the transaction reference, card identifier, supplier, date, amount, currency, authorization reference, and supporting document. Where relevant, match the transaction to a purchase order, goods receipt, service confirmation, travel record, refund, or credit note. Reference numbers alone may be insufficient because suppliers can reuse invoice numbers or the same transaction may appear under different identifiers in separate systems. Candidate matches should be reviewed when amounts, dates, or currencies differ. The process should separately identify transaction fees, foreign-exchange differences, unsettled credits, reversals, and timing differences. The reviewer should preserve the source statement and evidence of material matching decisions. A transaction should not be marked fully supported merely because it has been matched to an amount in the general ledger.

Study instruction:
- "Build a transaction-matching worksheet and test ambiguous matches, partial refunds, duplicate references, and date differences."

Key point:
- Reliable matching uses several transaction attributes and underlying evidence.
- Matching, documentary support, and accounting approval are distinct control checks.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation workflows
18. Q: How should independent reconciliation of a corporate card statement be performed?
A: Show answer
The reconciliation should begin with the complete statement population and compare every charge, credit, refund, fee, reversal, and adjustment against the corresponding transaction and accounting evidence. Verify arithmetic, account coding, transaction currency, business purpose, authorization, and the completeness of supporting records. Differences should be classified as timing items, missing evidence, unmatched transactions, unexplained residuals, disputed charges, or other identified exceptions. Do not force the reconciliation to balance through an unsupported journal or unexplained adjustment. Use exact decimal arithmetic for financial calculations, preserve currency precision, and avoid floating-point calculations that can introduce unexplained rounding residuals. The reconciler should be independent of the relevant cardholder and retain the reconciliation, supporting schedules, reviewer sign-off, and outstanding-item register. Certification should remain blocked when a material discrepancy is unresolved.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a sample statement to transaction-level records and demonstrate how every difference is classified, assigned, and resolved."

Key point:
- An unexplained reconciliation residual must remain open and visible.
- Independent review and traceable arithmetic are essential to reliable reconciliation.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger, reconciliation, and adjustment processes
19. Q: How should unexplained residuals and timing differences be treated during card reconciliation?
A: Show answer
A timing difference is a difference that can be explained by the timing of a legitimate transaction, such as a charge recorded in one system before it appears in another. It should be separately identified with its originating transaction, expected clearing date, owner, and follow-up requirement. An unexplained residual is a difference for which adequate evidence or explanation is not yet available. It should not be labelled a timing difference merely to close the reconciliation. Record the amount, currency, relevant statement lines, investigation steps, risk, assigned owner, and deadline. If the difference remains open, keep the affected reconciliation in an appropriate HOLD or OPEN state. Where a correction is justified, obtain approval and document the posting or other resolution. Periodic review should identify old residuals and recurring causes instead of allowing unresolved items to roll forward indefinitely.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a reconciliation containing one justified timing difference and one unexplained residual, then demonstrate the different approval and closure requirements."

Key point:
- Timing differences require evidence and expected clearance; unexplained residuals require investigation.
- A reconciliation must not be certified by concealing unresolved differences.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation adjustments
20. Q: How should refunds, reversals, chargebacks, and issuer credits be controlled?
A: Show answer
A refund or reversal should be linked to the original transaction wherever possible. Record the original charge reference, refund or reversal reference, amount, currency, dates, supplier, statement period, and current status. Check whether the credit fully or partially offsets the original charge and whether any fee or currency difference remains. A disputed transaction should be tracked separately from a confirmed refund because a claim or provisional credit does not necessarily establish final resolution. Reconcile the credit to the issuer statement and relevant accounting records. If a refund does not appear within the expected period, retain the supplier correspondence, issuer case reference, responsible owner, and verified follow-up date. Do not assume that cancelling a purchase, receiving a merchant promise, or opening a chargeback case means that the amount has been recovered.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a refund from the original charge through supplier confirmation, issuer posting, accounting treatment, and final reconciliation."

Key point:
- Every refund or reversal should be linked to the original transaction and its final outcome.
- Claims, provisional credits, and confirmed recoveries should have distinct statuses.
21. Q: How should unfamiliar charges and potential card fraud be reported and investigated?
A: Show answer
The organization should provide a designated reporting route for unfamiliar transactions and suspected unauthorized use. When a charge is identified, preserve the statement line, transaction details, card identifier, relevant communications, and any available issuer reference. Contact the actual issuer through a verified channel and follow its applicable dispute process and deadlines. Apply immediate containment where required, such as blocking the card or restricting a potentially compromised account, while coordinating with the designated Security or Fraud owner. Record the incident, affected cards, value, date identified, risk assessment, actions, owner, and follow-up status. Do not invent issuer deadlines or promise that a disputed amount will be recovered. Continue accounting and statement reconciliation while clearly distinguishing disputed amounts from confirmed transactions and credits. Closure requires evidence of the issuer's outcome, the accounting resolution, and any required corrective action.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an unrecognized online transaction and prepare the incident record, containment decision, issuer case, and accounting follow-up."

Key point:
- Suspected fraud requires prompt containment, documented investigation, and verified issuer procedures.
- A dispute remains open until its outcome and financial consequences are established.

Source links:
- Bank Indonesia — Regulation on Payment Service Providers
22. Q: What should employees do when a corporate card is lost, stolen, or potentially compromised?
A: Show answer
Employees should report the incident immediately through the organization's designated channel and use the verified issuer channel to block or secure the card when directed by the approved response procedure. The organization should record when the incident was discovered, when it was reported, the card's masked identifier, the issuer reference, the actions taken, and the person responsible for follow-up. Where account credentials or digital wallet tokens may also be affected, coordinate the necessary access revocation and security review. Preserve available evidence and assess whether data protection, legal, or insurance processes need to be triggered under verified requirements. The replacement card should not be treated as a continuation of the old control state without renewed identity, authorization, and configuration checks. Verify issuer confirmation of blocking and ensure no unresolved transactions are abandoned during the incident response.

Study instruction:
- "Run a lost-card exercise and document the reporting sequence, block confirmation, access review, replacement controls, and remaining risks."

Key point:
- Immediate reporting and verified blocking reduce the risk of additional unauthorized activity.
- A replacement card requires controlled activation and a linked incident record.

Source links:
- Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection — official Indonesian legal text
23. Q: How should card suspension, replacement, renewal, cancellation, and employee offboarding be controlled?
A: Show answer
Define a status-driven lifecycle procedure with clear entry conditions, responsible roles, required actions, and closure evidence. Suspension should record the trigger and evidence that the card is no longer usable where required. Replacement and renewal should verify continued eligibility, authorization, current terms, and configuration. HR should notify the card program owner of termination, role changes, or other events that could affect eligibility. Cancellation should include an issuer request, issuer confirmation, internal inventory update, revocation of relevant digital tokens or portal access, and handling of physical cards where applicable. Check recurring subscriptions, outstanding receipts, disputed charges, expected refunds, and residual statement activity. Cancellation is not complete merely because a request has been submitted. The organization must verify the final state and assign an owner for every remaining financial or documentary issue.

Study instruction:
- "Create an offboarding checklist and verify each required issuer, internal system, physical card, recurring payment, and residual-balance control."

Key point:
- A cancellation request is not proof that the issuer has cancelled the card.
- Residual transactions and refunds remain subject to control after card cancellation.
24. Q: How should recurring subscriptions and online purchases be monitored throughout the card lifecycle?
A: Show answer
Maintain a register linking each recurring payment to the service owner, business purpose, supplier, payment frequency, currency, renewal date, approved budget, cancellation terms, and approving authority. Online purchases may require additional approval or specific card permissions under the applicable policy and issuer settings. Review subscriptions periodically to confirm that services are still required, authorized, used, and chargeable to the relevant organizational budget. A change in project, employee role, vendor, price, or service scope should trigger a review. When a card is suspended, replaced, or cancelled, determine whether recurring charges and digital wallet arrangements require specific action. Unexpected renewals, unidentified subscription charges, and duplicate services should be recorded as exceptions. Closure should include evidence of cancellation or an approved decision to continue the service.

Study instruction:
- "Review a recurring-payment register and identify unused subscriptions, approaching renewals, unauthorized price changes, and offboarding risks."

Key point:
- Recurring expenditure requires ongoing business ownership and periodic reauthorization.
- Card cancellation does not automatically resolve every subscription obligation.
25. Q: How should accounting entries and general ledger coding for corporate card transactions be determined?
A: Show answer
Accounting should reflect the substance of the goods or services received, the organization's accounting framework, the relevant payment and settlement arrangements, and approved accounting policies. Depending on the transaction, the accounting process may recognize an expense, an asset, a prepaid amount, a payable, a recoverable amount, or another appropriate balance. The existence of a card charge does not alone determine the account, recognition date, project allocation, or financial statement classification. Obtain the transaction evidence and identify the period, supplier, business purpose, relevant coding dimensions, and settlement reference. Reconcile card transactions to the issuer statement and relevant general ledger balances using the organization's approved close procedures. Journal entries should be reviewed and posted by authorized personnel. The SOP should define the handoff to the accounting close process rather than inventing universal journal entries for every purchase type.

Study instruction:
- "Select examples of supplies, equipment, subscriptions, and prepayments and determine which accounting-policy questions must be resolved before posting."

Key point:
- Classification and recognition follow the applicable accounting framework and actual transaction facts.
- Statement reconciliation supports accounting accuracy but does not replace the accounting decision.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
26. Q: How should corporate card expenditure be allocated to projects, donors, budgets, and cost centers?
A: Show answer
The allocation should follow the actual activity and the applicable accounting, budget, grant, and internal cost-allocation requirements. Record the project, donor, cost center, funding source, and other required coding dimensions when the transaction is processed. Verify whether the expenditure is eligible under the executed award, whether the relevant budget is available, and whether any special approval or allocation basis applies. A cardholder's organizational department does not automatically determine which project should bear the cost. Shared costs may require a documented allocation methodology and independent review. Reconcile the sum of project allocations to the source transaction total to detect omitted or duplicated amounts. The same cost must not be charged twice to different awards or included in a donor claim without meeting the relevant terms. Uncertain funding eligibility should remain unresolved until Grants Finance obtains sufficient evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a multi-project card transaction from source invoice through allocation calculations, award eligibility review, and final reconciliation."

Key point:
- Project coding and donor eligibility require separate, evidence-based decisions.
- Total allocated expenditure must reconcile to the underlying source transaction.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and coding
27. Q: How should tax invoices, VAT or other indirect taxes, and withholding-tax questions be handled for card purchases?
A: Show answer
The card SOP should specify when tax review is required without attempting to replace the organization's tax procedures. The tax owner should assess the actual supplier, transaction type, jurisdiction, taxpayer status, document type, and applicable law. A standard card receipt or payment slip should not automatically be assumed to satisfy legal tax-invoice requirements or support input-tax recovery. For Indonesian transactions, the applicability of documents recognized as equivalent to tax invoices depends on the relevant current rules and the facts of the transaction. Withholding obligations, tax deductibility, VAT recovery, and reporting should be determined by the authorized tax function. Record the tax decision, required evidence, reviewer, and relevant filing reference. If a document is incomplete or the treatment is uncertain, maintain an exception until the tax owner provides a supported conclusion.

Study instruction:
- "Review a card transaction with an invoice and payment slip, and list the evidence needed before deciding whether the tax documentation is adequate."

Key point:
- Card payment evidence and valid tax documentation are not necessarily the same thing.
- Tax treatment must be determined using current law and transaction-specific facts.

Source links:
- Directorate General of Taxes — Documents Treated as Equivalent to Tax Invoices
28. Q: How should foreign-currency card transactions and issuer fees be reconciled?
A: Show answer
Preserve the original transaction currency and amount, the statement currency, the issuer-reported settlement amount, applicable foreign-exchange information, and any separately charged fees. If an accounting or reporting conversion is required, use the exchange-rate basis specified by the applicable accounting policy and retain the rate source, date, method, and calculation. Do not combine amounts in different currencies into a single total without a documented conversion basis. Distinguish issuer conversion from accounting translation where their purpose or timing differs. Investigate unusual rate differences, duplicate fees, unexpected currency conversions, and amounts that do not match the underlying transaction. Use controlled decimal calculations for reconciliation and document rounding rules. Any material unexplained difference should remain open until reviewed and resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a foreign-currency transaction by comparing the original charge, issuer conversion, fees, accounting conversion, and rounding differences."

Key point:
- Currency amounts require clear separation of original values, settlement values, and accounting conversions.
- Exchange rates and rounding differences must be reproducible and supported.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and revaluation processes
29. Q: What is the correct response when a cardholder attempts to bypass a spending limit by splitting a purchase?
A: Show answer
Transaction splitting occurs when a related purchase is divided into smaller transactions to avoid an approval threshold, issuer restriction, procurement requirement, or other control. The reviewer should examine the commercial purpose, related invoices, dates, supplier, purchase order, total commitment, and transaction sequence to determine whether the purchases form one underlying requirement. Legitimate staged payments should be distinguished from deliberate control circumvention based on the facts and contract terms. The SOP should prohibit artificial splitting to evade controls and define the escalation route for suspected breaches. Automated monitoring can flag repeated transactions near thresholds, but a flagged pattern requires review rather than an automatic fraud conclusion. Any confirmed breach should be documented, assessed for financial and donor consequences, and addressed through authorized corrective action.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze a sequence of similar supplier charges and determine whether it represents legitimate staged purchasing or possible approval-threshold circumvention."

Key point:
- Approval thresholds must be assessed against the underlying purchase, not merely each payment fragment.
- Pattern detection should trigger investigation rather than unsupported automatic conclusions.
30. Q: How should emergency corporate card use be authorized without weakening mandatory controls?
A: Show answer
The emergency procedure should define what qualifies as an emergency, who can invoke the procedure, the maximum scope of delegated authority, which approvals remain mandatory, and how the transaction must be documented. An urgent operational need does not automatically waive legal requirements, issuer terms, procurement restrictions, or donor conditions. Where policy permits a specific expedited approval route, record the decision-maker, rationale, amount, currency, intended supplier, business purpose, and time of authorization. Define any time-sensitive requirements for obtaining additional evidence and completing the subsequent review. An emergency transaction that exceeds authority or involves prohibited use must not be legitimized solely by a later explanation. The incident or exception register should record the outcome and whether changes to preparedness or standard procedures are needed.

Study instruction:
- "Work through an urgent field-purchase scenario and identify which requirements can be expedited and which cannot be waived."

Key point:
- Emergency procedures must be predefined and remain subject to mandatory requirements.
- Urgency requires documented authority and later verification, not an undocumented override.
31. Q: How should access to issuer portals, payment-card data, and administrator privileges be controlled?
A: Show answer
Grant access according to an approved role matrix and the principle of least privilege. Each user should have only the permissions required for their assigned responsibilities, and privileged administrative changes should receive independent authorization. Maintain records of the user, role, approval, effective date, scope, reviewer, and revocation date. Review access when personnel change roles, leave the organization, or no longer require particular permissions. Where the issuer supports activity logs, use them to investigate creation, modification, approval, or cancellation actions. Avoid shared user accounts wherever individual attribution is available. Never record full card numbers, CVVs, PINs, passwords, one-time passcodes, or reusable authentication secrets in workbooks, SOPs, or ordinary shared registers. Store secure references in approved systems and document the response to any suspected unauthorized access.

Study instruction:
- "Compare a sample issuer-portal access list with the authorized role matrix and test whether privileged changes have independent approval."

Key point:
- Access must be role-based, reviewable, and removed when no longer required.
- Authentication secrets and full payment-card credentials must not appear in ordinary control records.

Source links:
- Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection — official Indonesian legal text
32. Q: How should corporate card records be retained, protected, and made available for audit?
A: Show answer
Maintain an indexed evidence package that links each statement period to source statements, transaction evidence, internal approvals, cardholder certifications, reconciliation workpapers, dispute records, refunds, accounting references, and the final review. Retention periods and access restrictions must follow verified legal, contractual, donor, and organizational requirements. The SOP should identify the records owner, approved repository, naming convention, access roles, backup method, retention schedule, and secure disposal method. Evidence should not be silently overwritten when a document is corrected or replaced. Maintain version identifiers and the reason for material changes. Backup procedures should be tested through recovery exercises rather than assumed to work because a copy exists. Respond to legal or audit holds according to the applicable retention procedure and ensure that authorized reviewers can retrieve the evidence without requesting new copies from former cardholders.

Study instruction:
- "Retrieve one historical statement package and verify that every key document is readable, linked to the transaction, access-controlled, and subject to a documented retention rule."

Key point:
- A complete audit trail connects transactions, approvals, reconciliation, and final resolution.
- Retention, access, backup, and disposal rules must be verified before implementation.

Source links:
- Law No. 27 of 2022 on Personal Data Protection — official Indonesian legal text
33. Q: How should the organization respond to an issuer-system outage or an unavailable card program owner near a deadline?
A: Show answer
The SOP should identify the authorized alternate, official issuer support channel, escalation criteria, incident record, and recovery procedure. Record the start time, affected function, relevant cards or transactions, operational consequences, attempted recovery, support reference, and decisions made. A system outage should not be assumed to extend an issuer deadline or waive organizational obligations. The owner must verify the actual issuer instructions and any applicable fallback arrangement before proceeding. Where an alternate approver is required, confirm that the person's delegated authority is current and covers the relevant decision. After recovery, complete pending transactions, validate the final status, reconcile affected balances, and document any delayed evidence or approvals. Repeated outages or unavailable personnel should lead to corrective action that improves continuity without weakening authorization controls.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate an issuer outage and an absent approver, then test escalation, delegated authority, evidence preservation, and controlled recovery."

Key point:
- Contingency procedures require verified authority and documented communication.
- Recovery does not remove the need for subsequent reconciliation and review.
34. Q: How should exceptions be recorded, escalated, and closed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a central exception register with a unique case identifier, affected card and transaction references, amount and currency, category, issue date, severity, evidence, owner, reviewer, required approval, target date, status, and final outcome. Categories may include missing documentation, unrecognized charges, unmatched statement lines, prohibited purchases, unresolved refunds, unauthorized configuration changes, and incomplete cancellations. Each case should define its investigation steps, escalation threshold, and acceptable closure evidence. Use clearly separated statuses such as OPEN, HOLD, UNDER REVIEW, APPROVED EXCEPTION, RESOLVED, and CLOSED, with documented transition rules. Closure should require evidence that the underlying issue has been addressed, any necessary accounting or issuer correction completed, and required independent review performed. Repeated exceptions should be analyzed for root causes and corrective actions rather than repeatedly closed as isolated incidents.

Study instruction:
- "Create a sample exception register and demonstrate the evidence and approval required to move an item from OPEN to CLOSED."

Key point:
- Every exception requires ownership, a deadline, a documented decision, and verifiable closure evidence.
- Repeated exceptions should trigger process improvement and control reassessment.
35. Q: How should controlled forms and registers support consistent corporate card administration?
A: Show answer
Standard forms reduce inconsistent data entry and make responsibilities, approvals, and evidence requirements easier to enforce. The program may require a card request and approval form, card inventory, holder acknowledgment, transaction evidence checklist, statement certification, reconciliation record, exception register, dispute and incident log, change authorization, cancellation checklist, and periodic certification record. Every form should have a unique identifier, owner, purpose, required fields, permitted status values, approval dependencies, and retention rules. Data fields should correspond to real control decisions and should not request unnecessary sensitive information. Mandatory fields should be validated, while genuinely unknown facts should be marked unresolved rather than populated with fabricated defaults. Form changes should be version-controlled and tested against the current SOP. The organization should demonstrate that users can complete the forms correctly and that reviewers can retrieve the related evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed form inventory and identify the required fields, approval dependencies, and evidence needed for each control objective."

Key point:
- Standard forms turn policy requirements into repeatable operational records.
- Form design must support traceability without creating unnecessary data-security risks.
36. Q: What is the difference between an approval status, a reconciliation status, and a final closure status?
A: Show answer
Approval status indicates whether the requested decision has been authorized, rejected, held, or returned for further information. Reconciliation status indicates whether the statement population and transactions have been matched to supporting records and accounting data, including the disposition of differences. Closure status indicates whether the entire required process or case has reached its defined completion criteria. These are separate control dimensions. A transaction can be approved but not supported by an invoice, reconciled but still disputed with the issuer, or paid but awaiting the final cancellation confirmation for its card. Do not compress all states into a single generic “complete” flag. Define the prerequisites for each transition and ensure that an unresolved critical issue prevents downstream closure where appropriate. A status report should reflect verifiable evidence rather than an assumption that earlier steps must have succeeded.

Study instruction:
- "Classify sample cases using separate approval, evidence, reconciliation, issuer, and closure statuses."

Key point:
- Approval, reconciliation, and closure represent different control decisions.
- A final status must be supported by evidence of all required completion criteria.
37. Q: How can Excel and Power Query help manage corporate card transactions and statement reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Excel tables can provide controlled structures for card inventories, statements, transaction evidence, approval references, and exception records. Power Query can import recurring statements and supporting registers, standardize date and currency formats, map fields to a common structure, identify missing identifiers, and flag possible duplicate transactions. The workbook can produce reconciliation worksheets and exception reports showing unmatched items, incomplete evidence, unresolved refunds, and unusual amounts. Users should retain source-file references, extraction dates, row counts, monetary control totals, and refresh results. Imported data must be validated against the actual statement population, and transformations must be tested before they are relied on for reporting. These tools can improve consistency and reduce manual comparison, but they cannot establish that expenditure is permitted, that an approval is valid, or that a tax or donor decision is correct.

Study instruction:
- "Design a Power Query workflow that imports issuer statements, standardizes transaction fields, flags exceptions, and proves that source totals have been preserved."

Key point:
- Automation can improve data preparation, matching, and exception visibility.
- Policy, accounting, tax, and donor decisions remain subject to authorized review.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — What Is Power Query?
- Microsoft Learn — Power Query data transformation
38. Q: How should VBA and spreadsheet automation be tested before being used for card administration or financial reporting?
A: Show answer
Define the expected behavior of each automated function before deployment. Tests should cover ordinary supported purchases, missing receipts, duplicate records, refunds, partial credits, currency differences, unmatched statement lines, unauthorized changes, inactive cards, and failed imports. Use independently calculated expected results and compare them to the workbook output. Invalid or missing inputs should generate clear errors or controlled HOLD states rather than silently producing a completed result. Test that validation-only operations do not create or overwrite official documents, and verify any generated output files and audit logs. Changes to code, formulas, or configuration should require controlled review, version identification, and retesting of affected behavior. A passed local test is not evidence of successful live issuer integration, external system compatibility, or formal approval for production use.

Study instruction:
- "Build positive and negative test cases for transaction matching, exception detection, calculation accuracy, output generation, and access control."

Key point:
- Automation requires repeatable tests, independent expected results, and controlled change management.
- Local tests do not establish that external integrations or production settings are valid.

Source links:
- Microsoft Learn — Excel VBA reference
39. Q: How should a corporate card monitoring dashboard measure whether controls are effective?
A: Show answer
Define indicators with a clear calculation method, population, source, owner, frequency, target, and escalation rule. Useful measures include the proportion of eligible cardholders with current training and approvals, completeness of the statement population, transactions missing required evidence, unresolved reconciliation residuals, overdue exceptions, open disputes approaching verified issuer deadlines, cancellation failures, and unauthorized configuration changes. Monitoring should also identify recurring subscriptions without current business ownership and former employees with unresolved card-related items. Use source records and verified system status rather than assuming that a workbook flag reflects actual completion. Report both the number and the value of unresolved items where meaningful, and distinguish high-risk exceptions from routine delays. Management should review trends over time and assign corrective actions with owners and due dates. A dashboard should direct investigation, not replace the underlying evidence or independent review.

Study instruction:
- "Define a monitoring dashboard with reproducible indicators and trace each reported metric to its underlying inventory, statement, or exception register."

Key point:
- Effective KPIs have explicit definitions, complete populations, and verifiable sources.
- Dashboard indicators should lead to assigned actions and independent follow-up.
40. Q: How should a corporate card SOP be validated through an end-to-end walkthrough before adoption?
A: Show answer
Select a representative card and statement period, then trace the process from the business request and approval through card assignment, transaction, supporting evidence, cardholder certification, reconciliation, accounting handoff, and final closure. Include exceptions such as a missing receipt, a refund, a foreign-currency transaction, or an unrecognized charge where relevant. Record the evidence shown at each stage, the responsible roles, the dates, the system used, and the result of each control. Compare actual process behavior with the draft procedure and identify missing approvals, undocumented decisions, timing gaps, or status transitions that cannot be substantiated. Distinguish documentary evidence from verbal explanations. Record each gap with its risk, required remediation, responsible owner, target date, and retest requirement. The walkthrough supports validation but does not replace formal approval, legal review, or production testing.

Study instruction:
- "Perform an end-to-end walkthrough using real authorized records and document every control gap with evidence, ownership, and a remediation plan."

Key point:
- A walkthrough tests whether the documented process reflects actual evidence and operational behavior.
- Approval should follow resolution or formal acceptance of identified implementation risks.

Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and reconciliation workflows
41. Q: How should the organization handle changes to card limits, merchant restrictions, approval rules, or other configuration parameters?
A: Show answer
Treat a configuration change as a controlled authorization event. The change request should explain the business need, affected card or profile, current setting, requested setting, supporting source, risk assessment, effective date, and requested approver. The approver must have authority for the specific legal entity, scope, and value involved. The technical administrator should implement only the approved change and preserve evidence of completion. A reviewer should independently verify the final setting and confirm that the required tests have passed. Changes that affect policy, user permissions, or transaction eligibility should trigger updates to the relevant SOP, matrices, and training materials. Emergency changes should follow the defined exception procedure and be reviewed promptly afterward. Unapproved or unexplained changes should be contained and investigated rather than accepted because the system permits them.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a material profile change from request through approval, implementation, independent verification, testing, and register update."

Key point:
- Technical configuration must follow approved policy and delegated authority.
- Independent verification is required to confirm that implemented settings match the authorized change.
42. Q: What should management verify before certifying the corporate card program periodically?
A: Show answer
A periodic certification should reconcile the internal card inventory to the issuer's roster, assess current holder eligibility and training, confirm that limits and permissions have a valid approval basis, and review statement and reconciliation completeness. Management should inspect unresolved evidence exceptions, overdue disputes, open cancellation cases, dormant cards, recurring subscriptions, and unauthorized access or configuration changes. Significant issues should be reported with quantified exposure, status, owner, due date, and required decision. Certification should identify the period reviewed, the evidence used, the independent reviewer, and any matters that remain open. A certification should not assert that every transaction is compliant merely because the inventory is complete. Where evidence is missing or a critical gate remains unresolved, the report should clearly record the qualification and the remedial action required.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a periodic card-program certification and substantiate each assertion with inventory, access, training, statement, and exception evidence."

Key point:
- Certification must be based on a defined population and verified evidence.
- Unresolved critical controls must be disclosed rather than obscured by a general compliance statement.
43. Q: How should corrective actions and repeated control failures be monitored after an exception has been resolved?
A: Show answer
Corrective action should address the cause of the failure, not merely the individual transaction. Document what happened, the direct cause, the underlying process weakness, the risk and financial impact, the chosen action, the responsible owner, the target date, and the evidence needed to verify effectiveness. Examples include revised approval routing, better form validation, revised cardholder training, an updated alert, a corrected system permission, or a clearer evidence requirement. An independent reviewer should retest the affected control using an appropriate sample. The action should not close merely because a policy has been updated or an employee has been reminded. Monitor subsequent periods to determine whether the failure recurs. Repeated problems may require reassessment of the control design, staffing, system configuration, or management oversight.

Study instruction:
- "Take one recurring control failure from identification through root-cause analysis, remediation, independent retesting, and recurrence monitoring."

Key point:
- Corrective action must address the underlying cause and be independently tested.
- Closure requires evidence that the control works, not just evidence that an action was assigned.
44. Q: How should policy changes and lessons learned be communicated to cardholders and control owners?
A: Show answer
Assign an owner to monitor approved policy revisions, issuer changes, relevant regulatory developments, audit findings, and recurring operational issues. For each change, determine which roles, forms, system settings, spending categories, deadlines, and evidence requirements are affected. Record the source, change rationale, approval, effective date, testing completed, and communication required. Update the controlled SOP and related procedures before relying on the new requirements. Provide targeted training for people whose responsibilities are affected, including cardholders, approvers, Finance reviewers, and administrators. Retain an acknowledgment or completion record where required by policy. Historical transactions should continue to be assessed against the requirements applicable at the relevant time. Do not update limits, deadlines, or legal conclusions on the basis of an unverified email, informal summary, or unsupported assumption.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a proposed policy change from source verification through impact assessment, approval, testing, communication, and version control."

Key point:
- Change management requires verified sources, authorized approval, and clear communication.
- New rules must not be silently applied to historical records or untested system settings.

Source links:
- Bank Indonesia — Regulation on Payment Service Providers
45. Q: What conditions must be satisfied before a corporate card program is considered ready for production use?
A: Show answer
Production readiness requires more than completing the document. The organization must verify its legal entity and applicable requirements, actual issuer and executed agreement, current limits and operating settings, approved internal policies, valid delegation, holder eligibility, training, cardholder acceptance, evidence controls, accounting and tax interfaces, donor requirements, incident procedures, and records protections. System settings should be independently checked against approved specifications, and critical positive and negative test cases should pass. Live issuer status, statement delivery, dispute channels, blocking procedures, and cancellation confirmation should be validated where applicable. The implementation owner should keep an acceptance register that identifies each required gate, the evidence, reviewer, status, and unresolved issues. The organization must also complete any necessary independent document validation, application testing, visual review, and formal authorized adoption. An unresolved critical gate should remain BLOCKED or UNVERIFIED, not be converted into PASS for convenience.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a go-live checklist and determine whether each critical legal, contractual, authority, security, reconciliation, testing, and adoption requirement has verifiable evidence."

Key point:
- A completed document does not prove that the live program is compliant or operational.
- Production readiness requires verified prerequisites, tested controls, and authorized adoption.

Table 8
Cash and Bank Management
1. Q: How should an integrated cash and bank management SOP be designed for an international non-governmental organization (INGO)?
A: Show answer
A cash and bank management SOP should govern the entire cycle of receipts, custody, disbursements, transfers, reconciliations, reporting, and record retention. It should establish the objectives, scope, responsibilities, authorities, supporting documents, completion deadlines, controls, and procedures for handling exceptions. The process must ensure that funds are available for legitimate program requirements, payments reach the correct recipients, and every bank balance can be explained through accounting records. For an INGO, the SOP should also address accounts in multiple countries, foreign currency exposure, donor restrictions, field office requirements, and applicable local regulations. The owner of the financial process should confirm the procedures against actual operations, while management approves material policy decisions. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual can provide a reference for cash management processes, but organizational policies, donor agreements, and applicable laws must still be checked. The supplied DOCX link could not be accessed in this session, so this resource is a structured study reference rather than a verified analysis of that document's contents.

Study instruction:
- "Map one transaction from receipt or payment instruction through accounting, reconciliation, approval, and archiving to identify every SOP stage."
Key point:
- An SOP must connect operational processes, accounting records, and supporting evidence.
- UN guidance is a reference, not a substitute for applicable organizational policies or regulations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
2. Q: What are the main objectives of a cash and bank management policy in an INGO?
A: Show answer
A cash and bank management policy aims to safeguard funds, maintain adequate liquidity, control transactions, and provide management with accurate balance information. It should establish permitted account types, transaction approval authorities, payment limits, receipt procedures, reconciliation schedules, and physical cash controls. The policy must also ensure that funds are used consistently with donor agreements and approved budgets. For organizations operating in multiple countries, requirements concerning currencies, cross-border transfers, electronic banking access, and fraud prevention should be addressed specifically. The Finance Manager is responsible for implementation, while management or delegated authorities approve material policy changes. Effectiveness should be assessed using transaction evidence, reconciliation results, incidents, and liquidity management indicators.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the cash management policy with actual workflows and identify controls that lack an assigned owner or evidence of implementation."
Key point:
- Security, liquidity, compliance, and reliable reporting are core cash management objectives.
- Policies should reflect operational scale, risk exposure, and applicable requirements.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
3. Q: What is the difference between cash, cash equivalents, bank balances, and funds subject to restrictions?
A: Show answer
Cash includes physical currency and demand deposits within the definition and accounting policy applicable to the organization. Cash equivalents are short-term, highly liquid investments that can be converted into known amounts of cash and carry insignificant risk of changes in value, subject to the criteria of the applicable reporting framework. Bank balances are assets that must be reconciled against bank records and accounting records. Funds subject to restrictions require analysis of the nature of the restriction, the organization's access, donor agreements, and disclosure requirements. Donor restrictions do not automatically mean that every affected balance must be classified in a separate accounting category; presentation and disclosure depend on the reporting framework and relevant facts. Finance should document balances unavailable for general operating purposes so management does not interpret total cash as freely available liquidity without further analysis.

Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of operating bank balances, short-term deposits, restricted donor funds, and cash in transit using their characteristics and the applicable accounting policy."
Key point:
- Total cash does not necessarily equal cash freely available for use.
- Classification and disclosure must follow the applicable financial reporting framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
4. Q: How should duties for cash receipts, payments, accounting, and reconciliation be segregated?
A: Show answer
Duties should, where feasible, be separated among cash custodians, payment preparers, document reviewers, approvers, bank transaction operators, journal preparers, and reconciliation preparers. This prevents one person from controlling an entire process without independent oversight. In field offices with limited staff, full segregation may not be practical. In such cases, compensating controls should include independent bank statement reviews, daily transaction reports, additional verification of high-risk payments, and periodic oversight by headquarters. A responsibility matrix should specify who performs each activity, who approves it, who must be consulted, and who receives relevant information. Evidence of each control must be retained so reviewers can establish whether the segregation actually operates in practice.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a RACI matrix for receipts, payments, bookkeeping, and reconciliations, then identify incompatible duties and their compensating controls."
Key point:
- A transaction preparer should not be the sole reviewer and approver.
- Staffing limitations must be offset by documented independent controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
5. Q: How should the opening, maintenance, and closure of bank accounts be controlled?
A: Show answer
Every account opening should be supported by a clear operational need, such as the location of program activities, currency requirements, donor receipts, or local payment arrangements. The request should identify the account's purpose, legal owner, country, currency, authorized signatories, estimated activity, bank fees, and compliance requirements. Account openings and closures must be approved under the organization's delegated authority matrix. Finance should maintain a bank account register recording the bank, account identifier, currency, purpose, active status, signatories, approval date, and corresponding general ledger account. Confidential access credentials must not be stored in a general-purpose register. When an account is no longer needed, outstanding balances and transactions must be resolved, payment instructions lawfully redirected, relevant parties notified, and formal closure confirmed. Dormant accounts also require review because they may continue to incur fees or expose the organization to unauthorized access.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a bank account opening or closure from the request and approval through register updates and corresponding general ledger records."
Key point:
- Every bank account needs a defined purpose, owner, approval, and accounting mapping.
- Unnecessary accounts should be closed through a controlled process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
6. Q: How should bank signatory authority and electronic banking access be managed?
A: Show answer
The organization should assign bank signatories and electronic banking users according to their roles, responsibilities, and authority limits. Requirements for dual authorization, transaction thresholds, and approval levels must follow organizational policy and the bank's available facilities. Finance should document active users, transaction makers, checkers, approvers, and payment releasers. Access rights must be reviewed periodically and updated promptly when employees change roles, leave employment, or lose authorization. Individual accounts must be used according to the bank's security requirements; passwords, authentication codes, and other credentials must never be shared. Changes to beneficiaries, destination accounts, payment limits, and authentication methods require additional controls. Approval evidence and access change records must be retained. Reviewing available access logs helps identify unauthorized activity or changes that cannot be attributed to a legitimate user.

Study instruction:
- "Compare active bank users with the authority matrix and verify evidence that access was revoked for employees who are no longer authorized."
Key point:
- Access rights must follow job responsibilities and the principle of least privilege.
- Changes to access and signatory authority must be approved and traceable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Controls
7. Q: How should physical cash at headquarters and field offices be stored and safeguarded?
A: Show answer
Physical cash should be kept in a locked location accessible only to designated custodians. Cash holding limits should reflect operational needs, deposit frequency, security risks, and local conditions. Receipts and disbursements must be supported by numbered vouchers or another traceable transaction identifier. Personal money must never be mixed with organizational cash. Custodians should count cash at prescribed intervals and submit reports to an independent reviewer. Transporting cash must follow security procedures and approval requirements appropriate to the risk. Any loss, theft, or discrepancy must be reported promptly, and available evidence preserved. The organization should also consider staff safety and non-cash alternatives when transporting substantial amounts of cash creates unnecessary exposure.

Study instruction:
- "Inspect cash storage locations, keyholder lists, cash holding limits, and evidence of surprise cash counts at a field office."
Key point:
- Physical access restrictions and documentation reduce the risk of loss and misuse.
- Cash discrepancies must be reported and investigated without delay.
