- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and Petty Cash Processes
8. Q: How should receipts from donors, customers, and other parties be recorded and classified correctly?
A: Show answer
Each receipt should be supported by the payer's identity, date, amount, currency, receiving account, transfer reference, funding purpose, and available documentation. Finance must determine whether the receipt relates to donor funding, settlement of a receivable, cost recovery, repayment of an advance, an interoffice transfer, or another transaction. Receiving cash does not automatically create revenue; internal transfers and receivable settlements, for example, have different accounting implications. For grants and donor funding, the agreement and applicable revenue recognition framework must be reviewed. Receipts should be reconciled with bank statements and source records before the approved accounting classification and project codes are assigned. Unidentified receipts should be held in an appropriate suspense account and investigated rather than automatically recognized as revenue without sufficient evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of donor disbursements, receivable settlements, advance repayments, and internal transfers, and explain their supporting evidence and journal implications."
Key point:
- Bank receipts must be identified before their accounting classification is determined.
- Cash inflows and revenue recognition are separate concepts.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable
9. Q: How should bank receipts whose source or purpose is unknown be handled?
A: Show answer
Unidentified receipts should be recorded in an exception register containing the date, value, currency, bank reference, receiving account, and investigation status. Finance should inspect transfer references, donor notifications, receivable records, bank communications, and transactions associated with relevant offices or projects. When evidence is insufficient, the receipt should be recorded using an approved suspense or other appropriate account rather than being automatically recognized as revenue. The assigned owner should contact the relevant parties through official channels and retain the responses received. The register should record the item's age, next action, responsible person, and escalation deadline. Once the source is confirmed, the classification should be corrected through an approved journal and the suspense balance cleared. Old or material items must be reported to management to prevent unexplained balances from being carried forward indefinitely.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an unidentified receipts register and trace selected items from the bank statement to confirmed identification and the final journal entry."
Key point:
- Unidentified receipts require interim treatment and clearly assigned follow-up.
- Suspense accounts must not become permanent repositories for uninvestigated balances.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 13 — Accounts Receivable and Unapplied Cash
10. Q: How should donor-restricted funds be controlled in bank accounts and financial reports?
A: Show answer
The organization should record donor restrictions, permitted expenditure periods, eligible cost categories, refund requirements, and the projects entitled to use the funds. A dedicated bank account may be appropriate or mandatory where the agreement or applicable requirements demand it, but separate accounts are not automatically required for every grant. If one account holds funds from multiple sources, the accounting system must still distinguish balances and transactions using donor, project, and funding-condition codes. Finance must ensure that payments do not violate grant conditions. Bank balances should be reconciled with the general ledger and donor funding schedules. Reports must distinguish physically available cash from funds that cannot contractually or operationally be used for other purposes. The accounting treatment of revenue and liabilities must be determined under the applicable standards, grant terms, and organizational policies.

Study instruction:
- "Compare bank balances, project-level ledger balances, and donor agreements to confirm that funding restrictions are respected."
Key point:
- Donor funds must be traceable to their usage conditions and related projects.
- Separate bank accounts and separate accounting classifications are not necessarily the same thing.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
11. Q: What controls should be completed before payments to suppliers, staff, and program partners are authorized?
A: Show answer
Before payment, Finance should verify that the obligation or claim is legitimate, supporting documents are complete, the amount is correct, budget availability has been confirmed, donor and project codes are appropriate, and approval has been obtained from an authorized person. Supplier documentation may include invoices, purchase orders, evidence of goods or services received, and verified beneficiary bank details. Requirements for staff and partners depend on the payment type, such as an expense claim, advance, or funding disbursement. The reviewer should check for duplicates, previous payments, changes to bank details, and consistency between gross amounts, deductions, and net amounts. Payment instructions must reference the relevant transaction and supporting documents. Non-compliant payments should be returned for completion or escalated instead of being processed merely because a program deadline is approaching.

Study instruction:
- "Select payment samples and examine the links between source documents, beneficiary verification, authorization, bank instructions, and accounting entries."
Key point:
- Payment authorization must be based on sufficient evidence and valid authority.
- Beneficiary and duplicate checks are as important as checking the amount.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
12. Q: How should high-value payments and bulk payments be controlled?
A: Show answer
Bulk payments must originate from a transaction list that has already been checked and approved. Finance should confirm that the payment file uses the correct bank format, the number of transactions and total amount match the approved list, and no beneficiary or bank account has changed without verification. Where practical, the person preparing the file should be different from the reviewer or approver. Before submission, check for duplicates, missing account details, unusual amounts, currency inconsistencies, and authorization limits. After processing, compare each transaction's status with the bank's report to distinguish successful, rejected, pending, or cancelled payments. The total value of a generated payment file must not be assumed to equal the amount actually paid. All differences must be investigated and reconciled before the payment cycle is closed.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a bulk payment file containing valid transactions, duplicates, an incorrect bank account, and a rejected payment to test pre-submission and post-submission controls."
Key point:
- Bulk payment controls must examine both the entire file and individual transaction outcomes.
- Successful payment status must be supported by available bank information.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and Payment Processes
13. Q: How can emergency payments at field offices be processed without abandoning financial controls?
A: Show answer
The SOP should define qualifying emergencies, authorized approvers, value limits, minimum documentation, and post-payment review procedures. Emergencies must not become a general justification for bypassing beneficiary verification, transaction evidence, or accounting records. If prior approval is impossible, the organization must establish a valid alternative authorization mechanism and require prompt subsequent review. Finance should document the reason for urgency, the consequences of delaying payment, the amount, beneficiary, project, available evidence, outstanding documents, and responsible person. Every deviation from normal procedures should be recorded in an exception register. Management should assess whether emergency treatment was genuinely necessary and whether repeated use indicates weaknesses in cash planning or procurement processes.

Study instruction:
- "Develop an emergency payment scenario in a high-risk operating area and identify minimum controls, authorization, required evidence, and post-transaction review."
Key point:
- Emergency procedures must explicitly control exceptions rather than eliminate controls.
- Every deviation needs a documented rationale, authorization, and closure process.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Control Processes
14. Q: How should transfers between bank accounts and organizational units be reconciled?
A: Show answer
A transfer should have an approved instruction, source and destination account details, amount, currency, date, purpose, and consistent transaction reference. Finance must distinguish transfers between accounts owned by the same entity from payments to external parties. An internal transfer is not automatically revenue or expense; its accounting must reflect the movement of cash and, where relevant, balances between offices or entities. Both sides of the transfer should be recorded and settlement timing monitored. At period-end, amounts debited from one account but not yet credited to the other should be listed as transfers in transit. Foreign currency transfers may also create exchange differences or bank fees requiring appropriate accounting treatment. Reconciliation must confirm that both sides ultimately agree and that transfers have neither been recorded twice nor omitted from the general ledger.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one interbank transfer from the approved instruction through both account movements, bank fees, timing differences, and the final journal entries."
Key point:
- Internal transfers must be matched between the source and destination accounts.
- Timing differences must be monitored as reconciliation items.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank-to-Bank Transfers
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reconciliation
15. Q: How should an effective bank reconciliation be performed?
A: Show answer
Bank reconciliation compares bank statement movements and balances with the organization's cash records in the general ledger or cashbook for the same period. The process begins by confirming that all bank statements are complete and that the opening balance, period, currency, and ledger account are correct. Transactions are then matched using references, dates, amounts, payees, payers, and descriptions. Matched items are marked as cleared, while unmatched transactions enter an investigation list. Differences may arise from payments in transit, deposits not yet reflected by the bank, bank fees, interest, bookkeeping errors, unauthorized transactions, or cut-off issues. Adjusting journals should only be prepared once the cause and accounting basis are understood and the required approval has been obtained. The completed reconciliation must be independently reviewed and retained with supporting evidence and the list of outstanding items.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile one bank account for one month and trace every difference through its cause, any necessary journal entry, and reviewer approval."
Key point:
- The difference between the bank balance and book balance must be explained through documented reconciliation.
- Discrepancies must be investigated rather than forced to agree.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Adjustment
16. Q: What is the difference between automated matching and manual reconciliation in a financial system?
A: Show answer
Automated matching uses configured rules, such as transaction references, amounts, dates, or beneficiary identifiers, to link bank movements with operational records. It is efficient for consistent, structured transactions, but its quality depends on source data and the matching rules. Manual reconciliation is needed when references are incomplete, bank fees affect amounts, grouped transactions occur, or a payment cannot be uniquely matched. Users should not accept an automated match merely because the system labels a transaction as matched. High-value transactions, changes to bank details, and unusual patterns require additional scrutiny according to risk. The organization should test automated rules against samples, review false matches, and document reasons for manual matches and adjustments. Configuration changes must be tested and approved before implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Test automated matching against variations in dates, amounts, references, and duplicate transactions to identify safe matching conditions and cases requiring manual review."
Key point:
- Automation reduces routine work but does not replace professional judgment over exceptions.
- Matching rules must be tested, and configuration changes must be controlled.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
17. Q: How can transactions near month-end be assigned to the correct accounting period?
A: Show answer
Finance must evaluate the transaction date, date processed by the bank, date of receipt or delivery, date the obligation is recognized, and the applicable accounting requirements. A payment appearing on the following month's bank statement is not necessarily an expense of the following month; the transaction's substance and recognition timing must be analyzed. Similarly, a deposit that has not yet appeared on the bank statement may qualify as a deposit in transit when supported by appropriate evidence. Prepare a cut-off schedule covering significant transactions before and after the closing date, including bank references, supporting documents, accounting treatment, and the rationale for period classification. Accruals and other adjustments must not be recorded merely to make the bank balance equal the ledger. Reviewers should confirm that each journal has an adequate basis, the correct accounting period, and verifiable supporting evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Select transactions from several days before and after month-end and determine their accounting periods using supporting documentation and applicable policies."
Key point:
- Bank processing dates and accounting recognition periods are not always the same.
- Cut-off decisions require supporting evidence and a clear rationale.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Adjustments
18. Q: How should unresolved bank reconciliation items be monitored and cleared?
A: Show answer
Every outstanding item should have an origin date, bank account, amount, currency, description, provisional cause, assigned owner, target resolution date, and status. Items should be categorized by type, age, materiality, and risk so that significant transactions or suspected unauthorized activity receive priority even when recently identified. Finance should trace each item to bank evidence, internal documentation, the relevant counterparty, or another reliable source. Long-outstanding items must be escalated to management according to defined thresholds. Closure requires supporting evidence, such as a subsequent bank movement, counterparty confirmation, approved correcting journal, or completed administrative procedure. Moving an item into the next reporting period does not mean it has been resolved. Outstanding balances must remain visible in reconciliation reports so reviewers can assess the risks that persist.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an aging report for outstanding reconciliation items and prioritize investigations by age, value, type, and risk."
Key point:
- Outstanding items require an owner, target date, and evidence of resolution.
- Age and materiality should guide escalation rather than chronological order alone.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation and Exception Items
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Reconciliation
19. Q: How should bank fees, interest, and transfer charges be accounted for?
A: Show answer
Bank fees and interest must be identified from bank statements and recorded according to their substance and the applicable accounting policy. Administrative charges, transfer fees, service fees, and currency conversion charges may require different accounts or specific treatment under organizational policy. Interest received should be assessed in light of the account's terms, donor requirements, and applicable regulations, including whether it constitutes organizational income or is subject to restrictions. Finance must reconcile amounts appearing in bank statements with general ledger entries and investigate charges or interest that differ from normal patterns. Bank charges should not be ignored solely because they are small, since accumulated fees can become material and affect the accuracy of balances. Unusual charges should be confirmed with the bank before final adjustments are made.

Study instruction:
- "Review a bank statement, identify fees, interest, and transfers requiring journal entries, and match them to the general ledger."
Key point:
- Bank fees and interest must be reconciled and classified according to their nature.
- Donor requirements and organizational policies may affect the treatment of interest.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Entries
20. Q: How should cash and cheque receipts be processed until they appear in the bank account?
A: Show answer
Each cash or cheque receipt should be supported by a receipt record, payer identity, date, amount, currency, payment purpose, and receiving staff member. Receipts should be recorded promptly and kept securely until deposited. Where practicable, the person receiving cash should not be the only person responsible for depositing, recording, and reconciling it. Deposits must be supported by deposit slips or electronic evidence and matched with transactions appearing on the bank statement. For cheques that have not cleared, the organization should monitor their status and handle rejection or cancellation according to established procedures. Differences between receipt records, deposits, and bank movements must remain on the outstanding item list until their resolution is evidenced.

Study instruction:
- "Trace a sample cash or cheque receipt from the receipt record through bank deposit, cashbook entry, and reconciliation."
Key point:
- Every receipt must have a complete record and traceable deposit evidence.
- Cash received is not necessarily immediately available in the bank account.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Cash Receipts
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash and Imprest Processes
21. Q: How should petty cash and imprest funds be controlled?
A: Show answer
Petty cash is used for certain expenditures permitted by organizational policy, while an imprest arrangement requires accountability for the fund according to its approved amount and conditions. The organization should specify the fund custodian, holding limit, permitted expenditure categories, transaction limits, supporting documents, approvals, and counting schedule. Each disbursement requires valid evidence and a clearly documented purpose. Physical cash plus supported expenditures not yet reimbursed should reconcile to the fund balance under the relevant arrangement. Replenishment must be based on an expense report that has been reviewed, not merely on a verbal request. Finance should examine balances, documents, project classifications, and possible transaction splitting intended to avoid approval limits. Any discrepancy must be investigated and resolved through authorized procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Calculate the petty cash balance, total supported expenditure, and amount eligible for replenishment, then document the reconciliation and approval."
Key point:
- Replenishment must be based on documented and reviewed expenditure.
- Petty cash requires monitoring of physical balances, supporting evidence, and reconciliation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash and Imprest Accounts
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
22. Q: How should surprise cash counts and investigations of cash discrepancies be conducted?
A: Show answer
Surprise cash counts should be performed by someone independent of the custodian, with records of the date, location, custodian, denominations, physical total, and recorded balance. All supported expenditures not yet processed must be identified so the physical count can be compared correctly with the records. A discrepancy must not be closed immediately through a journal entry without investigation. The reviewer should trace receipts, payments, deposits, reimbursements, and potentially unrecorded transactions. The count results should be signed by relevant parties and reported according to escalation thresholds. Where loss, manipulation, or misuse is suspected, the organization must follow its incident reporting procedures and preserve evidence for investigation. Trends in discrepancies should also be analyzed to identify underlying process weaknesses.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a surprise cash count with a discrepancy and document the investigation, approval of any correction, and closure evidence."
Key point:
- Independent checks help detect discrepancies that routine processes may miss.
- Differences must be investigated before accounting adjustments are recorded.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Petty Cash Control
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Adjustment Controls
23. Q: What fraud risks are most relevant to INGO cash and bank management, and how should they be mitigated?
A: Show answer
Common risks include payments to fraudulent accounts, unauthorized changes to supplier bank details, fictitious invoices, duplicate payments, misuse of banking credentials, collusion, diversion of donor funds, and manipulation of reconciliations. Mitigation measures include independent verification of bank detail changes, restricted access, layered approvals, document matching, investigation of unusual transactions, and independent reconciliation. Urgent requests received by email or messaging must be verified through an established official channel, not by using a new contact number supplied in the request. The organization should define procedures for reporting suspected fraud, preserving evidence, conducting investigations, and communicating with relevant authorities according to policy. Controls should address actual risks and be reassessed after incidents so that the same weaknesses do not recur.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a scenario involving a fraudulent supplier bank change request and identify the verification controls that would prevent an unauthorized payment."
Key point:
- Changes to payment beneficiaries must be verified independently of the payment request.
- Access restrictions, segregation of duties, and transaction monitoring must work together.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Controls
24. Q: How does cash flow forecasting help an INGO plan funding requirements and avoid liquidity shortages?
A: Show answer
A cash flow forecast estimates receipts and payments by date, amount, currency, funding source, and transaction certainty. It should include payroll, suppliers, implementing partners, travel, rent, taxes, and program expenditure while considering donor disbursement schedules. Finance can prepare weekly and monthly forecasts, then compare them with actual results to assess accuracy. Receipts that are only planned or not yet confirmed must not be treated as equivalent to funds already available. Forecasts should also distinguish unrestricted available funds from restricted balances. When material differences arise, the responsible owner must explain the cause and update the forecast. Management uses this information to schedule legitimate payments, escalate funding shortfalls, and avoid decisions that could jeopardize program implementation.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a 13-week cash flow forecast and compare it with actual cash flows to identify the causes of forecast errors."
Key point:
- Cash forecasts must reflect timing, value, certainty, and restrictions on funds.
- Differences between forecast and actual cash flows require periodic analysis.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Position and Liquidity Forecasting
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Cash Flow Statement and Financial Disclosures
25. Q: What is the difference between a daily cash position report and a cash flow forecast?
A: Show answer
A cash position report describes balances and transactions as of a specific date based on available bank information and accounting records. A cash flow forecast estimates receipts and payments over a future period. The position report should identify balances by account and currency, restricted funds, transfers in transit, and unidentified items. The forecast adds expected payments, anticipated receipts, relevant commitments, and assumptions about donor disbursement timing. These reports complement each other: a high balance today does not guarantee the ability to meet next week's obligations if the funds are restricted or substantial payments are already scheduled. Finance should define the reporting date, data sources, owner, update frequency, and review of assumptions. Material differences must be followed up before they affect program operations.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a cash position report and cash flow forecast for the same date, then explain why currently available balances may differ from future payment capacity."
Key point:
- A cash position report describes current conditions; a forecast estimates future movements.
- Decisions must consider donor restrictions and scheduled payments.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Position and Liquidity Forecast Reports
26. Q: How should transactions and balances involving multiple currencies be reconciled and recorded?
A: Show answer
Each transaction should retain its original currency amount, functional or reporting currency amount, transaction date, exchange rate, and rate source in accordance with applicable policy and requirements. Foreign currency bank accounts should be reconciled in the account currency so transactions and balances can be matched with the bank statement. The balances should then be translated as required by the applicable financial reporting rules. The transaction rate, reporting-date rate, and settlement rate may differ. Resulting differences should be analyzed and recognized as exchange differences where appropriate under the reporting framework. Finance must control exchange rate sources, formulas, effective dates, and rounding rules. Rates must not be changed manually without adequate documentation and approval.

Study instruction:
- "Reconcile a foreign currency bank account and distinguish original transaction amounts, functional currency postings, bank balances, and reporting-date exchange differences."
Key point:
- Reconciliation in the account currency and translation for reporting are separate processes.
- Exchange rate sources and calculations must be reproducible.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Financial Statement Presentation and Disclosures
27. Q: How should cash balances be allocated and reported by donor, project, country, and cost center?
A: Show answer
The organization should use a consistent coding structure to link bank transactions to funding sources, projects, locations, activities, and responsible units. Every receipt and payment should be classified using appropriate supporting documentation and allocation policies. A bank account alone should not determine the project code, because one account may serve several projects and one project may use several accounts. Finance must ensure that allocations do not violate donor agreements or lead to duplicate charging. Reconciliation should compare bank transactions, the general ledger, project registers, and relevant donor reports. Mapping errors must be corrected through approved procedures without eliminating the change history. Reports should also identify remaining funds and payment commitments using consistent definitions to support decision-making.

Study instruction:
- "Select transactions from a bank account holding funds from multiple sources and trace their allocation to project codes and related financial reports."
Key point:
- Bank accounts and project funding sources do not necessarily have a one-to-one relationship.
- Allocations require correct coding, a documented basis, and reconciliation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Coding
28. Q: How do the cashbook, cash and bank general ledger accounts, and bank statements differ?
A: Show answer
A cashbook is an operational record of the organization's receipts and payments, while the general ledger classifies transactions into accounting accounts. A bank statement is the bank's record of account movements and balances. The three sources may temporarily differ because of bank processing delays, unrecorded transactions, unrecognized fees, or errors. Finance must understand each source's purpose and reconcile them periodically. Reconciliation must not eliminate differences merely to make all three reports show identical totals. Every adjusting journal requires an explanation and supporting evidence. Even when the cashbook forms part of an integrated accounting system, the organization must ensure that account mapping, bank references, and transaction statuses produce accurate general ledger balances. This documentation helps auditors trace financial statement balances back to bank evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the same transactions across a bank statement, cashbook, and general ledger, then classify the causes of each difference."
Key point:
- Each source has a different function and must be linked through reconciliation.
- Differences require evidence-based explanations rather than unsupported adjustments.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger
29. Q: What must be completed during month-end closing for cash and bank accounts?
A: Show answer
Month-end closing must ensure that complete bank statements are available for every account, receipts and payments have been identified, bank reconciliations are complete, bank fees and interest are recorded, transfers in transit have been traced, and outstanding items have been reported. Finance should compare reconciled balances with the general ledger and assess transactions requiring period adjustments. Foreign currency balances must be handled under the applicable translation policy. Petty cash and imprest funds should be reconciled, while donor-restricted balances must agree with funding registers. Reviewers must verify that every account in the official register has been examined and that no reconciliation is missing. The closing pack should also contain exception lists, follow-up status, completion dates, and approvals. After closing, material changes must follow controlled post-close journal procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Use a month-end checklist to test one complete period and confirm that every account has a reconciliation, reviewer, and documented follow-up for outstanding balances."
Key point:
- Closing must establish the completeness of all accounts and the accuracy of ledger balances.
- Unresolved balances must be reported to the appropriate authority.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Period-End Adjustment
30. Q: How should cash and cash equivalents be presented and disclosed in financial statements?
A: Show answer
Cash and cash equivalents must be presented in accordance with the financial reporting framework adopted by the organization. Finance must ensure that reported balances can be traced to the general ledger, bank reconciliations, and physical cash records. Balances unavailable for general use or subject to material restrictions must be assessed for appropriate disclosure. The organization should distinguish contractual restrictions, donor restrictions, pledged balances, blocked accounts, and internal operating requirements. Disclosures should provide relevant information about the amount and nature of restrictions in accordance with applicable requirements. The organization must not claim that all cash is available merely because the funds appear in bank accounts. Foreign currency balances and cash equivalent classifications should also be reviewed consistently with accounting policy and materiality considerations.

Study instruction:
- "Trace financial statement cash balances to the ledger and bank statements, then identify restricted balances requiring a disclosure assessment."
Key point:
- Cash recognized in the accounts is not necessarily freely available for every purpose.
- Presentation and disclosure must follow the applicable reporting framework.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 16 — Presentation of Financial Statements and Disclosures
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
31. Q: How should the completeness of source data for bank reconciliation be verified?
A: Show answer
Finance should use the official bank account register to verify that the required statement has been received for every account and period. Incoming data should be checked for account identity, currency, start and end dates, opening balance, closing balance, and completeness of pages or transactions. For electronic files, row counts and control totals should be compared with bank source information where available. Corrupt, truncated, or differently formatted files must be flagged before processing. Source data must not be overwritten by revised versions when the original is needed as evidence. If a statement is delayed, the assigned owner should contact the bank through official channels and document the status and effect on closing. Data must not be considered complete simply because the import process succeeded.

Study instruction:
- "Test statement completeness for all accounts by matching the account register, file period, control balances, and transaction population."
Key point:
- Source completeness must be verified before reconciliation begins.
- A successful file import does not prove that every transaction has been loaded.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Electronic Bank Statements
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Data Controls
32. Q: How do staff and partner advance disbursements and repayments affect cash and bank management?
A: Show answer
Paying an advance reduces cash or bank balances and establishes an advance or receivable balance according to the transaction's nature. The payment does not automatically become a program expense if expense recognition depends on eligible expenditure or accountability documents. Finance should record the recipient, purpose, project, amount, disbursement date, accountability deadline, and outstanding balance. Once supporting documents are received, eligible expenditure is recognized under the applicable policy, while any remaining amount must be repaid or otherwise resolved through approved procedures. Advance repayments must be matched to bank records and reduce the appropriate outstanding balance. Open advance reports should be linked to cash forecasts so new disbursements do not overlook unresolved previous advances. For partners, agreement requirements and expenditure reports must be reviewed before balances are cleared.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one advance from the request and bank transfer through accountability documents, expense recognition, repayment of unused funds, and final balance clearance."
Key point:
- An advance disbursement is a cash flow that must be distinguished from recognition of eligible expenditure.
- Advances must remain monitored until accountability and settlement are complete.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable and Disbursement Processes
33. Q: How should digital wallets, mobile money, and electronic payment providers be incorporated into the cash and bank SOP?
A: Show answer
Electronic payment instruments must be accounted for according to their substance, ownership of balances, provider terms, and organizational policies. Finance should identify who controls each account, how authorization works, which transaction reports are available, what service fees apply, what limits exist, and how balances are withdrawn or settled. Balances held by payment providers must not be ignored merely because they are not held at a traditional bank. Reconciliation should compare provider reports, related bank movements, operational transactions, and general ledger records. The organization must also control authentication security, device restrictions, telephone number changes, and access recovery. In locations with limited connectivity, offline transaction evidence or delayed confirmations must be handled using approved controls and reviewed when systems become available again.

Study instruction:
- "Map the recording process for mobile money payments and determine the supporting evidence, clearing accounts, service fees, and reconciliation procedures."
Key point:
- Electronic instruments still require balance controls, authorization, evidence, and reconciliation.
- The payment method does not remove the need for complete accounting records.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
34. Q: How can centralized banking or cash pooling affect an INGO's financial controls?
A: Show answer
Centralized cash management can improve visibility over balances and help coordinate payments across countries or offices. However, its design must consider account ownership, legal relationships between entities, ownership of funds, donor restrictions, cross-border transfer risks, and local requirements. Physically pooling balances does not mean that funds from every project can be used for any purpose. Each transfer must have a documented basis, approval, amount, currency, source and destination accounts, and appropriate accounting entries. The organization should evaluate transfer delays, bank fees, foreign exchange movements, and field office access to operating funds. The policy must specify whether pooling is permitted, which accounts participate, transaction limits, and how inter-entity balances are reconciled. Implementation requires assessment under relevant legal, tax, and treasury requirements.

Study instruction:
- "Map cash flows under a centralized banking arrangement and identify approval requirements, inter-entity accounting, donor restrictions, and liquidity risks."
Key point:
- Consolidated cash visibility does not automatically eliminate restrictions on funds.
- Transfers must comply with legal structures, organizational policies, and funding conditions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Inter-Entity Reconciliation
35. Q: How should changes to beneficiary bank accounts, vendor details, or payment instructions be verified?
A: Show answer
Changes to destination bank accounts are high-risk because they can be used to divert funds fraudulently. Requests must be verified through an independent channel already recorded in the master data or existing contract. Finance must not rely solely on an email, message, or new telephone number supplied with the change request. The master data custodian should record the request date, requester identity, verification evidence, approver, and effective date. Payments must use formally approved and updated beneficiary information. Last-minute changes, unusual confidentiality requests, or mismatches between account names and beneficiary details should trigger additional review. If fraud is suspected, the process must be stopped within the applicable authority, the incident reported, and communications preserved as evidence.

Study instruction:
- "Test the vendor bank account change process using a fraudulent request scenario and confirm that no payment-affecting change can occur without independent verification."
Key point:
- Bank account changes must be verified through a trusted independent channel.
- Payments must use approved beneficiary master data.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 14 — Accounts Payable
36. Q: How should bank confirmations and supporting evidence be prepared for an audit?
A: Show answer
An audit package should include the bank account register, complete bank statements, account reconciliations, general ledger balances, outstanding item lists, evidence of transfers in transit, adjusting journals, and reviewer sign-offs. When an auditor requests bank confirmation, the request should follow an approved independent process that meets audit requirements and organizational policy. Finance must ensure that confirmed balances correspond to the relevant reporting date and that differences have been analyzed. Bank signatory and access evidence may also be needed to test authorization. Material outstanding items should be explained with relevant supporting evidence rather than comments alone. The package must use controlled copies so figures and documents provided to auditors agree with the approved version. Audit questions and responses affecting balances should be tracked until resolved.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare an audit package for one bank account and verify that each balance can be traced to bank evidence, ledger records, reconciliations, journals, and approvals."
Key point:
- Audit evidence must link reported balances to bank sources and accounting records.
- Explanations of outstanding items require verifiable supporting evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation
37. Q: How should cash and bank transaction archives be organized for traceability and retention compliance?
A: Show answer
Records should be organized consistently by year, period, bank account, transaction type, and a standardized reference or batch number. Evidence should link payment instructions, source documents, approvals, bank confirmations, journals, reconciliations, and exception follow-up. Original source files must not be overwritten by corrected versions without retaining the change history. File names should contain helpful search information, such as the period, document type, account or project, and version. Access must be restricted to protect bank information and beneficiary details. Retention periods must be determined according to applicable law, donor contracts, audit requirements, and organizational policies. Backups should be tested through periodic recovery exercises to ensure that records can actually be opened. Final approved records must be kept separately from working documents still being processed.

Study instruction:
- "Select one payment and demonstrate that its source documents, approval, bank movement, journal, reconciliation, and archive can be located using a consistent reference."
Key point:
- Archives must preserve evidence integrity and support two-way transaction tracing.
- Retention, access, versioning, and backups require formal controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Supporting Records
38. Q: How should cash and bank management reports be designed to support operational decisions?
A: Show answer
Management reports should display balances by account and currency, unrestricted cash available for use, restricted funds, expected receipts, scheduled payments, outstanding reconciliation items, and future liquidity requirements. Reports should also explain material changes from the previous period and highlight risks requiring management decisions, such as funding shortages, delayed donor receipts, unidentified balances, or significant payments awaiting completion. Each indicator needs a definition, source, reporting date, owner, and update frequency. Preliminary figures must be distinguished from reconciled final figures. The Finance Manager should review the reasonableness of the information and ensure that restricted balances are not treated as freely available funds. A useful report does more than show total cash: it explains availability, utilization, changes, and actions required.

Study instruction:
- "Design a cash position report showing balances by account, currency, donor restriction, outstanding item, and projected payment requirement."
Key point:
- Cash reports should help management understand available balances and liquidity risks.
- Each indicator needs a clear definition and supporting evidence.

- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Position and Liquidity Forecast Reports

39. Q: How can Excel and Power Query automate bank reconciliation without weakening financial controls?
A: Show answer
Power Query can import bank statement and cashbook data, standardize date and number formats, normalize references and currencies, and combine the data into a structured reconciliation model. Excel can calculate differences and produce lists of candidate matches and unresolved transactions. Matching rules may use combinations of amount, date, reference, and payee, but uncertain candidates must remain subject to human review. Source data should be preserved, and transformation results should be tested using independently calculated samples. Finance should compare row counts and monetary control totals before and after transformation to detect missing or duplicate data. Automation must not determine accounting classifications requiring professional judgment. Query changes should be tested, approved, and version-controlled before reports are used as final outputs.

Study instruction:
- "Build a basic reconciliation query and test its output against manually reconciled samples, including normal transactions, duplicates, and transactions with insufficient references."
Key point:
- Power Query improves repeatability and data standardization.
- Matching results require validation and review of exceptions.
Source links:
- What Is Power Query? — Microsoft Learn
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
40. Q: How can VBA automate cash and bank reports and maintain an audit trail?
A: Show answer
VBA can prepare reconciliation packages, update completion statuses, produce exception lists, export reports, and write process records to a log. Every automation must have a specification, owner, input conditions, validation rules, error handling, and defined success criteria. Macros should not delete unmatched transactions, overwrite source data, or post journals without the appropriate approval controls. Logs should capture the timestamp, user, period, workbook version, data sources, processing result, and number of exceptions. Access to modify code must be restricted. Testing should cover blank inputs, missing files, unexpected formats, and transactions producing unreasonable results. Automated outputs may be used for final reporting only after review under the organization's established procedures.

Study instruction:
- "Design an automated reconciliation reporting process and test error handling without deleting source transactions or bypassing approval requirements."
Key point:
- VBA automates repetitive steps but does not replace accounting responsibility.
- Code, logs, access, and automated outputs must be controlled and tested.
Source links:
- Excel Visual Basic for Applications Reference — Microsoft Learn
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Workflows
41. Q: How should an exception register and escalation process be established for cash and bank management?
A: Show answer
The exception register should record the issue identifier, detection date, bank account, value, risk category, description, provisional cause, required evidence, owner, reviewer, deadline, and status. Categories may include rejected payments, unidentified receipts, reconciliation differences, unauthorized bank detail changes, delayed statements, transfers in transit, and system access problems. Priorities should consider materiality, fraud risk, reporting deadlines, and operational impact. Escalation to the Finance Manager or management should follow approved thresholds. An item may be marked resolved only after its cause is substantiated, the necessary action is completed, and closure evidence is available. Recurring issues should be analyzed to determine whether their causes lie in source data, control weaknesses, segregation of duties, or system configuration. The register provides a basis for monitoring risk across reporting periods.

Study instruction:
- "Classify bank reconciliation exceptions by risk and determine who may resolve, approve, and independently verify the closure of each category."
Key point:
- Exceptions require prioritization, ownership, deadlines, and closure evidence.
- Recurring problems should lead to process improvements rather than repeated individual corrections alone.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation and Exception Items
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and Adjustment
42. Q: How should business continuity procedures address the unavailability of banking systems, internet access, or finance staff?
A: Show answer
Continuity procedures should identify critical functions, backup personnel, approved communication channels, permitted alternative payment methods, and escalation arrangements. The organization must specify when alternatives may be used, who authorizes them, and how normal controls are restored after the disruption. Evidence of the incident should record the start time, affected system or account, pending transactions, recovery attempts, and temporary decisions. Internet outages do not automatically justify the use of personal bank accounts or shared banking credentials. Alternative methods must continue to comply with authorization requirements, security policies, local regulations, and donor restrictions. After systems recover, all temporary transactions must be recorded, matched with bank movements, and independently reviewed. Backup procedures should be tested periodically through simulations to ensure they can be executed during an actual disruption.

Study instruction:
- "Simulate a banking access outage near a payment deadline and test escalation, delegated authority, temporary records, and post-recovery reconciliation."
Key point:
- Business continuity does not remove authorization and transaction evidence requirements.
- Alternative transactions must be reconciled after normal systems are restored.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Control Processes
43. Q: What should an INGO consider when making international transfers or cross-border payments?
A: Show answer
Cross-border transfers require verification of the beneficiary's identity, payment purpose, currency, account details, transfer charges, exchange rate, authorization, and supporting documentation. Finance must check applicable requirements in the sending and receiving countries, bank requirements, reporting obligations, and relevant sanctions or restrictions. Payments to local organizations, implementing partners, or service providers must be consistent with valid agreements and transaction purposes. The amount sent, amount received, intermediary bank charges, and exchange differences must be monitored so discrepancies can be explained. When a transfer is delayed or rejected, the responsible officer should obtain information from the bank and determine the next steps without creating a duplicate payment. The policy should establish minimum documentation, approval authority, escalation thresholds, and reconciliation procedures until the funds are received or returned.

Study instruction:
- "Trace one international transfer from approval to receipt or return, including intermediary charges, foreign currency amounts, and compliance with relevant requirements."
Key point:
- Cross-border transfers require evidence, authorization, and monitoring through final settlement.
- Local regulations and bank requirements must be checked for every relevant operating jurisdiction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Transaction Controls
44. Q: How should training, updates, and periodic reviews of the cash and bank SOP be managed?
A: Show answer
The organization should appoint an SOP owner responsible for reviewing whether the procedure remains consistent with delegated authority, banking systems, accounting policies, and operational needs. Training should cover actual workflows, required documents, authorization, reconciliation, exception handling, and security procedures. New staff and designated deputies must understand their responsibilities before receiving the appropriate system access. Changes to bank accounts, systems, organizational structures, or donor requirements may trigger an unscheduled review. Every revision should have a version number, effective date, change summary, approval, and evidence that users were informed. Feedback from field offices, incidents, audit findings, and recurring reconciliation issues should be used to improve controls. Previous SOP versions must be retained in accordance with the organization's records policy so historical decisions remain explainable.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the approved SOP with actual practice, document process changes, and prepare a training plan and approval record for the revised procedure."
Key point:
- The SOP must reflect the processes, systems, and authorities actually in force.
- Revisions must be tested, approved, communicated, and retained in the control history.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Controls
45. Q: How can the effectiveness of the cash and bank SOP be tested through walkthroughs and performance indicators?
A: Show answer
A walkthrough should trace selected transactions from beginning to end, including source documents, receipts or payment instructions, verification, authorization, bank movements, journals, reconciliations, review, and archiving. Testing must establish not only whether a procedure exists but also whether the control is performed by the appropriate person and produces sufficient evidence. Performance indicators may include the percentage of reconciliations completed on time, outstanding item counts, average item age, monthly closing time, unidentified transaction volumes, and payment error frequency. Each indicator needs a definition, source, measurement period, owner, target, and escalation rule. Results should be compared across periods and offices to identify recurring weaknesses. Findings must lead to improvement plans with assigned owners, deadlines, and evidence of completion. An SOP is effective because testing supports its implementation and performance, not merely because the document has been approved.

Study instruction:
- "Walk through samples of receipts, payments, and transfers; calculate reconciliation timeliness and outstanding item aging, then document improvement recommendations."
Key point:
- Walkthroughs test actual implementation, while performance indicators reveal process trends.
- Improvements must have an owner, deadline, and verification of results.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger, Reconciliation and Adjustment
