Table 9
Bank Reconciliation Controls and Procedures
1. Q: How should an NGO perform a complete bank reconciliation from source data to final approval?
A: Show answer
Bank reconciliation is a documented comparison of authentic bank statement records with the corresponding general ledger (GL) cash account for the same entity, bank account, currency, and reporting period. The objective is to establish that recorded cash is complete, differences are explained, corrections are properly authorized, and the closing balance is supported by evidence. The process should cover account population, statement retrieval, GL extraction, opening-balance continuity, transaction matching, reconciling-item schedules, journal adjustments, independent review, approval, and archiving. A zero residual is necessary but insufficient: source authenticity, transaction completeness, valid classification, and independent review must also pass. The supplied SOP is explicitly a fictional demonstration, so actual organizational procedures and authorities must be confirmed before operational use.

Study instruction:
- Trace one completed reconciliation from original bank statement through ledger matching, adjustments, independent review, and final archive.
Key point:
- Reconciliation requires both arithmetic agreement and reliable supporting evidence.
- A fictional example is not evidence of actual implementation or approval.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
2. Q: What are the main objectives and boundaries of a bank reconciliation SOP?
A: Show answer
The SOP should establish a repeatable control for verifying bank cash against ledger records, identifying timing differences, detecting missing or duplicate transactions, documenting corrections, and supporting period-end reporting. Its scope should specify covered entities, accounts, currencies, reconciliation frequency, source systems, material exceptions, approval levels, and required evidence. It should also define interfaces with treasury, accounts payable, accounts receivable, general ledger, foreign exchange, fraud response, and records management. Reconciliation does not independently authorize payments, change bank mandates, establish donor eligibility, determine complex accounting classifications, or replace incident investigations. Those activities should follow their own approved procedures. Define the boundary of responsibility explicitly so that a reconciler knows when to investigate, when to request an adjustment, and when to escalate a matter to another control owner.

Study instruction:
- Map the reconciliation process and identify which activities belong to Treasury, Accounting, bank administration, and incident response.
Key point:
- The SOP defines control responsibilities and process boundaries.
- Reconciliation is not a substitute for payment authorization or incident investigation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
3. Q: Which accounting and organizational authorities should govern bank reconciliation procedures?
A: Show answer
The organization should identify applicable mandatory law, its financial reporting framework, bank mandates and account terms, donor or grant conditions within their contractual scope, approved accounting policies, delegated authority, and system configuration. These sources do not have interchangeable purposes. Mandatory law and the applicable accounting framework govern within their scope, bank terms govern the banking relationship, and internal policies establish organizational controls without overriding higher requirements. The UN IPSAS Finance Manual provides useful accounting-process guidance, including bank reconciliation in Chapter 12 and general ledger controls in Chapter 18. Its UN-specific procedures should not automatically be adopted as the policy of another NGO. Where sources conflict, applicability is unclear, or an internal policy is missing, retain the issue on hold and seek a competent decision before relying on the affected procedure.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an authority register showing each source, its scope, effective date, applicability decision, reviewer, and approval status.
Key point:
- Accounting authority and operational implementation guidance must be distinguished.
- Unverified or conflicting requirements should not be silently converted into policy.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls
4. Q: Why must every bank account be registered and linked to its correct general ledger account?
A: Show answer
A complete bank-account register establishes the population subject to reconciliation. For every account, maintain a unique account identifier, legal entity, bank name, masked account reference, currency, purpose, linked GL cash account, owner, statement delivery method, status, and supporting mandate or ownership evidence. Include active, dormant, restricted, newly opened, and closing accounts where relevant to the reporting period. Reconcile the account register to the GL account mapping and authorized bank records so that an account cannot disappear from the monthly process simply because it has little activity. New accounts, changed account details, and account closures should require documented approval and an effective date. Closure should not be considered complete while required final statements, outstanding items, or residual balances remain unexplained.

Study instruction:
- Compare the registered account population with bank mandates, treasury records, and the GL cash-account list for a selected reporting date.
Key point:
- Population completeness starts with an authoritative account register.
- Account closure and inactivity do not automatically eliminate reconciliation obligations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
5. Q: How should responsibilities be separated among the preparer, reviewer, journal approver, and bank administrator?
A: Show answer
The reconciliation preparer should assemble source data, match transactions, calculate adjustments, and identify exceptions. A reviewer independent of preparation should inspect original sources, reperform material calculations, challenge questionable matches, and assess whether unresolved items are adequately supported. Journal preparation, journal approval, journal posting, bank-channel administration, and access administration should be separated where practicable. Each role must operate within a current delegation defining the entity, account scope, amount or currency limits, validity period, and revocation status. Where staffing constraints make complete separation impossible, document the conflict, risk assessment, compensating independent review, approval, expiry date, and evidence of retrospective checking. Internal Audit should remain independent of operating the controls it later evaluates.

Study instruction:
- Build a responsibility matrix and test whether any individual can prepare, approve, post, and finalize the same reconciliation without independent oversight.
Key point:
- Approval authority must be evidenced and appropriate to the transaction.
- Compensating controls need defined ownership, frequency, and evidence.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Workflows
6. Q: How can finance verify that a bank statement is authentic, complete, and appropriate for reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Obtain the statement through an approved banking channel or another formally authorized source. Record the account identity, statement number, reporting period, currency, opening balance, closing balance, retrieval timestamp, operator, filename, and source reference. Verify that the statement covers the entire required period and that its balances and movements are internally consistent. Check that the statement belongs to the correct entity and account, that pages or transaction sequences are not missing, and that the original evidence has not been altered. If the electronic statement is missing, defective, incomplete, or inconsistent with another source, stop reliance on it and document the issue. A substitute bank record should be used only under an approved procedure, with its authenticity, limitations, and approval clearly recorded.

Study instruction:
- Inspect a sample statement and document the checks used to validate source identity, completeness, balance continuity, and integrity.
Key point:
- A reconciliation cannot be reliable when its underlying statement is defective.
- Approved substitute evidence must remain traceable to its origin.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Statement Processing
7. Q: What controls are required when extracting general ledger transactions for reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Obtain the GL cash-account extract from an authorized reporting source using documented parameters. Record the entity, account, currency, period, extraction timestamp, report name and version, preparer, opening balance, transaction count, closing balance, and control-account status. Verify that all relevant transactions are included and that the extract reconciles to the applicable ledger balance. Check whether posting-date filters, document-status filters, reversals, late postings, or currency settings could exclude or duplicate entries. Preserve the source extract and use a controlled working copy for transformations. If the ledger extract does not agree with the official account balance or its parameters cannot be explained, hold the reconciliation until the report is corrected or an approved explanation is established.

Study instruction:
- Reproduce a GL extract and compare its opening balance, movements, and closing balance with the official ledger report.
Key point:
- GL source validation is separate from bank statement validation.
- A correct calculation using an incomplete extract is still unreliable.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Reconciliation
8. Q: Why must opening balances and prior-period reconciling items be rolled forward?
A: Show answer
The current-period reconciliation must continue from the approved prior-period position. The opening bank and ledger balances should agree with the preceding period's supported closing records, adjusted for any properly documented subsequent changes. Every outstanding payment, deposit in transit, bank dispute, unidentified transaction, and approved outstanding correction should retain its original identifier, date, amount, currency, owner, evidence, and status. New periods should update the status and age without resetting the original date. Cleared items should be linked to the subsequent bank or GL transaction that resolves them. An opening difference or unexplained change to a carried-forward item requires investigation rather than an automatic adjustment. This continuity prevents unresolved differences from being concealed by repeatedly preparing new schedules from scratch.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile the prior-period closing workpaper to the current opening balances and trace a sample of outstanding items through subsequent clearance.
Key point:
- Outstanding items must have a continuous history across periods.
- Original dates and references must not be overwritten to make items appear recent.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Controls
9. Q: What is a transaction matching rule, and why should matching confidence be controlled?
A: Show answer
A matching rule defines the evidence required to associate a bank transaction with a GL entry or other supported accounting record. A strong match may require equal signed amounts, the same currency, compatible dates, a unique transaction or payment reference, and a consistent counterparty or account. Some references contain formatting differences or are reused across transactions, so a reference number alone is not sufficient. Secondary matching can accommodate approved settlement timing or other known differences, but its criteria must be documented and tested. Each match should record the source transaction IDs, method, evidence reference, variance, decision, and reviewer where required. Low-confidence matches should remain proposed or under investigation rather than being automatically accepted merely because the amounts are similar.

Study instruction:
- Test matching rules on exact matches, date-shifted matches, repeated references, and similar-value transactions from different counterparties.
Key point:
- Match quality depends on multiple attributes and valid source records.
- Matching confidence should determine whether independent review or investigation is required.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Transaction Matching
10. Q: How should duplicate transactions, partial payments, and split or aggregate settlements be handled?
A: Show answer
Duplicate detection should compare transaction identifiers, account, currency, signed amount, dates, counterparty, invoice or payment reference, and other source attributes. However, identical amounts can represent legitimate recurring payments, and one bank settlement can correspond to several ledger entries. A single invoice may also be settled through several bank transactions. The procedure should therefore distinguish exact one-to-one matches from legitimate split or aggregate matches, partial settlements, reversals, and genuine duplicates. Preserve all original transactions and record how the accepted relationship was established. A suspected duplicate or partial match should be flagged for review; it should not be deleted, netted, or force-matched automatically. Where a transaction is reversed and subsequently reprocessed, link the reversal and replacement without losing the history.

Study instruction:
- Build test cases covering a duplicate debit, a valid split payment, a grouped bank settlement, and a reversal followed by a replacement transaction.
Key point:
- A similar amount does not establish that transactions are duplicates.
- Complex matching requires a traceable decision and appropriate review.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
11. Q: How should adjusted bank and adjusted ledger balances be calculated?
A: Show answer
The workpaper should calculate an adjusted bank balance and an adjusted ledger balance using documented, supported reconciling items. A common presentation is adjusted bank balance equals statement closing balance plus supported deposits in transit minus supported outstanding payments, with any other bank-side corrections treated according to their nature. Adjusted ledger balance starts with the GL closing balance and incorporates supported book-side transactions or corrections not yet recorded correctly in the books. The exact sign convention must be stated in the SOP and applied consistently. Do not include an item on both sides or use an unexplained balancing figure to force agreement. The final residual is adjusted bank balance minus adjusted ledger balance. It must be zero at the organization's approved currency precision, while all other source and control gates must pass independently.

Study instruction:
- Calculate both adjusted balances for a sample reconciliation and independently reperform the signs and residual calculation.
Key point:
- Each reconciling item must affect the correct side of the calculation.
- A zero residual does not prove completeness, authenticity, or authorization.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
12. Q: What is a deposit in transit, and how should it be evidenced?
A: Show answer
A deposit in transit is a receipt already recorded in the GL before the bank has credited it by the statement closing date. It generally adjusts the bank side of the reconciliation rather than creating a second GL receipt. Evidence may include an approved receipt record, deposit slip, receipt register, transfer confirmation, remittance advice, and subsequent bank statement entry. The reconciler should check that the amount belongs to the correct account and currency, that the GL entry exists, and that the deposit has not already cleared under a different reference. When the bank subsequently credits the deposit, link that credit to the original reconciling-item record and mark the item cleared. If the deposit remains outstanding beyond its expected timing, investigate whether it was rejected, misdirected, duplicated, or recorded incorrectly.

Study instruction:
- Trace a recorded deposit from its original receipt evidence through the reconciling schedule to the subsequent bank credit.
Key point:
- A deposit in transit is a timing difference, not new revenue or cash merely because it appears on the schedule.
- Subsequent clearance must be evidenced and linked to the original item.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation and Incoming Payments
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 3 — Receipts and Unapplied Cash
13. Q: How should outstanding payments be tracked until they clear the bank?
A: Show answer
An outstanding payment is a payment recorded in the GL that has not yet cleared the bank by the statement closing date. Its schedule should record the payment ID, transaction date, payee, amount, currency, linked ledger entry, original approval evidence, expected clearing period, owner, status, and subsequent bank reference. Before treating it as a timing difference, confirm that the payment was genuinely authorized and entered in the GL. A payment should not remain outstanding indefinitely without follow-up. Investigate long-aged payments, cancelled instructions, rejected transfers, duplicate payment risks, and possible replacement payments. If the original payment is cancelled or reversed, any related journal correction must follow approved procedures. The original item should remain traceable so that the reconciliation history shows why it was cleared, corrected, or escalated.

Study instruction:
- Select an outstanding payment and trace its authorization, ledger posting, subsequent bank clearance or cancellation, and final accounting treatment.
Key point:
- Outstanding payments require continued ownership and evidence.
- Cancellation and replacement must not create duplicate settlement or erase the original audit trail.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Outgoing Payments and Bank Reconciliation
14. Q: How should bank charges, interest, direct debits, and other bank-originated transactions be handled?
A: Show answer
A bank statement may contain service charges, interest, automatic collections, payment reversals, or other transactions not yet reflected in the GL. The reconciler should verify each transaction against available bank advice, account terms, contracts, receipts, or other evidence and determine whether it is a valid bank-originated item or an error. A supported transaction that requires a book entry should be routed through the approved journal process. Determine the correct account, accounting period, currency, project coding, and tax treatment, where relevant, before posting. Do not assume every unexplained bank debit is a fee or every credit is income. Material or unusual items should remain open while their nature is established. After posting, the journal reference and resulting ledger transaction must be linked back to the reconciliation and independently verified.

Study instruction:
- Review a sample of bank-only debits and credits and document the evidence supporting each classification and proposed journal.
Key point:
- Bank statement descriptions are evidence to investigate, not automatic accounting classifications.
- Adjustments must be approved, posted, and checked against the ledger.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Adjustments
15. Q: What should the finance team do when a bank transaction cannot be identified?
A: Show answer
An unidentified transaction is a bank or ledger entry whose purpose, counterparty, or source is not sufficiently supported. Record its date, account, signed amount, currency, reference, original evidence, first detection date, risk assessment, owner, next action, due date, and current status. Request clarification from the appropriate internal team or use approved bank contact channels when bank investigation is required. Do not force a match, classify the amount as convenient miscellaneous income or expense, or close the item solely because the period is ending. Where a controlled temporary account is permitted by the applicable policy, use it only after the appropriate accounting assessment and authorization, with a clear owner and resolution deadline. Suspicious or unauthorized transactions must be referred through incident-response channels without compromising evidence.

Study instruction:
- Work through an unidentified credit from initial detection to source investigation, accounting decision, approval, and documented closure.
Key point:
- Unknown items require investigation and accountable ownership.
- Temporary classification is not the same as resolving the underlying transaction.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation and Exception Investigation
16. Q: How should book-side errors and bank-side errors be distinguished and corrected?
A: Show answer
A book-side error arises when the accounting records contain an incorrect, missing, duplicated, or misclassified entry. A bank-side error arises when the bank's record or processing is incorrect or requires correction by the bank. The reconciler should identify which source is wrong by comparing original evidence, transaction instructions, ledger postings, bank confirmations, and subsequent activity. Book corrections must follow the journal approval and posting procedure. Bank errors should be documented in a bank dispute or investigation register, supported by correspondence and follow-up. Do not correct a bank error by arbitrarily changing the GL, or correct a ledger error by assuming the bank will adjust it. The reconciliation should show the issue, investigation result, action owner, correction reference, and verification that the final balances are properly supported.

Study instruction:
- Compare one suspected GL error with one suspected bank error and establish the required correction path for each.
Key point:
- The correction must address the source that is actually wrong.
- Bank disputes and journal corrections require separate evidence and controls.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Journal Controls
17. Q: What is the required process for preparing, approving, posting, and verifying reconciliation journals?
A: Show answer
A proposed journal should identify the underlying transaction, evidence, accounting assessment, accounts, debit and credit amounts, currency, period, entity, project coding, preparer, reviewer, and required approver. Journal preparation does not prove that the entry has been authorized or posted. An authorized approver must assess the proposed treatment, and the posting operator must follow the prescribed ledger process. Record the resulting journal or posting reference and verify that the expected entry appears in the GL with the correct period, amount, and account. Then update the reconciliation and confirm that the relevant discrepancy has been resolved without creating a new difference. Reject unsupported journals, self-approval where prohibited, and entries created solely to force a zero residual. Retain both the original request and evidence of its final disposition.

Study instruction:
- Trace one book-side adjustment through its evidence, accounting assessment, approval, GL posting, and post-posting reconciliation check.
Key point:
- Drafted, approved, posted, and verified are distinct journal states.
- A balanced journal is not evidence of correct accounting by itself.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Entries and Journal Workflows
18. Q: How should an NGO manage stale reconciling items and prevent them from remaining open indefinitely?
A: Show answer
Define aging thresholds and escalation criteria according to the organization's approved policy, transaction risk, materiality, and operational circumstances. The aging record should retain the original transaction date, first period identified, current age, amount, currency, owner, explanation, evidence, next action, and due date. When an item reaches a trigger, require documented reassessment rather than automatically carrying it forward. The owner should investigate why it remains outstanding, assess possible misstatement or loss, and propose a supported resolution. Actions may include obtaining evidence, requesting bank clarification, correcting the GL, cancelling an obsolete payment, or escalating a suspected incident. An item should only be cleared or closed when the required evidence and approval exist. Repeated carry-forward without progress should trigger management attention and possible control redesign.

Study instruction:
- Build an aging report and investigate the oldest items by original date, risk, amount, owner, and history of follow-up.
Key point:
- Carry-forward preserves history; it does not resolve the item.
- Aging and escalation should drive actions and documented closure.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Period-End Controls
19. Q: How should multiple currencies and foreign exchange differences be handled in bank reconciliation?
A: Show answer
Reconcile each bank account using its statement currency and corresponding GL account. Preserve the original currency, foreign-currency amount, transaction date, settlement date, statement amount, ledger amount, and relevant exchange-rate information. A foreign-currency account may contain genuine timing differences as well as exchange differences arising from accounting measurement. These are different issues and should not be combined into one unexplained adjustment. Apply the organization's applicable accounting framework and approved foreign-exchange policy to determine when and how remeasurement differences are recognized. Use currency-specific precision and ensure that calculations do not mix amounts in different currencies. If the exchange rate, conversion method, or transaction classification cannot be supported, hold the affected reconciliation or adjustment pending authorized review.

Study instruction:
- Reconcile one foreign-currency account and separate settlement timing differences from accounting exchange differences.
Key point:
- Bank-to-GL matching must respect account currency and transaction currency.
- Foreign exchange adjustments require a supported accounting basis and consistent methodology.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Controls
20. Q: How should transfers between two NGO bank accounts be reconciled?
A: Show answer
An internal bank transfer creates related movements in two bank accounts and may pass through a transfer-clearing account or another approved accounting mechanism. The source account should show the outgoing movement, and the receiving account should show the corresponding incoming movement, subject to actual settlement timing and accounting policy. Match both legs using the transfer reference, amount, currency, dates, and supporting authorization. If one leg clears before the other, maintain a controlled in-transit or clearing item with an owner and expected completion date. Do not treat an internal transfer as external income or expenditure merely because it appears as a bank receipt or payment. Verify both ledger entries, both bank statement movements, and any FX or transfer charges separately. Close the transfer only when the relationship between the two sides is supported.

Study instruction:
- Trace one internal transfer from its approval to the source-bank debit, receiving-bank credit, GL entries, and final clearing.
Key point:
- Internal transfers require linked evidence for both sides.
- Transfer charges and currency differences should be assessed separately from the principal amount.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank-to-Bank Transfers
21. Q: What procedures apply when a bank account has no activity, a statement is missing, or an account is being closed?
A: Show answer
A lack of transactions does not automatically prove that a required reconciliation is complete. Confirm the account's registered status, required statement period, currency, opening and closing balances, and any account maintenance or dormant-account activity. If a statement is missing, seek the authorized bank source or an approved substitute record. Document why the evidence is unavailable, who was contacted, what alternative was considered, and who approved any permitted exception. For a closed account, verify closure evidence, final bank activity, outstanding payments, residual funds, linked GL balances, and unresolved disputes. Do not assume an account can be excluded simply because its status changed during the year. Population completeness must be demonstrated for the relevant period, and unsupported accounts should remain on hold until the missing evidence or approved disposition is obtained.

Study instruction:
- Test a zero-activity account, a missing-statement account, and a closed account against the same completeness checklist.
Key point:
- No activity is a conclusion requiring evidence, not an assumption.
- Missing sources and incomplete closures require explicit control dispositions.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
22. Q: How should suspicious or potentially fraudulent bank transactions be handled?
A: Show answer
Transactions that appear unauthorized, duplicated, altered, inconsistent with known payment activity, or otherwise suspicious should be referred through the approved incident-response process. Preserve the original bank statement, payment instruction, GL record, relevant communications, access logs, and other evidence. Limit access to sensitive information and avoid altering the original evidence or contacting potentially implicated parties outside approved channels. The designated security, fraud, treasury, or compliance owner should determine containment and investigation steps. The reconciler should record the referral reference and required follow-up but should not independently conduct an investigation beyond delegated authority. Reconciliation approval must not be used to close an unresolved fraud concern. Once the incident is addressed, confirm any required journal corrections, bank actions, reporting implications, and control improvements under the appropriate procedures.

Study instruction:
- Simulate an unauthorized debit and document evidence preservation, escalation, assigned roles, and conditions required before closure.
Key point:
- Suspicion must trigger controlled investigation, not force-matching or routine clearance.
- Evidence preservation and separation of duties are essential during an incident.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Transaction Exceptions
23. Q: What must an independent reviewer verify before approving a reconciliation?
A: Show answer
The reviewer should obtain the original statement and independently sourced GL extract, verify that both relate to the same account, currency, entity, and period, and check the completeness of the registered account population. The review should reperform the main reconciliation calculations, assess opening-balance continuity, challenge significant or unusual matches, inspect the evidence supporting timing differences, and confirm that bank-only items have been assessed for appropriate accounting treatment. Review the journal approval and posting trail, stale-item register, unresolved exceptions, and subsequent clearance evidence where relevant. The reviewer should also verify that there is no unexplained residual and that all mandatory acceptance conditions have been met. Questions raised during review must be resolved with evidence, correction, or an authorized exception—not simply acknowledged verbally. The reviewer's identity, date, findings, and conclusion must be recorded.

Study instruction:
- Reperform one reconciliation using original sources and document the exceptions challenged, corrections requested, and final review conclusion.
Key point:
- Review means independent challenge and reperformance, not only a signature.
- Final approval must be tied to the exact version reviewed.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Reconciliation and General Ledger Controls
24. Q: When may a bank reconciliation be finalized and certified as complete?
A: Show answer
Finalization should occur only after mandatory acceptance gates have passed. These typically include valid bank evidence, a complete GL population, confirmed opening balances, supported transaction dispositions, correct reconciliation calculations, a zero unexplained residual at approved currency precision, appropriate journal processing, and a documented independent review. Every unresolved item must be handled according to the organization's approved rules; any permitted exception must have an explicit authority, justification, owner, and follow-up. A system status such as “prepared” or “balanced” is not equivalent to final approval. The authorized approver should confirm that the correct period and account are covered and that the evidence archive is complete. If the reconciliation is changed after approval, assess the impact, rerun affected checks, and obtain renewed approval when required.

Study instruction:
- Create a finalization checklist and test whether any single failed gate can still produce a closed or approved status.
Key point:
- Final sign-off requires all mandatory control conditions to pass.
- A non-zero unexplained residual cannot be waived solely because the amount is small.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Reconciliation and Adjustments
25. Q: How should bank reconciliation be integrated into the monthly closing process?
A: Show answer
Bank reconciliation should be included in a controlled close calendar alongside bank statement retrieval, GL extraction, journal cut-off, payroll, accounts payable, accounts receivable, foreign exchange, and financial reporting. Assign an owner and deadline to each account, establish internal completion dates ahead of management reporting deadlines, and define escalation steps for missing evidence or late corrections. The close coordinator should monitor the full account population rather than only the reconciliations already submitted. Outstanding reconciling items must be carried forward with stable identifiers and aging. Significant unresolved balances should be communicated to the appropriate manager, including the cause, value, risk, next action, and target date. Final closing reports should distinguish reconciliations completed and approved from those still pending, on hold, or affected by unresolved issues.

Study instruction:
- Build a monthly close tracker and simulate a delayed statement, an overdue journal, and a reconciliation that fails review.
Key point:
- The close process must cover every in-scope bank account.
- Late or unresolved reconciliations require visible ownership and escalation.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — Period-End Processes
26. Q: What should a bank reconciliation workpaper and supporting registers contain?
A: Show answer
The workpaper should identify the reconciliation, period, entity, bank account, currency, statement reference, GL extract reference, opening and closing balances, supported adjustments, adjusted balances, residual, preparer, reviewer, and status. Supporting registers should cover transaction matching, reconciling-item aging, proposed journals, bank disputes, access reviews, source integrity, exceptions, and final approval. Each record should have a stable identifier and links to the underlying source evidence. Define mandatory fields, permitted statuses, required approvals, and closure conditions so users cannot mark incomplete items as resolved without the necessary support. Keep operational records concise enough to use, but detailed enough for another authorized reviewer to reconstruct the decision. Ensure forms reflect actual organizational policy rather than treating fictional fields as adopted requirements.

Study instruction:
- Compare the current workpaper with the proposed control register and identify missing fields, evidence references, or closure requirements.
Key point:
- Stable identifiers connect transactions, decisions, journals, and evidence.
- Required fields and status definitions should be controlled and tested.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Documentation
27. Q: What does the fictional IDR reconciliation example demonstrate about arithmetic control?
A: Show answer
The supplied example uses a fictional bank statement closing balance of IDR 12,400 and a fictional GL closing balance of IDR 11,250. After the example's supported adjustments, both adjusted balances are IDR 11,600, producing a residual of zero. The arithmetic demonstrates that two different starting balances can reconcile when the adjustments on each side are properly supported. However, the figures and evidence are expressly invented and do not establish actual bank ownership, actual transactions, posted journals, or organizational approval. The example also does not establish the nature of any adjustment beyond what is documented in the workpaper. In a real reconciliation, each adjustment must be independently traced to evidence and classified under the applicable accounting policy. Never infer missing adjustment details merely from the final arithmetic result.

Study instruction:
- Reperform the stated calculations, then list the additional source evidence required before the example could support a real reconciliation approval.
Key point:
- IDR 12,400 and IDR 11,250 are fictional starting balances in the example.
- Zero residual confirms arithmetic agreement only after the adjustment basis has been validated.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
28. Q: What should happen when a foreign-currency reconciliation has an unexplained non-zero residual?
A: Show answer
A residual in a foreign-currency account should remain unresolved until its cause is identified or an authorized procedure provides a supported disposition. Check whether the bank statement and GL extract use the same currency, period, account, and transaction population. Reperform the arithmetic and inspect currency precision, conversion treatment, duplicate records, timing differences, omitted charges, and incorrect posting signs. A separate currency account should not be cleared by an unsupported adjustment to another currency balance. In the supplied fictional SOP, the example EUR account remains on hold because an unidentified EUR 75 credit causes an unresolved difference. This illustrates that a non-zero residual prevents final sign-off. The example does not prove how a real EUR account should be treated; the underlying transaction and applicable policy must be investigated first.

Study instruction:
- Investigate the fictional EUR 75 credit using a structured exception checklist and document the evidence required before releasing the hold.
Key point:
- Unexplained foreign-currency differences remain open until properly investigated.
- Currency-specific balances and adjustments must be independently supported.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management
29. Q: How can Excel tables improve the reliability of a bank reconciliation workbook?
A: Show answer
Excel tables provide a structured way to store bank transactions, ledger entries, matches, reconciling items, and exception records. Named columns and structured references make formulas easier to understand and maintain as the transaction population grows. Tables also support filtering, consistent headers, and controlled data validation. A reconciliation workbook should separate source imports, transformation or calculation sheets, working schedules, management summaries, and final output. Raw source evidence should remain preserved, while processing changes should be traceable. Workbook design does not determine the accounting treatment, and formulas must be tested against independently calculated examples. Any table structure adopted for production should be documented, protected as appropriate, and reviewed when source formats or business requirements change.

Study instruction:
- Convert sample bank and GL data into structured tables and test how formulas, filters, and row expansion behave when new records are added.
Key point:
- Structured tables improve repeatability but do not eliminate source-data or accounting risks.
- Keep raw data, calculations, exceptions, and approved output distinct.
Source links:
- Create and format tables — Microsoft Support
30. Q: How can Power Query automate bank statement and GL data preparation?
A: Show answer
Power Query can import supported bank statement exports and GL extracts, normalize field names and data types, standardize reference formats, combine files, and produce repeatable transaction populations. Its transformation steps are saved so the process can be refreshed instead of rebuilt manually each period. A controlled query should preserve source identity, period, currency, transaction direction, reference numbers, and an auditable link to the original input. Validate row counts, opening and closing balances, movement totals, and any excluded records before using the transformed data. Schema changes, missing columns, altered date formats, or unexpected files should trigger visible exceptions rather than silent misclassification. Power Query prepares data; it does not establish whether a transaction is authorized or what its correct accounting treatment should be.

Study instruction:
- Build queries for bank and GL exports and compare row counts and control totals before and after transformation.
Key point:
- Repeatable data preparation reduces manual rework and format inconsistency.
- Data transformation must not conceal missing transactions or replace accounting judgment.
Source links:
- What Is Power Query? — Microsoft Learn
31. Q: What role can VBA play in reconciliation automation, and what controls should surround it?
A: Show answer
VBA can help generate reconciliation workpapers, refresh or coordinate approved processing steps, prepare exception reports, update workflow statuses, export controlled reports, and record execution events. These capabilities should operate within an approved workbook design and should never bypass journal authorization, independent review, or source validation. Macros should have a defined owner, documented logic, error handling, permitted actions, version history, and test evidence. Users should be able to distinguish a proposed journal from an approved or posted journal. Failures should stop the workflow safely, display useful error details, and record the affected records where feasible. Macro security and distribution must follow the organization's approved IT controls. A macro's successful execution is not proof that its accounting output is correct.

Study instruction:
- Test a macro with missing source files, invalid currency codes, incomplete records, and a deliberately incorrect calculation; verify that it stops safely and reports the issue.
Key point:
- VBA can automate controlled tasks but cannot replace review and authorization.
- Macro logic, permissions, errors, and outputs must be tested and traceable.
Source links:
- Excel VBA Reference — Microsoft Learn
32. Q: How should exception reports and reconciliation status dashboards be designed?
A: Show answer
An exception report should surface unmatched transactions, suspected duplicates, missing source records, unexplained residuals, stale items, unsupported adjustments, source integrity failures, and items waiting for approval. Each exception should show a stable ID, account, period, amount, currency, severity, owner, due date, evidence reference, next action, and status. A dashboard can summarize completion rates, overdue items, unresolved balances, review returns, and trends across accounts. Status definitions should distinguish prepared, pending review, returned, approved, on hold, and finalized. Reports must be based on the complete registered population, not merely those records that were successfully imported. Every displayed status needs a defined source and validation rule. The dashboard helps managers prioritize action but does not replace examination of the underlying transaction evidence.

Study instruction:
- Define the status rules for a reconciliation dashboard and test whether missing accounts and unresolved items remain visible.
Key point:
- Exceptions need accountable owners and measurable deadlines.
- Dashboard accuracy depends on complete source populations and controlled status transitions.
Source links:
- What Is Power Query? — Microsoft Learn
- Create and format tables — Microsoft Support
33. Q: How should reconciliation evidence, audit trails, and retention be controlled?
A: Show answer
Maintain an indexed evidence repository that links each reconciliation to statements, GL extracts, working schedules, match decisions, adjustment requests, approvals, journal references, review comments, and final sign-off. Record the source origin, filename, record identifier, period, retrieval or creation time, custodian, version, access classification, and integrity information where supported. Preserve originals and record transformations or corrections in a separate controlled history. Retention periods must be confirmed against applicable law, contractual or donor terms, and approved organizational policies; a sample SOP cannot establish the legally required period for a specific NGO. Apply access restrictions and approved backup processes, and periodically test restoration. Evidence should remain retrievable for authorized audit, management review, and subsequent-period investigation without compromising confidential banking information.

Study instruction:
- Retrieve a randomly selected reconciliation and confirm that every material balance, adjustment, approval, and closure can be traced to its supporting evidence.
Key point:
- An audit trail must preserve both the original evidence and the history of changes.
- Retention requirements need verified authority and approved schedules.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Documentation
34. Q: How should access to banking systems and reconciliation records be reviewed?
A: Show answer
Maintain a register of users, roles, account scope, permissions, access owner, approval reference, effective date, and revocation date. Grant access according to actual duties and the principle of least privilege. Separate transaction execution, approval, reconciliation, and administration wherever practicable. Review access after staff transfers, departures, changes of responsibility, or consultant engagement changes, and perform periodic checks for shared accounts or excessive privileges. Credentials, authentication codes, and security secrets must never be embedded in reconciliation workbooks or ordinary evidence logs. Where activity cannot be attributed to an individual, assess the risk and apply approved corrective controls. Any access exception should have an owner, justification, approval, and expiry or review date.

Study instruction:
- Compare the current bank-system user list with the approved responsibility matrix and test a sample of permissions and recent access changes.
Key point:
- Access should follow documented responsibilities and be removed when no longer required.
- Access administration is a separate control from reconciliation preparation and approval.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Process and Control
35. Q: What controls are needed when the bank platform, statement interface, or responsible employee is unavailable near closing?
A: Show answer
The SOP should identify who assesses the outage, who contacts approved support channels, who informs management, and which alternative procedures may be authorized. Record the outage start and end times, affected accounts, period, operational impact, evidence, support ticket or correspondence, and actions taken. Do not assume that a system outage automatically extends a deadline or removes an obligation. If a substitute statement or manual processing method is considered, verify that it is allowed under the applicable policy and approved by the designated authority. After service is restored, process delayed records, verify successful receipt or posting, reperform affected reconciliations, and document any resulting correction. If the regular owner is absent, invoke a documented delegation rather than sharing credentials or bypassing authorization.

Study instruction:
- Simulate an outage one day before closing and test the escalation, substitute-evidence approval, continuity, and recovery procedures.
Key point:
- Business continuity must preserve control requirements, not merely keep the process moving.
- Recovery requires verification and documented completion of deferred tasks.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Processing
36. Q: How should management measure the effectiveness of bank reconciliation controls?
A: Show answer
Define indicators such as the percentage of registered accounts reconciled on time, percentage approved by the internal deadline, number and value of unexplained residuals, aged reconciling items, unresolved bank disputes, review returns, journal-posting delays, missing statements, and recurring exception categories. Each indicator needs a consistent definition, data source, calculation method, reporting frequency, owner, target, and escalation rule. Timeliness measures should use the complete population of required reconciliations as the denominator, not only the reconciliations submitted. Management should analyze trends, identify recurring causes, and assign corrective actions with deadlines and evidence of closure. High completion rates alone do not prove control effectiveness if reconciliations are approved without genuine independent review or source validation.

Study instruction:
- Calculate monthly reconciliation KPIs from the full account register and compare them with exception and review records.
Key point:
- KPI definitions and population denominators must be consistent and verifiable.
- Management should investigate both delays and weaknesses in the quality of completed reconciliations.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Financial Control Processes
37. Q: How should changes to matching rules, workbook formulas, or bank interfaces be approved and tested?
A: Show answer
Every material change should have a documented request, reason, impact assessment, owner, reviewer, approver, test plan, version identifier, effective date, and release decision. Test both ordinary transactions and edge cases, including missing statements, duplicate references, split payments, reversals, multi-currency items, incorrect signs, and non-zero residuals. Compare results with independently calculated expectations and preserve test evidence. Changes should not be deployed merely because the workbook refreshes successfully. Where possible, retain the prior approved version and a rollback procedure. Following release, verify real processing results against the approved design and monitor for unexpected differences. If a change alters accounting treatment or approval logic, obtain the appropriate accounting and control-owner decision before implementation.

Study instruction:
- Create a regression test register for a proposed matching-rule change and test both positive and negative cases before release.
Key point:
- Technical changes must be tested against accounting and control requirements.
- Version history and rollback capability reduce the risk of repeated errors.

- Excel VBA Reference — Microsoft Learn
38. Q: How should a bank reconciliation SOP be adopted and introduced to the organization?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the actual legal entity, accounting framework, bank accounts, internal policies, reporting requirements, and delegated authorities. Compare current practice with the proposed SOP through walkthroughs of completed reconciliations and document evidence-based gaps. Assign owners for procedures, registers, approval workflows, system configuration, and training. Test the draft against representative real-world scenarios using appropriately protected data before authorizing operational use. The authorized approver must confirm the document version, effective date, responsible roles, and implementation arrangements. Communicate the approved procedure to preparers, reviewers, Treasury, Accounting, and relevant support functions. Retain the draft and change history under document control, and establish a periodic review cycle. The supplied SOP is explicitly marked as a fictional demonstration and must not be treated as an approved organizational procedure.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an adoption plan showing evidence reviewed, identified gaps, accountable owners, required testing, approvals, training, and the proposed effective date.
Key point:
- A sample SOP becomes operational only after entity-specific verification and authorized adoption.
- Document approval and staff training must be evidenced.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Cash Management and Bank Reconciliation
39. Q: How can a finance team distinguish a timing difference from an accounting error or an unresolved risk?
A: Show answer
A timing difference is supported by evidence that a transaction is correctly recorded on one side but has not yet appeared on the other because of settlement or processing timing. An accounting error means that one or more records require correction. An unresolved risk exists when the source, authorization, classification, or transaction purpose cannot yet be established sufficiently. These categories require different actions. Timing differences are tracked until clearance; accounting errors are corrected through the approved journal process; and unresolved risks are investigated and escalated. The category should be supported by evidence rather than chosen to reduce the residual. Where new evidence changes the assessment, update the classification while retaining the original record and decision history. This distinction improves reporting and helps management direct investigations to the right team.

Study instruction:
- Classify a sample of unmatched bank items into timing differences, book errors, bank errors, or unresolved exceptions and document the decision evidence.
Key point:
- Similar-looking differences can require different resolution paths.
- Classification must be revisited when evidence changes.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger Adjustments
40. Q: What acceptance tests should be completed before a bank reconciliation process is considered ready for operational use?
A: Show answer
Test the full workflow, including account-population completeness, source authenticity, GL control totals, opening-balance continuity, exact transaction matching, duplicate detection, supported reconciling-item treatment, journal approval and posting, independent review, and final sign-off. Include negative tests in which a source is missing, an account is omitted, an opening balance is incorrect, a suspected duplicate exists, a journal lacks approval, or a small unexplained residual remains. The expected result should be a visible exception or hold, not a false approval. Reopen generated documents and validate their structure, and perform page-by-page visual review if controlled Word outputs are part of the release. Separate locally performed tests from claims requiring independent validation, live bank evidence, legal review, or organizational approval. Record the actual result and evidence for each acceptance gate.

Study instruction:
- Create a test register with test ID, scenario, expected result, actual result, evidence reference, tester, status, and acceptance decision.
Key point:
- Negative tests demonstrate whether controls fail safely.
- Operational readiness requires evidence of testing, applicable authority, and authorized organizational adoption.
Source links:
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 12 — Bank Reconciliation
- UN IPSAS Finance Manual, Chapter 18 — General Ledger and Reconciliation Controls
